) with e.g. the relator
[a,a^{(a^{t})}]=1
, etc., in which choosing to break down using
a
immediately yields a free group, but breaking down using
t
can take an arbitrary finite number of steps to reach a free group. We also here mention in this connection a result of Weinbaum
[
Wei72
]
who proved in 1972 that in a one-relator group
\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
, with
r
cyclically reduced, no proper non-empty subword of
r
is equal to
1
. Thus even this result, which seems straightforward, is not immediate to prove using Magnus’ solution to the word problem. We will discuss the time complexity, and related questions, of the word problem in one-relator groups in §
1.8.3
. Before this, however, we must first cross the barren landscape of inactivity that followed Magnus’ results.

1.4.
The Lull Phase (1932–1960)

Following Magnus’ 1932 solution to the word problem in all one-relator groups, progress on one-relator groups slowed down. Indeed, as for significant theorems related directly to one-relator groups themselves, in the next three decades or so only one would be proved: the Identity Theorem of Lyndon. We will discuss this result in §
1.4.2
. However, these three decades, which we will refer to as the
lull phase
, saw a number of very significant developments in combinatorial group theory as a subject, many of which would eventually end up being directly relevant to one-relator group theory. In this section, we will give an overview of this phase of the development of one-relator group theory, ending it in §
1.4.4
by discussing some reasons for what caused progress to slow down, and subsequently resume.

1.4.1.
Some results on free groups

We begin with some developments on the theory of free groups that occurred in the 1930s. As we shall see, they would have rather an important impact on the development of one-relator group theory in the 1960s, particularly the notion of
residual nilpotence
. We begin with Magnus’ main result during this time: the representation of free groups in (the group of units of) a certain ring. This can be fairly easily explained. Let
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}
be
n
non-commuting variables, and let
\mathbb{Z}[[\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}]]
be the ring of formal power series in the non-commuting variables. Let
a_{i}=1+\xi_{i}
. Then each
a_{i}
is, in fact, invertible, with inverse given by

(1.15)

a_{i}^{-1}=1-\xi_{i}+\xi_{i}^{2}-\xi_{i}^{3}+\cdots=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}\xi_{i}^{j}.

Thus the
a_{i}
generate a group; and Magnus proved that the group they generate is a free group. This has a number of immediate consequences.

Theorem 1.4.1

(Magnus, 1935
[
Mag35
]
)
.

The group generated by
1+\xi_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq n
) is free of rank
n
, freely generated by these generators. As a consequence, free groups are residually nilpotent.

Here, a group
G
is said to be
residually nilpotent
if the intersection of all terms in the lower central series of
G
is trivial; alternatively, we will see a more general definition of residual properties in §
1.4.3
. The residual nilpotence of free groups is a direct consequence in Theorem
1.4.1
as follows: let
F
be a free group. Then any element of the
k
th term of the lower central series of
F
, considered as an element of
\mathbb{Z}[[\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}]]
in the above representation, must have the degree of a certain component be at least
k
(see
[
MKS66
, p. 313]
). This implies, of course, that the terms of the lower central series intersect trivially. A standard reference for this representation, now often called the
Magnus representation
, is
[
MKS66
, Chapter 5]
. See also
[
Che54
,
Cha68
]
for some extensions to other finitely generated groups; indeed, the representation was in the first place motivated in part by a 1928 article by Shoda
[
Sho28
]
on the automorphism group of abelian groups.

The Magnus representation would end up inspiring much research on representing free groups. While we cannot write the full history here (it shall instead appear elsewhere), one particularly famous case bears a brief mention. In the late 1930s, a group of three Soviet students working in St Petersburg, supervised by V. A. Tartakovskii, picked up the subject: these were Kh. A. Doniyakhi, D. I. Fuchs-Rabinovich, and I. N. Sanov. First, Fuchs-Rabinovich proved results about “determinators” of free groups, and uses these to recover Magnus’ theorem above on the residual nilpotence of free groups; this appeared in a 1940 article (written in English!)
[
FR40b
]
21
21

21

Lyndon
[
Lyn53
]
later elaborated on this article and its connection with Magnus’ work, and we primarily refer the interested reader to consult this latter source.
. Doniyakhi proved results about extending these results to free products of abelian groups
[
Don40
]
. Fuchs-Rabinovich
[
FR40a
]
, in another article, also proved results about representing free groups in
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, giving another proof of Magnus’ result. At this time, Sanov
[
San40
]
instead worked on a different problem, namely the Burnside problem for exponent
4
, which he solved affirmatively in 1940.

The three students would have rather different fates. When war broke out in 1941, Fuchs-Rabinovich was drafted into the USSR Baltic Fleet. He died of dysentery in 1942, during the Siege of Leningrad, at the age of 29. Doniyakhi was also drafted, and died in battle less than two months later in 1941, at the age of 24. Sanov survived the war.
22
22

22

Indeed, Sanov served as Head of Mathematics for one year at the University of Pyongyang (North Korea) in 1949.
After the war, Sanov would carry on the research his fellow students had initiated, and in 1947 published the famous article
[
San47
]
. This begins by acknowledging the articles by Fuchs-Rabinovich and Doniyakhi, then presents his theorem: the (multiplicative) group generated by the two matrices

(1.16)

\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
2&1\end{pmatrix}

is a free group of rank
2
. This can be used to give yet another proof of the residual nilpotence of free groups, and would directly inspire many important results in combinatorial group theory, including the Ping-Pong Lemma
[
Mac63
]
.

Sanov and his friends were of course not the only researchers to be affected by the war; we have already seen in §
1.2.2
how Dehn was affected. Magnus, who had been appointed as an assistant at Königsberg in 1939, was forced to resign from this position shortly thereafter because he refused to join the NSDAP (the Nazi Party). Instead, he found employment at
Telefunken
, a radio company, during the war.
23
23

23

During this time, he would gain an interest in
special functions
, which would eventually lead to him joining a project with Erdélyi, Oberhettinger & Tricomi to publish their famous book
[
EMOT53
]
on transcendental functions. This book remains, by a wide margin, Magnus’ most cited work.
This abrupt break in his usual research environment could also explain the lack of progress on one-relator group theory during this time, cf. §
1.4.4
.

Let us now turn back to some further results on free groups pertaining to one-relator groups. The first concerns the study of
primitive
elements in free groups, as discussed in §
1.2.3
. Whitehead, using topological methods, proved two remarkable theorems in this direction in a 1936 article
[
Whi36
]
. The first states simply that there is an algorithm for deciding whether or not a given word is a primitive element or not. That is, one can decide whether a given element
w\in F_{A}
can be part of a basis or not (cf. Nielsen’s result Proposition
1.2.4
, which gives an algorithm for deciding if a pair of words is a basis for
F_{2}
). Furthermore, Whitehead completely characterized when a one-relator group is free.

Theorem 1.4.2

(Whitehead, 1936
[
Whi36
, Theorem 4]
)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
with
r
non-trivial. Then
G
is free if and only if
r
is a primitive element of
F_{A}
.

As we shall see in §
1.6
, there is also a complete characterization of when a one-relator group is
virtually
free (Theorem
1.6.8
), but this would not be proved until 1972.
24
24

24

Indeed, the fact that a non-free virtually free one-relator group must have elements of finite order is not at all obvious, but follows from Stallings’ theorem
[
Sta70
]
.
Next, a result which can be seen as a generalization of Whitehead’s result was proved by Magnus in 1939.

Theorem 1.4.3

(Magnus, 1939
[
Mag39
]
)
.

Let
G
be a group given by a presentation with
n+r
generators and
r
defining relations, with
n,r\geq 0
. Then:

(1)

If
G
can be generated by
n
elements, then
G
is free of rank
n
.

(2)

If
G^{\operatorname{ab}}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, then some set of
n
of the generators of
G
generate a free group.

(3)

If
G
has the same sequence of lower central factors as the free group on
n+r
generators, then
G
is free.

Thus Theorem
1.4.3
(2) can also be seen as an extension of the
Freiheitssatz
to the case of more than one defining relation. We delay the definition of
lower central factors
for when we revisit Theorem
1.4.3
(3) in the context of
parafree
groups in §
1.7.6
.

Finally, we remark on a set of results that relate to one-relator groups only by association. In his 1931 article, his second on one-relator groups, referenced above, Magnus
[
Mag31
]
also proved that the commutator subgroup of the modular group
G=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid a^{2}=b^{3}=1\rangle\cong C_{2}\ast C_{3}
is a free group of rank
2
. His argument is essentially a simplified version of the machinery developed to deal with one-relator groups. Later, in 1948, Nielsen
[
Nie48
]
extended this result to arbitrary free products of finite cyclic groups, proving using a geometric argument that that the commutator subgroup of
C_{m_{1}}\ast C_{m_{2}}\ast\cdots\ast C_{m_{r}}
is free of rank

1+m_{1}m_{2}\cdots m_{3}\cdot\left(-1+\sum_{i=1}^{r}(1-\frac{1}{m_{i}})\right).

Many more results could be mentioned here, as we now are heading head-first into the dawn of combinatorial group theory. We have said nothing, for example, of the automorphism groups of free groups, a topic which interested Magnus, and many others, around this time. However, these subjects increasingly stray from one-relator groups, to which we now instead firmly return.

1.4.2.
Lyndon’s Identity Theorem

Recall Dyck’s fundamental result (see §
1.2.1
) that in a group
G
with underlying free group
F
and given by defining relations
r_{1},\dots,r_{n}
, an element
W
is equal to one in
G
if and only if it can be written of the form

(1.17)

W=\prod_{i=1}^{m}T_{i}r_{t_{i}}^{\pm 1}T_{i}^{-1}\quad\textnormal{where }T_{i}\in F,\textnormal{ and }t_{i}=1,2,\dots,n.

One might ask how
unique
such an expression (
1.17
). For example, in the group
\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid[a,b]=1\rangle
, isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, we obviously have
[a^{2},b^{2}]=1
, since
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is commutative. This equality is witnessed by the fact that:

(1.18)

\begin{cases}\>[a^{2},b^{2}]=\left(aRa^{-1}\right)\cdot R\cdot\left(baR(ba)^{-1}\right)\cdot\left(bRb^{-1}\right)\\
\>[a^{2},b^{2}]=\left(aRa^{-1}\right)\cdot\left(abR(ab)^{-1}\right)\cdot R\cdot\left(bRb^{-1}\right)\end{cases}

where we have let
R=[a,b]=aba^{-1}b^{-1}
. How different are the two expressions in (
1.18
)? Up to reordering the terms, we notice that they are identical, except for the conjugates of
R
by
ab
resp.
ba
. However,
ab=ba
in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and so the two expressions in (
1.18
) are
identical up to reordering the terms and equality in
G
. This is an instance of Lyndon’s Identity Theorem, which states that this behaviour always occurs in a torsion-free one-relator group; there are no “relations between the relations”.

Formally, the problem of determining relations between relations asks: what are the non-trivial relations holding between the relations
r_{1},\dots,r_{n}
of the form

(1.19)

\prod_{i=1}^{m}T_{i}r_{t_{i}}^{\pm 1}T_{i}^{-1}=1\quad\textnormal{where }T_{i}\in F,\textnormal{ and }t_{i}=1,2,\dots,n?

There are some trivial relations always holding; for example, if
r_{1},r_{2}
are two different relations, then
(r_{2}r_{1}r_{2}^{-1})\cdot(r_{2})\cdot(r_{1}^{-1})\cdot(r_{2}^{-1})=1
, which is a product of the form (
1.19
) but not a particularly interesting one. Such relations are called
Peiffer identities
. According to Chandler & Magnus
[
CM82
, p. 118]
, the idea of studying identities between relations had been described by Dehn to Magnus in the early 1930s, but this was not pursued further at the time. Instead, this would not be done until the late 1940s, when it was investigated by Peiffer (a student of Reidemeister) in 1949
[
Pei49b
]
and, in the same year, by Reidemeister
[
Rei49
]
. The latter proved that the problem of finding all non-trivial relations (
1.19
) reduces to understanding the case when all
T_{i}
are taken modulo
r_{1},\dots,r_{n}
, and disregarding the order of the factors in the product. If we set
G=F/R
, i.e.
R
is the normal closure of
r_{1},\dots,r_{n}
, then this amounts to stating that we must understand the group
R/[R,R]
. This abelian group is naturally a
{\mathbb{Z}}G
-module via the conjugation action of
G
, and is called the
relation module
of
G
(
with respect to

r_{1},\dots,r_{n}
, see below). Furthermore, it can be shown that the action of
G
on
R/[R,R]
is faithful, i.e. only the identity element fixes all of
R/[R,R]
, see
[
AL55
]
.

In 1948, Reidemeister visited the Institute for Advanced Study for a year, which had a great influence on a young postdoc in Princeton at that time: R. Lyndon, who had been a PhD student of S. Mac Lane’s
[
App84
, p. 3]
. Reidemeister, who took an early interest in one-relator groups (see e.g. his 1932 proof of the
Freiheitssatz
in §
1.3.1
), does not appear to have been the one to introduce Lyndon to one-relator groups. Instead, Lyndon recalls that after presenting his projective resolution (see below) in a seminar at the IAS, Reidemeister came up to him and explained a natural topological interpretation of his resolution
[
Rat84b
, p. 25]
, cf. also
[
Lyn50
, p. 651]
. Thus in the late 1940s, combinatorial group theory was certainly on Lyndon’s mind, and in a brief 1949 article
[
Lyn49
]
he proved a result for the cohomology of groups defined by generators and relations, before finally turning his attention to one-relator groups. He was able to prove the following theorem, by using the Magnus breakdown (§
1.3.1
):

Theorem 1.4.4

(Lyndon’s Identity Theorem, 1950
[
Lyn50
]
)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
, with
r=w^{n}
where
w
is not a proper power and
n\geq 1
. Then
\prod_{i}T_{i}r^{\varepsilon_{i}}T_{i}^{-1}=1
implies that the indices can be grouped into pairs
(i,j)
such that
e_{i}=-e_{j}
and
T_{i}\equiv T_{j}w^{c_{i}}
in
G
for some
1\leq c_{i}\leq n
.

In other words, there are no non-trivial relations holding among the relation of a one-relator group. We now give a restatement of the theorem in more modern terminology, using the language of homological algebra. Let us make clear that at this point, group cohomology was a nascent area of research, and many of the general statements that are today a standard part of the subject were not yet formulated.
25
25

25

To highlight this, we remark that group cohomology had essentially only been computed for finite abelian groups at the time of Lyndon’s 1947 PhD thesis
[
Lyn47
]
. The subject of this thesis, later published as
[
Lyn48
]
, was the cohomology of group extensions, leading to what is today known as the
Lyndon–Serre-Hochschild spectral sequence
. For a lucid account of this early history of group cohomology, we refer the reader to
[
ML84
]
.
We begin with a restatement of Theorem
1.4.4
in the case of
n=1
, i.e. the torsion-free case (see §
1.5.1
):

Corollary 1.4.5

(Lyndon
[
Lyn50
, p. 663]
)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
be a torsion-free one-relator group. Then the relation module of
G
with respect to this presentation is a free
{\mathbb{Z}}G
-module.

The relation module and the second homology group of any group are, as is well-known (and as we shall see below), closely related; this was observed by Peiffer
[
Pei49a
]
and Miller
[
Mil52
]
. We can thus use the Identity Theorem to gain information about the cohomological dimension of a one-relator group:

Corollary 1.4.6

(Lyndon
[
Lyn50
, Corollary 11.2]
)
.

Let
G
be a torsion-free one-relator group. Then
G
has cohomological dimension at most
2
.

Let us now give a restatement of the Identity Theorem in terms of an explicit resolution. Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\mid r^{n}=1\rangle
be a one-relator group with
r
not a proper power and
n\geq 1
. By attaching a
1
-cell for each generator and a single
2
-cell for the defining relator, we can always find a sequence of free
G
-modules

F_{2}\xrightarrow{\partial_{2}}F_{1}\xrightarrow{\partial_{1}}\mathbb{Z}\to 0

where
F_{2}
is free of rank
1
, and
F_{1}
is free of rank
m
. Lyndon’s Identity Theorem then states that the kernel of
\partial_{2}
is generated by the element
r-1
. If
n=1
, then of course
r-1=0
in
{\mathbb{Z}}G
, so it follows that
\partial_{2}
is injective, and hence any torsion-free one-relator group
G
has cohomological dimension
2
, and one can explicitly compute the (co)homology groups. For example, for trivial coefficients it follows almost immediately from Corollary
1.4.5
that
H_{2}(G,\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}
if and only if
r\in[F,F]
, and otherwise
H_{2}(G,\mathbb{Z})=0
. Hughes
[
Hug66
]
used the Identity Theorem to compute the cohomology of
G=F/R
with coefficients in the relation module
R/R^{\prime}
. Then, when
r=w^{n}
with
n\geq 1
and
w
not a proper power, he proves that
H^{k}(G,R/R^{\prime})
is trivial for odd
k
and cyclic of order
n
for even
k>2
. Furthermore, he shows that
H^{2}(G,R/R^{\prime})
is an extension of an easily describable group
D(G)
by a cyclic group of order
n
. For example, in the case of
G=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{K}_{g})
from (
1.4
), Hughes is able to show that
H^{2}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{K}_{g}),R/R^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{Z}
. Finally, we remark that in 1984 Ratcliffe
[
Rat84a
]
gave an explicit description of the cup product
\smile\colon H^{1}(G,M)\times H^{1}(G,M)\to H^{1}(G,M)
for an arbitrary
{\mathbb{Z}}G
-module
M
.

In 1963, Cohen & Lyndon
[
CL63
]
gave a new proof of the Identity Theorem, and strengthened it by giving an explicit free basis of the relation module. Concretely, if
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r^{n}=1\rangle
, with
r\in F_{A}
not a proper power and
n\geq 1
, then Cohen & Lyndon construct a set
X\subset F_{A}
of words such that
\{x^{-1}r^{n}x\mid x\in X\}
is a free basis of the normal closure
\langle\langle r^{n}\rangle\rangle
in
F_{A}
. Their results, proved via the usual Magnus hierarchy but with a stronger inductive hypothesis, also included, as a byproduct, a new proof of the
Freiheitssatz
. Two other simpler proofs are due to Karrass & Solitar
[
KS72
]
and
[
Bur74
]
, in 1972 and 1974 respectively.

We end with a word of caution regarding the terminology “relation module of a one-relator group”. Indeed, relation modules depend on the presentation chosen, although if
G\cong F/R\cong F/S
, then it is known that there is a free
{\mathbb{Z}}G
-module
X
such that
R/R^{\prime}\oplus X\cong S/S^{\prime}\oplus X
, see
[
Lyn62
]
. By combining the Identity Theorem with the above statement, we find that the relation module of a one-relator group
G\cong F/S
is always
{\mathbb{Z}}G
-projective. However, if
S
is not the normal closure of a single element – even though
G
is isomorphic to a one-relator group! – then the relation module
S/S^{\prime}
need not be
{\mathbb{Z}}G
-free. Indeed, Dunwoody
[
Dun72
]
showed that in the case of the trefoil knot group
G=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid a^{2}=b^{3}\rangle
(see §
1.2.2
), there is a presentation
G\cong F/S
for which the relation module
S/S^{\prime}
cannot be generated by a single element, and indeed cannot contain
{\mathbb{Z}}G
as a direct summand. Thus
S/S^{\prime}
is not free, but it is projective.

1.4.3.
Residual properties of groups

The notion of residual properties goes back rather far, and while we will not attempt to survey it in full here, we will mention some generalities. Let
\mathcal{P}
be a property of groups (e.g. being finite, free, etc.). A group
G
is termed
residually

\mathcal{P}
if for every
1\neq g\in G
there exists a group
H_{g}
with property
\mathcal{P}
and a homomorphism
\varphi_{g}\colon G\to H_{g}
such that
\varphi(g)\neq 1
. The terminology
residually

\mathcal{P}
is originally due to P. Hall
[
Hal54
, p. 349]
in 1954, but the concept can be traced further back. For example, in 1940 Mal’cev
[
Mal40
]
, who called residually finite groups
finitely approximable
, proved that every finitely generated linear group is residually finite. He also proved, in connection to the
Hopf problem
(the connection of which to one-relator groups we shall explore thoroughly in §
1.5.2
) that no finitely generated linear group is hopfian; his argument can be easily extended to show that in fact every finitely generated residually finite group is hopfian.

Free groups are the prototypical examples of residually finite groups. Even before Mal’cev’s result on linear groups – and free groups are linear, using e.g. the representation by Fuchs-Rabinovich
[
FR40a
]
, see §
1.4.1
– it was well-known that free groups are residually finite. For example, in 1933 Levi
[
Lev33
, Theorem 1]
gave a proof of the residual finiteness of free groups. Indeed, he proved the stronger statement that the intersection of any decreasing series of characteristic subgroups in a free group is trivial, which shows that, in particular, free groups are residually nilpotent (cf. Magnus’ 1934 proof in §
1.4.1
). For other early proofs of the residual finiteness of free groups, see also
[
Iwa43
,
Neu61
]
. Free groups are also known to satisfy the stronger property of being residually
p
-groups for any prime
p
, as proved by Marshall Hall
[
Hal50
]
and Takahasi
[
Tak51
]
. Another natural class of residually finite groups are the finitely generated nilpotent groups, as proved by Hirsch in 1946
[
Hir46
]
. Thus residually nilpotent groups are also residually finite, and we will encounter many examples of residually nilpotent groups throughout this survey. Residual nilpotence, in particular, has a strong connection with the Magnus representation of free groups (§
1.4.1
) and many results on residual nilpotence of particular quotients of free groups and related topics, e.g. a well-known result due to Gruenberg
[
Gru62
]
or Neumann
[
Neu62
]
, can often be proved by appealing to the Magnus representation, see
[
BH66
]
.

One of the advantages of residual finiteness is that any finitely presented residually finite group has decidable word problem. The algorithm is simple: given such a group
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid R\rangle
, to decide whether a given word
w\in F_{A}
is equal to
1
in
G
or not we run two algorithms in parallel: (1) enumerate all words equal to
1
in
G
; and (2) enumerate all homomorphisms from
G
onto finite groups. If
w=1
, then the first algorithm detects this; if
w\neq 1
, then by residual finiteness the second algorithm will detect this. Thus
G
has decidable word problem.
26
26

26

This algorithm is often attributed to McKinsey, particularly to his 1943 article
[
McK43
, Theorem 3]
, but this is not accurate, for several reasons. First, McKinsey works in a considerably more general context of “finite reducibility” in algebras, and as McKinsey himself points out in a footnote, “this principle has been known for a long time […] It was employed by L. Löwenheim […] [McKinsey] also made use of it in his article
[
McK41
]
”
[
McK43
, p. 69, footnote 6]
. The reference to Löwenheim refers to Hilbert & Ackermann
[
HA38
, p. 95]
, which in turn refers to a 1915 article by Löwenheim
[
Löw15
]
, wherein the principle is admittedly well-disguised. The first to write the principle in its modern form, purely speaking of groups, was Mostowski
[
Mos66
]
in 1966 and, independently, V. Dyson
[
Dys64
]
(see also
[
Dys74
]
). The argument was also known to G. Higman and A. Turing, but not published (as pointed out by Boone and Schupp in their MR review of
[
Mos66
]
).
Since all one-relator groups have decidable word problem (§
1.3.2
, Theorem
1.3.6
), this raises the following very natural question: is
every
one-relator group residually finite? The answer is a resounding
no
, and we will encounter many such counterexamples in the form of
Baumslag–Solitar groups
in §
1.5.3
, which are of central importance due to giving rise to non-hopfian groups. Finally, we mention that Meskin
[
Mes74
]
proved in 1974 that there exist finitely generated residually finite groups with undecidable word problem. Hence, for the above algorithm, the assumption of finite presentability is crucial.

1.4.4.
Why did progress halt?

As we shall see in the next section (§
1.5
), the area of one-relator group theory would see a resurgence of interest after the lull phase ended just before 1960. As described by Chandler & Magnus, Baumslag proposed the following idea to explain the lull phase: “the
Freiheitssatz
and the subsequent solution of the word problem for one-relator groups by Magnus left the impression that one-relator groups are something almost as easy to deal with as free groups and that this delayed a more thorough investigation of one-relator groups for quite a while.”
[
CM82
, p. 119]
. While somewhat compelling, we would like to add two further suggestions to this, which have not appeared elsewhere in the literature.

First, given the large number of PhD students supervised by Magnus who would end up active in the area of one-relator group theory, it also bears noticing that Magnus only had two PhD students while in Berlin (F. Oberhettinger and F. Shäfke) with neither working in group theory. As soon as he arrived in New York in 1950, however, he started supervising a large number of doctoral students, with the first graduating in 1954; one of the first was Abe Shenitzer
27
27

27

Shenitzer and Magnus would develop a life-long friendship, summarized briefly in the touching tribute
[
She95
]
. In particular, Abe Shenitzer (1921–2022) had been imprisoned in several concentration camps during the war. After the war, he arrived in New York, and studied under Magnus. Early on in their friendship, Magnus, visibly moved, said “You’ve done more for me than any person can do for another. […] You are a Jew who was in German concentration camps, and I am a German”, cf. also
[
Sti22
]
. Magnus choosing to return to his pre-war work on one-relator groups at this point might thus have been in large part stimulated by his interaction with Shenitzer.

[
She54
]
, who gave a necessary and sufficient condition for a one-relator group to split as a free product. This result can thus be seen as Magnus’ return to considering one-relator groups, following the turbulence of the war. Magnus would end up supervising 66 PhD students at New York University between 1950 and 1974, with some ending up as the most important names in the theory of one-relator groups and combinatorial group theory: to mention a few early names, we have D. Solitar (1958), M. Greendlinger (1960), B. Chandler (1962), and J. Birman (1968). The same idea can be proposed for B. H. Neumann, given his early contact with Magnus while in Berlin. He moved to the University of Manchester in 1948, where he supervised both J. Britton (1954) and G. Baumslag (1958), both of whom would be of central importance to the subject (in this line, cf. also
[
CM82
, pp. 197–198]
).

The second reason, and in our view more important, is the general undecidability of the word problem. We first give a brief summary of these developments. The word problem, having originated with Dehn and Thue as outlined in §
1.2.1
, gave rise to the first undecidability result in “real” mathematics, following relatively artificial undecidable problems on Turing machines as given by Turing
[
Tur36
]
. This first “real” undecidability result was the 1947 proof by Post
[
Pos47
]
and, independently Markov
[
Mar47b
,
Mar47a
]
, that there is a finitely presented
semi
group with undecidable word problem. This would in short order lead to the undecidability of the word problem for groups. Turing
[
Tur50
]
worked on the problem, in 1950 proving the undecidability of the word problem in
cancellative
semigroups
28
28

28

We mention here another connection to B. H. Neumann: around 1950, when he and Turing were both at the University of Manchester, the two of them had announced a resolution to the word problem for groups. However, their resolutions were contradictory: Turing announced the
undecidability
of the word problem in finitely presented groups, whereas Neumann announced the general
decidability
of the same. Both later retracted their claims, but Turing’s work led to the undecidability for cancellative semigroups in the work
[
Tur50
]
cited above
[
Col85
, p. 169]
.
, a class of semigroups in which either of
xy=xz
or
yx=zx
implies
y=z
. This article would be used
29
29

29

Turing’s article contains a number of gaps, somewhat amended by Boone
[
Boo58
]
, and Novikov & Adian
[
NA58
]
later produced their own proof of the same result to avoid this dependency.
by Novikov
[
Nov52
,
Nov55
]
to produce a proof of the undecidability of the word problem in groups; this was done independently by Boone
[
Boo57
,
Boo59
]
and Britton
[
Bri58
,
Bri63
]
around the same time. Today, it is known that there exists a 12-relator group with undecidable word problem
[
Bor69
]
, arising from an encoding of a 3-relation semigroup with undecidable word problem due to Matiyasevich
[
Mat67
]
.

In connection with the undecidability of the word problem, in the mid-1950s a number of further undecidability results for groups would appear, including the famous
Adian–Rabin Theorem
, stating that deciding “most” reasonable properties (e.g. finiteness, triviality, being abelian or free, etc.) about finitely presented groups is undecidable
[
Adi55
,
Rab58
,
NB22
]
. Thus, around 1960 the area of combinatorial group theory had undergone something of a shock. Indeed, one might reasonably speculate that a contemporary researcher reading Magnus’ 1932 article
[
Mag32
]
would imagine that perhaps the same method could be extended, at great combinatorial cost and with little gain, to
all
finitely presented groups, and that therefore there was nothing particularly special about one-relator groups
per se
. The undecidability results of the 1950s shattered this illusion. Now, research could be directed to classes of groups where the word problem was decidable in order to see what was special about them, because something had to be special if the word problem were to be decidable. One-relator groups certainly turned out to be special indeed.

1.5.
The resurgence of one-relator groups (1960s)

Topology gave rise to combinatorial group theory. As we have seen in §
1.2
, the early articles on one-relator groups were all clearly motivated by topology, with surface groups and knot groups being particular flagship examples. As the machinery of one-relator groups and combinatorial group theory grew increasingly powerful, it also became increasingly useful to reapply these tools back to answer topological questions. In the late 1950s, an important connection between the Poincaré Conjecture and combinatorial group theory was made by C. Papakyriakopoulos. While this connection would not result in a proof of the Poincaré Conjecture, it did result, directly and indirectly, in the development of one-relator group theory. Other topological questions, related directly to the old
hopfian
problem, would also play a key role during this time. In this section, we will outline how these topological considerations led to a resurgence of one-relator group theory, and some of the subsequent developments in the 1960s. We will also showcase Magnus’ continued influence on the area by illustrating how one of his PhD students gave rise to a new school of combinatorial group theory in the USSR.

1.5.1.
The Poincaré Conjecture and torsion

Around this time, new stirrings of topological methods appeared in combinatorial group theory. An important stirrer was C. D. Papakyriakopoulos, who rose to prominence by giving a proof of
Dehn’s Lemma
in 1957
[
Pap57a
,
Pap57b
]
(the original “proof” of this result, given by Dehn
[
Deh10
]
in 1910, had a gap, as pointed out by Kneser
[
Kne29
, p. 260]
). Soon thereafter, Papakyriakopoulos
[
Pap58
]
became interested in proving the Poincaré Conjecture
30
30

30

The Poincaré Conjecture, which other than its brief connection to one-relator groups expounded here lies wholly outside the scope of this survey, states:
a closed
3
-manifold with trivial fundamental group is homeomorphic to a
3
-sphere
. The conjecture was proved by Perelman in the early 2000s; for more historical details, see Stillwell’s translation and commentary in
[
Poi10
, pp. 1–12]
.
, and in 1963
[
Pap63b
,
Pap63c
]
he considered the following construction (with a slight correction provided by Maskit
[
Mas63
]
). Let
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
be the surface group of genus
g>1
, defined by (
1.3
), let
w
be any word in the commutator subgroup of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
, and let
P_{g}(w)
be the quotient of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
by the relation
[a_{1},b_{1}w]=1
. Then Papakyriakopoulos was able to reduce the Poincaré Conjecture to the following group-theoretic conjectures: (a)
P_{g}(w)
is torsion-free; and (b) the cover of
\Sigma_{g}
corresponding to the kernel of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})\to P_{g}(w)
is planar. In connection to the first of these two conjectures, Papakyriakopoulos wrote a letter to Magnus, asking whether the then-known methods of combinatorial group theory could be applied to solve (a). This letter would become the basis of a 1960 article by Karrass, Magnus & Solitar
[
KMS60
]
(D. Solitar was a student of Magnus’, and A. Karrass had been a student of Solitar’s). The three authors did not solve Papakyriakopolous’ question in full, but provided some partial results indicating it had a positive answer. The groups
P_{g}(w)
are two-relator groups, and as part of their investigations of the elements of finite order in this class, the authors of
[
KMS60
]
were able to completely characterize the elements of finite order in one-relator groups:

Theorem 1.5.1

(Karrass, Magnus & Solitar, 1960
[
KMS60
, Theorem 1]
)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
be a one-relator group with
r
cyclically reduced. Then
G
has elements of finite order if and only if
r
is a proper power in
F_{A}
, i.e.
r=w^{n}
with
n>1
and
1\neq w\in F_{A}
.

The proof, of course, goes via the classical Magnus hierarchy, by induction on the relator length (Gruenberg
[
Gru70
]
gave a cohomological proof of Theorem
1.5.1
in 1970). The theorem states, in other words, that a one-relator group has torsion precisely when it obviously has torsion, and the authors of
[
KMS60
]
further characterize the elements of finite order as the obvious ones, namely those which are conjugate to a power of the relator. The authors of
[
KMS60
]
proceed to provide some further insight into one-relator groups with torsion as follows:

Theorem 1.5.2

(Karrass, Magnus & Solitar
[
KMS60
, Theorem 3]
)
.

Consider the two one-relator groups
G_{1}=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r_{1}^{n}=1\rangle
and
G_{2}=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r_{2}^{n}=1\rangle
where
n>1
. If
G_{1}\cong G_{2}
, then also
\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r_{1}=1\rangle\cong\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r_{2}=1\rangle
.

This shows that isomorphisms for one-relator groups with torsion is, in a sense, at least as “rigid” as isomorphisms of torsion-free one-relator groups. Note that the converse of Theorem
1.5.2
fails, but this would not be proved until more than a decade later, see §
1.6.3
. Indeed, at this point in the story, one-relator groups with torsion were still viewed with the same lens as their torsion-free counterparts; there was even some indication that the presence of torsion was a complicating factor. When we return to the topic of one-relator groups with torsion in §
1.6
, we shall see that this indication was very far from the truth.

As mentioned above, Karrass, Magnus & Solitar were not able to completely resolve Papakyriakopoulos’ conjecture (a), but provided some partial progress
[
KMS60
, Theorem 5]
. Indeed, their work can be seen as a very early form of “relative one-relator groups”, an area that would grow in the decades to follow
[
How81
,
How84
,
How87
]
. In 1964, another PhD student of Magnus’, E. Rapaport, would provide a full affirmative solution to conjecture (a). In a beautiful 1964 article, Rapaport
[
Rap64b
]
adapted the Magnus hierarchy to the very particular two-relator setting of conjecture (a), as suggested to her by G. Baumslag (a name we shall encounter frequently from this point onwards), and was able to prove conjecture (a). For further work in this direction, see
[
Pap63a
]
. Ultimately, the Poincaré Conjecture would not be proved by means of Papakyriakopoulos’ reduction, as in 1981, Moran
[
Mor81
]
and independently McCool
[
McC81
]
proved that conjecture (b) is false, using a further reduction due to Swarup
[
Swa79
]
.

Nevertheless, topological methods were to remain in combinatorial group theory. Indeed, soon after the new proof of Dehn’s Lemma, J. Stallings, motivated in part by the old work of Kneser
[
Kne29
]
and encouraged by Papakyriakopoulos (see
[
Sta65b
, p. 1]
), gave a new proof of Grushko’s theorem on generators of free products using purely topological methods. His proof also dropped the finite generation hypothesis appearing in Grushko’s proof, reproving the more general result due to Wagner
[
Wag57
]
. Topological methods in combinatorial group theory would bloom in the coming decades. Stallings’ 1983 article
[
Sta83
]
introducing simple graph-theoretic methods to the study of free groups marked one turning point in the history of combinatorial group theory; and similarly the “tower method” of Papakyriakopoulos used to proved Dehn’s Lemma would become crucial in the study of locally indicable groups and one-relator groups, as pioneered by Howie
[
How83
]
in 1983. These methods, however, pass outside the scope of this present history.

Instead, we now return to the state of one-relator group theory in 1960, where many problems from topology motivated further problems and progress. One problem of particular importance regarded
hopfian
groups, and it is here that one-relator groups would begin to reveal the full extent of their rich, versatile, and often mysterious nature. We first give a brief background on hopfian groups, and then expand on how they led to a resurgence of one-relator group theory.

1.5.2.
Hopfian groups

A group is said to be
hopfian
if it is not isomorphic to any proper quotient of itself. The terminology arises from the fact that H. Hopf
[
Hop31
]
in 1931 studied the fundamental groups
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
of genus
g>1
, which we defined in (
1.3
) and he denoted by
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
. In particular, he proved by a rather complicated topological argument that
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
is hopfian. In this context, one is naturally lead to ask: are there finitely generated non-hopfian groups? Hopf asked this question, although not explicitly in any of his own articles; already by 1934, however, Magnus
[
Mag35
, p. 276]
noted that he had been informed by B. H. Neumann of Hopf asking this question. Of course, there are many non-finitely generated examples of non-hopfian groups, e.g.
\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}
, but the assumption of finite generation makes the problem significantly more difficult, and it would take nearly 20 years to resolve (affirmatively).

It was known already at this point that finitely generated free groups were hopfian; indeed, it is not difficult to prove using Nielsen’s 1921 results (see §
1.2.3
). Magnus
[
Mag35
]
explicitly gives a proof of the hopficity of free groups. Furthermore, Mal’cev
[
Mal40
]
proved in 1940 that any finitely generated linear group is hopfian. Indeed, he proved that any such group is residually finite, a sufficient condition for hopficity as mentioned in §
1.4.3
, and Fuchs-Rabinovich
[
FR40b
]
moreover proved that the any finite free product of finitely generated abelian groups is hopfian. One of the first to consider the hopfian problem explicitly was Baer
[
Bae44
]
in 1944, who extended Fuchs-Rabinovich’s theorem to a more general setting. He also claimed to have given a construction of a finitely generated non-hopfian group
[
Bae44
, p. 272]
; however, this was later withdrawn due to an error
[
BW49
, Appendix, p. 741–743]
. Thus, for some time, the problem of finding a finitely generated non-hopfian group remained open.
31
31

31

We remark here that being hopfian is
not
a Markov property of groups in the sense of the Adian–Rabin Theorem
[
Adi55
,
Rab58
]
, as every finitely presented group can be embedded into a hopfian group
[
MS71
]
. However, Collins
[
Col69b
]
proved in 1969 that it is nevertheless undecidable whether a given finitely presented group is hopfian. From these results, of course proved only long after the results about to be presented, it follows that there exists an abundance of finitely presented non-hopfian groups.

The first example of a finitely generated non-hopfian group was finally given in 1950 by B. H. Neumann
[
Neu50
]
. This group is not finitely presented. The next year, Higman
[
Hig51
]
constructed a two-relator non-hopfian group, given by the simple presentation

(1.20)

\textnormal{}\langle a,b,c\mid a^{-1}ca=b^{-1}cb=c^{2}\rangle.

A further finitely generated non-hopfian group was constructed in 1961 by Hall
[
Hal61
]
. In 1962, Gilbert Baumslag constructed a new example of finitely presented non-hopfian groups
[
Bau62a
]
: take two integral Heisenberg groups, and amalgamate them over a common free abelian subgroup of rank
2
. Up to that point, the above examples were essentially the only known non-hopfian groups. But it would not be long before Baumslag would contribute a remarkable number of results in this direction.

Gilbert Baumslag (1933–2014) first heard of Wilhelm Magnus in 1956 while attending lectures at the University of Manchester given by his PhD supervisor B. H. Neumann
[
Bau94
, p. 99]
. The subject of the lectures was
amalgamated free products
, which we have encountered before. Neumann pointed out that although Nielsen introduced amalgamated free products, it was Magnus who recognised their value in his work on one-relator groups. These amalgamated free products (or
Schreier products
, as Baumslag calls them in e.g.
[
Bau63b
]
), would become a central part of the toolbox he used to prove a remarkable set of theorems.

Thus, at this point, in 1962, Baumslag would be part of two remarkable results relating one-relator groups and hopfian groups. The first was a positive result. He proved that the amalgamated free product of a free group with a free abelian group, with amalgamated subgroup maximal cyclic in the free group, is residually free
[
Bau62b
, Theorem 1]
(cf. also
[
Dye68
]
). This general result implies many interesting corollaries. In particular, it implies that the amalgamated free product
F\ast_{u=\overline{u}}\overline{F}
of two isomorphic free groups, with isomorphism given by
x\mapsto\overline{x}
and
u\in F
being any word generating its own centralizer, i.e. not a proper power, is residually free. Thus, many one-relator groups are residually free; and hence, by Magnus’ 1935 result (see §
1.4.1
) also residually nilpotent and residually finite. One example comes surface groups, which we saw in §
1.2.2
. Baumslag
[
Bau62b
, p. 425]
states that Magnus had asked in a seminar whether all surface groups
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
are residually nilpotent. This question can be answered by using Baumslag’s aforementioned theorem, by taking a commutator
u=[a_{1},a_{2}]
, which makes

\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})\cong\textnormal{}\langle a_{1},a_{2},\overline{a}_{1},\overline{a}_{1}\mid[a_{1},a_{2}]=[\overline{a}_{1},\overline{a}_{2}]\rangle\cong F_{2}\ast_{\mathbb{Z}}F_{2}

be residually free (and hence also all
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
for
g>1
by the discussion following Theorem
1.2.3
). Baumslag’s results show that
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
is residually nilpotent and hence also hopfian, thus answering Magnus’ question positively, giving many new hopfian one-relator groups.
32
32

32

Baumslag mentions in a footnote
[
Bau62b
, p. 425]
that Magnus’ question was also answered, independently and by a very different method, by K. Frederick
[
Fre61
,
Fre63
]
, a PhD student of Magnus’. Furthermore, G. Higman also communicated a different proof to Baumslag.
He also proves that all
2
-generator subgroups of certain one-relator groups are free (
[
Bau62b
, Theorem 2]
), comparing this result to the
Freiheitssatz
. Indeed, this would be one of the first results about subgroups of one-relator groups since the
Freiheitssatz
, cf. §
1.7
. The second contribution from 1962 would be of a negative nature, and was joint between Baumslag and D. Solitar (a former PhD student of Magnus’): the construction of the famous
Baumslag–Solitar groups
.

1.5.3.
Baumslag–Solitar groups

These facts destroy several conjectures long and strongly held by the reviewer and others, and some published and unpublished ‘theorems’.

—B. H. Neumann, MR review of
[
BS62
]
.

The hopficity of one-relator groups was, for some time, an overlooked question. In fact, in connection with the construction of his
two
-relator group (
1.20
), Higman
[
Hig51
, p. 61]
states that it can be shown, using an argument due to B. H. Neumann and H. Neumann, that all one-relator groups are hopfian. This is, as we shall now see, very far from the truth. In 1962, Baumslag & Solitar
[
BS62
]
would consider very simple examples of one-relator groups:

(1.21)

\BS(m,n)=\textnormal{}\langle x,y\mid x^{-1}y^{m}x=y^{n}\rangle,

Their main result, appearing in a bulletin article, would be to prove that many of the groups
\BS(m,n)
, now called
Baumslag–Solitar groups
, are not hopfian (see below for a correction of their initially incorrect statement). One of the main examples of non-hopfian groups would be the following remarkable statement:

Theorem 1.5.3

(Baumslag & Solitar, 1962
[
BS62
]
)
.

The group
\BS(2,3)
is not hopfian.

We will not present the proof of this here, but recall that by Mal’cev’s theorem (§
1.4.3
) all finitely generated residually finite groups are hopfian. Thus
\BS(2,3)
is not residually finite. This, at least, is easy to observe directly. Indeed, let

(1.22)

B:=\BS(2,3)=\textnormal{}\langle x,y\mid x^{-1}y^{2}x=y^{3}\rangle,

and let
\varphi\colon B\to F
be any homomorphism onto a finite group
F
. Let
\alpha=\varphi(x),\beta=\varphi(y)
. A simple inductive argument shows that
x^{-n}y^{2^{n}}x^{n}=y^{3^{n}}
for all
n\geq 0
in
B
, and hence
\alpha^{-n}\beta^{2^{n}}\alpha^{n}=\beta^{3^{n}}
in
F
. In particular, if
n
is the order of
\alpha
, then we deduce that the order of
\beta
must divide
k=3^{n}-2^{n}
. Since
k
is obviously coprime with both
2
and
3
, by Bézout’s identity there are integers
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
1=m_{1}k+2m_{2}
, and hence
\beta=\beta^{2m_{2}}
is a power of
\beta^{2}
. Thus, letting
\beta_{1}=\varphi(a^{-1}ba)
, we have that
\beta_{1}
is a power of
\alpha^{-1}\beta^{2}\alpha=\beta_{1}^{2}
, and
\beta_{1}^{2}=\beta^{3}
. Thus
\beta_{1}
is a power of
\beta
, and so we must have
[\beta_{1},\beta]=1
. In other words, since
\varphi
was arbitrary, the word
c=[x^{-1}yx,y]
must vanish in all finite homomorphic images of
B
. But
c\neq 1
in
B
, as Magnus’ breakdown procedure shows that if
c=1
, then in the torus knot group
\textnormal{}\langle y_{0},y_{1}\mid y_{0}^{2}y_{1}^{3}=1\rangle
we have
[y_{0},y_{1}]=1
, which is false by standard properties of amalgamated free products (or indeed since torus knots are not unknots). Thus
B
is not residually finite.

The above argument extends to show that if
\gcd(m,n)=1
, and
|m|,|n|\geq 2
, then
\BS(m,n)
is not residually finite. However, characterizing precisely when
\BS(m,n)
is residually finite is somewhat non-trivial. Indeed, in
[
BS62
, p. 199]
, it is claimed that if
m
or
n
divides the other, then
\BS(m,n)
is residually finite. This is not correct, as one can show e.g. that
\BS(2,4)
is
not
residually finite. The correct characterization would only be given by Meskin in 1972, as follows:

Theorem 1.5.4

(Meskin, 1972
[
Mes72
, Theorem C]
)
.

A Baumslag-Solitar group
\BS(m,n)
is residually finite if and only if
|m|=1
or
|n|=1
or
|m|=|n|
.

Many other complications arise in the literature of Baumslag–Solitar groups, including for stating precisely when they are hopfian. Baumslag & Solitar themselves state that
\BS(m,n)
is hopfian if and only if either
m
divides
n
or
n
divides
m
, or if
\Pi(m)=\Pi(n)
, where
\Pi(k)
denotes the set of prime divisors of
k
. This is not correct, as e.g.
\BS(2,6)
can be shown to not be hopfian. The correct characterization is as follows.

Theorem 1.5.5

(Collins & Levin
[
CL83
]
)
.

A Baumslag–Solitar group
\BS(m,n)
is hopfian if and only if it is residually finite, or if
\Pi(m)=\Pi(n)
.

Thus, for example,
\BS(12,18)
is hopfian, but it is not residually finite. Indeed, as stated (but not proved) by Baumslag & Solitar
[
BS62
, Theorem 2]
, it has a finite index subgroup which is not hopfian (and therefore also not residually finite). The earliest complete published proof of Theorem
1.5.5
that we are aware of only appeared in 1992, see
[
ARV92
, Corollary 1]
. There, the authors attribute this result to Collins & Levin
[
CL83
]
, who do not give a full proof of the result, but prove that for all
|m|,|s|\neq 1
, if
\Pi(ms)=\Pi(m)
, then
\BS(m,ms)
is hopfian. This is one of the more difficult parts of proving Theorem
1.5.5
, and it seems very likely that Collins & Levin were aware of, and could have written down a proof of, Theorem
1.5.5
. The above characterization also leads to some curious results: for example, it is not difficult to show that in
\BS(12,18)=\textnormal{}\langle x,y\mid x^{-1}y^{12}x=x^{18}\rangle
the group generated by
\langle x,y^{6}\rangle
is isomorphic to
\BS(2,3)
. Thus
\BS(2,3)\leq\BS(12,18)
, even though the former is non-hopfian, but the latter is hopfian because
\Pi(12)=\Pi(18)
. Thus the Baumslag--Solitar groups also serve to illustrate just how elusive a property hopficity can be from a group-theoretic point of view.
33
33

33

Even understanding when direct products of hopfian groups is hopfian is rather complicated, see e.g.
[
Cor65
,
Hir69
]
.

Baumslag–Solitar groups have come to play a central role in the theory of one-relator groups since their introduction (indeed, recall that some examples of them appeared already in Magnus’ 1930 article, §
1.3.1
). They act as counterexamples, as we have seen, to many natural conjectures about one-relator groups, and have a rich structural theory. We now give an overview of some of this structure to finish this section. We first remark that the groups
\BS(1,m)
, with
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, which above are distinguished as residually finite as well as hopfian, are in fact solvable. Indeed, the derived subgroup is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{m}]
, and the groups split as a semidirect product
\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{m}]\rtimes\mathbb{Z}
, with obvious conjugating action. Indeed, they are linear, arising as subgroups of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, via the representation

(1.23)

x\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}m^{\frac{1}{2}}&0\\
0&m^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{pmatrix}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad y\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

In fact, a Baumslag–Solitar group is linear if and only if it is residually finite. One direction is Mal’cev’s famous theorem, and the other is obtained by constructing linear representations for all groups in Theorem
1.5.4
. Thus, in view of the linear representation (
1.23
), it suffices to show that
\BS(m,m)
is linear for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. However, it is not hard to prove that
\BS(m,m)
has a finite index subgroup isomorphic to a direct product
F_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}
, where
F_{n}
is a free group of rank
n
. Since such direct products are obviously linear, so too is
\BS(m,m)
.

The results by Baumslag & Solitar were quickly picked up by Rapaport, who in 1964 used them to prove certain results on free products of groups. In a rather obscure article
[
Rap64a
]
, she proves among other results two which are relevant to our story: first, the minimal number of defining relations of a free product of two finitely presented groups may be less than the sum of that of the two factors (later rediscovered by
Hog, Lustig & Metzler
[
HLM85
]
). Second, she proved a result on the
deficiency
of a one-relator group. Here, the deficiency of an
n
-generator
k
-relator presentation is defined to be
n-k
, and the deficiency of a finitely presented group is the maximum, taken over all presentations of the group, such deficiency. Thus the deficiency of an
n
-generator one-relator group is at least
n-1
, and Rapaport proved that it is no larger:

Theorem 1.5.6

(Rapaport, 1964
[
Rap64a
, Corollary 2]
)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
be a one-relator group. Then
G
has deficiency
|A|-1
.

Baumslag–Solitar groups continue to be a rich area of research, and although we cannot possibly mention all results regarding them, we will mention a few which are naturally connected to hopficity and the above considerations. First, the endomorphisms of
\BS(m,n)
with
\gcd(m,n)=1
and
m,n\neq\pm 1
were described by Anshel
[
Ans71
,
Ans72
,
Ans73
]
(see
[
Ans76c
]
for corrections). Using this, he proceeded to prove that in this case,
G=\BS(m,n)
has a fully invariant subgroup
N
such that
G/N\cong G
, a very strong form of non-hopficity. We mention also that Hirshon
[
Hir75
]
has given a full description of the intersection of the subgroups of finite index in Baumslag–Solitar groups. Finally, regarding the quotients of Baumslag–Solitar groups, Moldavanskii & Sibyakova
[
MS95
]
proved (with classical techniques) that the solvable Baumslag–Solitar groups
\BS(1,k)
are
profinitely rigid
among residually finite one-relator groups, i.e. for any residually finite one-relator group
G
, the set of finite quotients of
G
is the same as that of
\BS(1,k)
if and only if
G\cong\BS(1,k)
, cf. also
[
BS89
]
. We remark finally that a natural line of investigation beyond Baumslag–Solitar groups comes from considering relations of the type
x^{-1}w^{m}x=w^{n}
, where
w
is some arbitrary word (possibly with more generators). Hopficity in this case has been investigated e.g. by Collins
[
Col78b
]
, Rosenberger
[
Ros80
]
, and Collins & Levin
[
CL83
]
.

1.5.4.
The Magnus–Moldavanskii hierarchy

One of Magnus’ most prominent students was M. Greendlinger (b. 1932), who completed his PhD in 1960.
34
34

34

Martin Greendlinger was born in New York in 1932. In 1957, he met Elena Ivanovna in Moscow while visiting for a conference, and they married the next year. Because Elena was unable to get a US citizenship, Martin moved to the USSR in 1960, renounced his US citizenship, and became a Soviet citizen in 1961, eventually settling in Tula. Thus Magnus’ school of combinatorial group theory also made its way to the USSR. For more details, see
[
VD22
]
. Occasionally, Greendlinger’s name is incorrectly transliterated (via Russian) in English as
Grindlinger
.
The subject matter of his thesis
[
Gre60b
]
was extending Dehn’s algorithm from surface groups
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
with
g>1
(see §
1.2.2
) to broader classes. This class of groups, which lie somewhat outside the scope of this survey, is now called
small cancellation groups
, as they satisfy the “small cancellation condition”
C^{\prime}(1/6)
(historically, groups satisfying this condition were also called
sixth groups
). This condition, which traces back to the 1940s in work by Tartakovskii
[
Tar49
]
(and independently Britton
[
Bri56
,
Bri57
]
), is intuitively speaking a condition on how much the relators of a given group presentation overlap. It can be used to guarantee a solution to the word problem and the conjugacy problem, using a well-known result known as
Greendlinger’s lemma
, which gives rise to a similar algorithm to Dehn’s in the setting of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
. Greendlinger would expand on his work in a series of articles throughout the 1960s
[
Gre60c
,
Gre60d
,
Gri
]
, which would be generalized by Lyndon
[
Lyn66
,
Lyn72
]
and his student Schupp
[
Sch68
,
Sch70
]
. Today, a classical reference for small cancellation theory is
[
LS77
, Chapter V]
.

Greendlinger himself did not work directly with one-relator groups, even though his results were all directly inspired by this subject. After he moved from New York to the USSR (forgoing an NSF Grant for working with B. H. Neumann in Manchester), Greendlinger would end up as one of the core members of the algebraic school of mathematics in Ivanovo State University, and then in Tula, where he began working in 1967
[
BDTU17
]
. He would bring many of the results and methods from Magnus to the USSR, and many of Greendlinger’s students would end up proving significant results on one-relator groups. One of his students in Ivanovo was D. I. Moldavanskii, who became a graduate student of Greendlinger’s in 1964, but ”almost immediately declared that small cancellation theory did not appeal to him”
[
Aza16
, p. 4]
. Greendlinger, very familiar with, and having access to, Western literature on the subject, gave Moldavanskii a copy of Baumslag’s 1964 survey
[
Bau64
]
on one-relator groups, which ends with a question on the abelian subgroups of one-relator groups. Moldavanskii began working on this question, which would turn out to be very fortuitous for the development of one-relator groups. His approach to the subject would pass (more or less explicitly) via
HNN-extensions
, and would lead to a simplification of the Magnus hierarchy. This, in turn, leads to simplifications of the classical results, and for deeper results to be proved, heralding in a new era of one-relator group theory.

Before presenting Moldavanskii’s method, we give a brief summary of HNN-extensions. Such extensions were first introduced by G. Higman, B. H. Neumann, and H. Neumann
[
HNN49
]
in 1949, and carries their initials. The main idea of the extensions is that if
G
is a group with two isomorphic subgroups
H_{1},H_{2}\leq G
, then such an isomorphism can be extended to an inner automorphism of a larger group
G^{\ast}
into which
G
embeds. Concretely, if
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid R\rangle
, and
\varphi\colon H_{1}\to H_{2}
is an isomorphism between two subgroups of
G
, then the
HNN-extension

G^{\ast}_{\varphi}
of
G
with
associated subgroups

H_{1}
and
H_{2}
is the group with the presentation

(1.24)

G^{\ast}_{\varphi}=\textnormal{}\langle A,t\mid R,\>th_{1}t^{-1}=\varphi(h_{2})\>(\forall h_{1}\in H_{1})\rangle

where
t
is some new symbol, called the
stable letter
. A basic result proved in
[
HNN49
]
is that the natural map
G\to G^{\ast}_{\varphi}
is injective, and one original motivation for introducing these extensions was to prove that
every countable group
G
is isomorphic to a subgroup of a two-generator group
H
. However, 10 years after its introduction, the construction would find its key use by J. L. Britton (a student of B. H. Neumann’s) in his proof of the undecidability of the word problem for groups.

Let
G^{\ast}_{\varphi}
be the group in (
1.24
), and let
w
be a word written as

(1.25)

u_{0}t^{\varepsilon_{1}}u_{1}t^{\varepsilon_{2}}\cdots t^{\varepsilon_{n}}u_{n}

where
u_{i}\in F_{A}
and
\varepsilon_{i}=\pm 1
for all
0\leq i\leq n
. Suppose for simplicity of formulation that
n>0
. Then Britton realized that deciding that the word problem in
G^{\ast}_{\varphi}
can be formulated in terms of
H_{1},H_{2}
, and
G
in the following way:

Lemma 1.5.7

(Britton
35
35

35

HNN-extensions, and an essentially equivalent form of Britton’s Lemma, also appear independently in Novikov’s proof of the undecidability of the word problem in groups, where they play a key role
[
Nov52
,
Nov55
]
.

[
Bri57
, Lemma 4]
)
.

If
w=1
in
G_{\varphi}^{\ast}
, then
w
contains a subword of the form
th_{1}t^{-1}
or
t^{-1}h_{2}t
, where
h_{1}\in H_{1}
and
h_{2}\in H_{2}
.

Thus, via this “spelling theorem”, one can immediately get a solution to the word problem as soon as one can solve membership in
H_{1},H_{2}\in G
. A stronger form of Britton’s Lemma, called Theorem A by Britton
[
Bri63
]
, has a direct proof via normal forms in amalgamated free products, see
[
Mil68
]
. A direct consequence of Britton’s Lemma is, of course, the aforementioned result that the map
G\to G^{\ast}_{\varphi}
is injective. But, more generally speaking, the lemma allows
encodings
into groups of “combinatorial sequences”, particularly those associated to the instructions of a (universal) Turing machine. In finitely presented
semi
groups, such encodings are rather straightforward (cf. §
1.4.4
), but for groups the first known encodings, by Boone
[
Boo57
]
and Novikov
[
Nov55
]
, were messy and full of combinatorial intricacies. The simplicity of Britton’s formulation permitted the explosion of new undecidability results in the 1950s (again see §
1.4.4
).

This simplicity of formulation would also turn out to be applicable to one-relator group theory. In 1967, Moldavanskii
[
Mol67
]
would be the first to notice this, and used some of the properties of HNN-extensions in the study of the Magnus hierarchy. First, we remark that if
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
is a one-relator group in which some generator
t
, actually appearing in
r
, has exponent sum zero in
r
, then it is not hard to see that
G
is an HNN-extension of a one-relator group
G_{1}
, where the associated subgroups are Magnus subgroups of
G_{1}
and
t
is the stable letter (for a proof see
[
MS73
, Theorem 1]
). Indeed, continuing the example (
1.10
) and (
1.11
), the stable letter is
b
, the first associated subgroup is
\langle a_{0},c_{0}\rangle
, the second is
\langle a_{1},c_{1}\rangle
, and the isomorphism is induced by
a_{0}\mapsto a_{1},c_{0}\mapsto c_{1}
. Furthermore, by using Nielsen transformations and Euclidean division in the obvious way, we can always assume that at least one generator of
G
has exponent sum zero in
r
and appears in
r
. Let us call this the
Euclidean trick
. For example, in the torus knot group
\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid a^{2}=b^{3}\rangle
, we can apply the Nielsen transformation
a\mapsto ba
and fixing
b
, followed by the Nielsen transformation fixing
a
and mapping
b\mapsto a^{2}b
, which results in the isomorphism

\mathcal{T}_{2,3}=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid a^{2}=b^{3}\rangle\cong\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid ababa^{-2}b=1\rangle.

In this new presentation,
a
has exponent sum zero. Hence, our group is an HNN-extension with stable letter
a
, and applying the Magnus method (from §
1.3.1
) we see that it is an HNN-extension of
\textnormal{}\langle b_{0},b_{1},b_{2}\mid b_{1}b_{2}b_{0}=1\rangle
, a free group.

The Euclidean trick is sufficient for proving certain results about one-relator groups, and Moldavanskii uses it e.g. to give a necessary and sufficient criterion for a one-relator group to be (f.g. free)-by-cyclic (see §
1.5.5
below). However, in general the Euclidean trick will not lend itself easily to inductive arguments, since it can increase the length of the relator.
36
36

36

Moldavanskii seemingly missed this fact at first, since he only in a footnote
[
Mol67
, Proof of Theorem 3]
remarks that the Magnus trick is needed to make a proof of his correct.
Instead, we need another trick. Suppose (for simplicity) that
G_{1}=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid r(a,b)=1\rangle
, where the exponent sums of both
a
and
b
are non-zero, say
\alpha
resp.
\beta
. Then we can map
G_{1}
into the group

G_{1}^{\prime}=\textnormal{}\langle y,x,b\mid r(y,b)=1,b=x^{\alpha}\rangle\cong G_{1}\ast_{b=x^{\alpha}}\mathbb{Z}

where the exponent sum of
x
is zero. When rewriting the relator via the Magnus process, the relator length does indeed decrease compared to that of
G_{1}
, allowing the use of induction. Let us call this the
Magnus trick
. Using this trick, we can then prove the following:

Theorem 1.5.8

(Moldavanskii, 1967
[
Mol67
]
)
.

Every one-relator group
G_{1}=\textnormal{}\langle A_{1}\mid r_{1}=1\rangle
can be embedded in a one-relator group
G^{\prime}_{1}
which is the HNN-extension of a one-relator group
G_{2}=\textnormal{}\langle A_{2}\mid r_{2}=1\rangle
, in which the associated subgroups are Magnus subgroups and
|r_{2}|<|r_{1}|
.

The finite hierarchy of one-relator groups
G_{1},G_{2},\dots,G_{n}
that this gives rise to is called the
Magnus–Moldavanskii hierarchy
. Notice that the groups of this hierarchy are the same as the original Magnus hierarchy, with the only conceptual difference being the addition of the HNN-extensions. Furthermore, Theorem
1.5.8
as stated does not appear in Moldavanskii’s article, but is implicit in his reasoning (e.g.
[
Mol67
, p. 1379]
). The main result of Moldavanskii’s article is the following:

Theorem 1.5.9

(Moldavanskii, 1967
[
Mol67
]
)
.

Any abelian subgroup of a one-relator group is cyclic, free abelian of rank
2
, or locally cyclic.

This
almost
resolved Baumslag’s 1964 question of the abelian subgroups of one-relator groups. However, it leaves unanswered the question of which locally cyclic groups can appear as subgroups of a one-relator group, and in particular whether
(\mathbb{Q},+)
can appear as a subgroup of some one-relator group. This has a negative answer, and we shall see its full resolution, given by B. B. Newman in 1968, later in §
1.7.3
.

Moldavanskii continued to use this new hierarchy, and other tools, to prove more results about one-relator groups and combinatorial group theory in general. For example, in 1969 he gave an algorithm for deciding whether two subgroups of a free group are conjugate
[
Mol69b
]
(cf. also
[
Gre70
]
), and an extension of the Nielsen method to free products of groups, resulting in a new proof of Grushko’s theorem
[
Mol69c
]
(for more on extensions of Nielsen’s methods in the context of one-relator groups, see §
1.6.3
). The same year, he also gave a complete proof of the following claim made by Magnus
[
Mag32
, p. 297]
in 1932:

Theorem 1.5.10

(Magnus / Moldavanskii
[
Mol69a
]
)
.

Except for
\mathbb{Z}
and
\BS(1,k)
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, every one-relator group contains a free group of rank
2

Note that the class of exceptional groups in Theorem
1.5.10
has a number of other characterizations, and appears as a frequent class of “easy” one-relator groups. They are, among other things precisely the
amenable
one-relator groups, and as proved by Gildenhuys
[
Gil79
]
, the groups in Theorem
1.5.10
also form precisely the class of solvable groups of cohomological dimension
\leq 2
. We shall see some extensions by other students of Greendlinger of Theorem
1.5.10
in §
1.7
.

For a final note on the Magnus–Moldavanskii hierarchy, we note that a modern and simple proof of its validity was given by McCool & Schupp
[
MS73
]
, which continues to be an excellent resource for understanding the elements of one-relator group theory. Indeed, therein they use the well-developed machinery of HNN-extensions to give simple proofs of the
Freiheitssatz
(§
1.3.1
), the decidability of the word problem (§
1.3.2
), and the B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem (which we will encounter later in §
1.6.1
).

1.5.5.
Free-by-cyclic one-relator groups

One important class of one-relator groups are those which are
free-by-cyclic
. Recall that a group
G
is said to be free-by-cyclic if there exists a short exact sequence

(1.26)

1\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow 1,

where
F
is a free group and
C
is cyclic. We do not
a priori
assume that
F
is finitely generated, nor non-trivial; similarly, we do not require
C
to be non-trivial. However, in essentially all cases we will encounter in this brief section
F
will be finitely generated, and
C
will be infinite cyclic. Note that in the case that
C=\mathbb{Z}
,
G
splits as a semidirect product
G=F\rtimes_{\varphi}\mathbb{Z}
, where
\mathbb{Z}
acts by
F
via conjugation by some automorphism
\varphi\in\Aut(F)
.

Let now
G
be a one-relator group. In 1967, Moldavanskii
[
Mol67
]
proved that if
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}
is any surjective homomorphism, then it is possible to find a generating set
a_{1}^{\prime},a_{2}^{\prime},\dots,a_{n}^{\prime}
of
G
, with a defining relator
r^{\prime}=1
, such that
\ker(\phi)
is precisely the normal closure of
a_{2}^{\prime},\dots,a_{n}^{\prime}
. In particular,
a_{1}^{\prime}
has exponent sum zero in
r^{\prime}
. Using this result, he proved that
[
Mol67
, Corollary 2]
the commutator subgroup of
G
is finitely generated if and only if the following holds:
n=2
, and the normal closure of
a_{2}^{\prime}
is a finitely generated free group. Combining his results, we obtain the following characterization of precisely when this happens:

Theorem 1.5.11

(Moldavanskii, 1967
[
Mol67
]
)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid r=1\rangle
be a non-abelian
37
37

37

In the statement of
[
Mol67
, Corollary 2]
, this assumption is inadvertently omitted, but it is there in the proof, cf. the statement of
[
Mol67
, Theorem 1]
.
one-relator group with the exponent sum of
a
in
r
equal to zero. Let
b_{m}
resp.
b_{M}
be such that
m
resp.
M
is the smallest resp. largest index occurring when rewriting
r
over
b_{i}=a^{i}ba^{-i}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

G
is (finitely generated free)-by-cyclic;

(2)

The commutator subgroup
G^{\prime}
is finitely generated;

(3)

m<M
, and
a_{m}
resp.
a_{M}
occur exactly once in the rewritten form of
r
.

Remark 1.5.12
.

The equivalence (1)
\iff
(3) is often called “Brown’s criterion” for a one-relator group to be (finitely generated free)-by-cyclic, as it was independently rediscovered in 1987 by Brown
[
Bro87
, §4]
.

Now, (finitely generated free)-by-cyclic groups are residually finite, as proved by Mal’cev
[
Mal58
, Theorem 1, p. 50]
in 1958, see also
[
Bau71a
]
(also extended by Wong
[
Won87
]
). Thus one consequence of Moldavanskii’s results is the following:

Corollary 1.5.13

(
[
Mol67
, Corollary 1]
)
.

If the commutator subgroup of a one-relator group
G
is finitely generated, then
G
is residually finite.

Given a one-relator group
G
and a surjective homomorphism
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}
, Moldavanskii also notes that his criterion gives an effective procedure for deciding whether or not
\ker(\phi)
is finitely generated. In particular, one corollary of his methods is the following.

Corollary 1.5.14

(Moldavanskii, 1967
[
Mol67
, p. 1375]
)
.

There is an algorithm for deciding if a one-relator group is (finitely generated free)-by-cyclic.

Residual finiteness is, in general, a significantly weaker property than being free-by-cyclic, even in the class of one-relator groups. It remains an open problem whether residual finiteness is decidable for one-relator groups.

1.6.
One-relator groups with torsion (1960–1980)

It seems to be commonly believed that the presence of elements of finite order in a group with a single defining relation is a complicating rather than a simplifying factor. This note is in support of the opposite point of view.

—G. Baumslag, 1967
[
Bau67c
]

[…]
apart from the complications introduced by having elements of finite order, one-relator groups with torsion behave much like free groups.

—S. J. Pride, 1977
[
Pri77b
]

This far into the story, none of the theorems about one-relator groups have singled out one-relator groups with torsion as forming a particularly simple class compared to their torsion-free counterparts. Indeed, sometimes the opposite seems true: for example, Lyndon’s Identity Theorem (Theorem
1.4.4
, §
1.4.2
) is significantly neater to state in the torsion-free case. Nevertheless, in the 1960s and 1970s a number of remarkable results would be proved for one-relator groups with torsion which, in full accordance with the above quotations. The crowning achievement of such theorems was the
B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem
, which in modern terminology states that
one-relator groups with torsion are hyperbolic
. Before stating this result, we state a number of important conjectures made by G. Baumslag around this time:

Conjecture 1.6.1

(Baumslag’s Conjectures)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r^{n}=1\rangle
, where
n>1
, be a one-relator group with torsion. Then the following hold:

(1)

G
is virtually free-by-cyclic
[
Bau86
, Problem 6]

(2)

G
is coherent
[
Bau86
, Problem 1]

(3)

G
is residually finite
[
Bau67c
, Conjecture A]
,

(4)

G
is hopfian
[
Bau67c
, Conjecture A
\flat
]
,

We make some remarks on these conjectures. First, even for arbitrary groups
G
, (1) is the strongest of these properties, and implies all others: (1)
\implies
(3), and (3)
\implies
(4) are both classical results. The implication (1)
\implies
(2) is due to Feighn & Handel
[
FH99
]
in 1999. Since
\BS(2,3)
is non-hopfian, it follows that the only one of the four conjectures that can hold in the torsion-free case is (2). In fact, that is precisely the conjecture that Baumslag made in
[
Bau86
, Problem 1]
:
all
one-relator groups are coherent. We note that already in 1974, Baumslag
[
Bau71b
]
had stated that it remained an open problem whether all one-relator groups are coherent. The above conjectures would be driving forces behind many of the developments of one-relator group theory in the 1970s until the present day. Today, we know that they all have affirmative answers, including (2) in the general case. In this section, we will present some of the partial progress made on these conjectures before their full resolution, and present some general theorems indicating why one-relator groups with torsion are significantly simpler than their torsion-free counterparts.

1.6.1.
The B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem

I don’t believe it! I don’t believe it!

—W. Magnus in 1968, upon seeing B. B. Newman present his Spelling Theorem
[
NB21a
, p. 119]

He could not believe that an unheard-of mathematician, from some unknown university in outback Australia, could have come up with these results.

—B. B. Newman, remarking on Magnus’ reaction above
[
NB21a
, p. 120]

Spelling theorems, being theorems which provide information on the spelling of words equal to
1
in a group, date back to the very beginning of one-relator group theory, the prime example being Dehn’s Spelling Theorem (Theorem
1.2.2
) for the fundamental groups of
2
-surfaces. Britton’s Lemma (see Lemma
1.5.7
), with its importance for the Magnus–Moldavanskii, is another. And in 1968, B. B. Newman, a PhD student of Baumslag’s, provided perhaps the most important one. We will be brief on the biographical details of this story, since this is expanded on at length by the author in
[
NB21a
]
. We mention only that the starting point for Newman’s work on the subject was Baumslag giving him a draft of the chapter on one-relator groups from the now famous book by Magnus, Karrass & Solitar
[
MKS66
]
. After some work, he was able to prove the following remarkable theorem:

Theorem 1.6.2

(The B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem, 1968)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r^{n}=1\rangle
be a one-relator group with torsion, with
n>1
, and
r
cyclically reduced and not a proper power. Let
w\in F_{A}
be a non-trivial word such that
w=1
in
G
. Then
w
contains a subword
u
such that either
u
or
u^{-1}
is a subword of
r^{n}
, and such that the length of
u
is strictly more than
\frac{n-1}{n}
times the length of
r^{n}
.

The proof of this theorem goes, as expected, by the Magnus hierarchy. However, the 1968 article in which the theorem was first announced was only a bulletin article
[
New68a
]
, and no proofs appeared therein. The proof only appeared in Newman’s PhD thesis
[
New68b
]
, which was until recently completely inaccessible: indeed, the author’s (eventually successful) hunt for this thesis is the subject of
[
NB21a
]
. A simpler proof of the Spelling Theorem was given by McCool & Schupp
[
MS73
]
in 1973, using the full power of HNN-extensions (see §
1.5.4
).

We now give some immediate consequences of the Spelling Theorem, all of which appear in B. B. Newman’s thesis. First, and most obviously, it gives a linear-time solution to the word problem in all one-relator groups with torsion. This is significantly better than the time-complexity of Magnus’ solution to the word problem in general one-relator groups (see §
1.3.3
). Next, Newman realized that his Spelling Theorem gave an easy solution also to the conjugacy problem:

Theorem 1.6.3

(B. B. Newman, 1968
[
New68b
, Theorem 3.2.3]
)
.

The conjugacy problem is decidable in any one-relator group with torsion.

Newman also solved (see
[
New68b
, Theorem 3.3.1]
) the
power problem
in one-relator groups with torsion, i.e. the problem of deciding whether a given element in a group is a proper power or not. This problem, which we will not discuss further, appeared in a similar setting of small cancellation groups also in work by Lipschutz
[
Lip65
,
Lip68
]
and Reinhart
[
Rei62
]
. The next corollary of the Spelling Theorem was a stronger form of the
Freiheitssatz
.

Theorem 1.6.4

(B. B. Newman, 1968
[
New68b
, Corollary 2.1.6]
)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{k}\mid r^{n}=1\rangle
with
n>1
be a one-relator group with torsion, where
r
is cyclically reduced involving the letters
a_{1},a_{2}\in A
non-trivially. Let
\beta
be any integer which does not divide the exponent sum of
a_{1}
in
r^{n}
. Then
\{a_{1}^{\beta},a_{2},\dots,a_{k}\}
freely generates a free subgroup of
G
.

Thus, for example, in
\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid(a^{2}b^{3})^{2}=1\rangle
, the subgroup generated by
\{a^{3},b\}
is free on that basis. Note that the ordinary
Freiheitssatz
only gives information about cyclic subgroups for this example. Thus Theorem
1.6.4
gives an abundance of free subgroups of one-relator groups with torsion. The same year, Ree & Mendelsohn
[
RM68
]
had proved a weaker version of this result, for the two-generator case, by using representations for some one-relator groups with torsion in
\SL(2,\mathbb{C})
. Magnus
[
Mag75
]
, however, has shown that their technique cannot yield the full strength of Theorem
1.6.4
. As a final application of the B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem, we mention the following subgroup result.

Theorem 1.6.5

(Newman, 1968
[
New68b
, Corollary 2.3.4]
)
.

The centralizer of every non-trivial element of a one-relator group with torsion is cyclic.

This theorem was also proved and extended by Karrass & Solitar
[
KS71a
, p. 956]
, who elaborated on the properties of free products with a
malnormal
amalgamated subgroup. B. B. Newman had made central use of some of the properties of such amalgams, particularly using the malnormality of Magnus subgroups in one-relator groups to prove his results. This property of Magnus subgroups remains also a key theme in the modern setting. We elaborate on this theme in §
1.7.1
.

These were only some the initial applications of the B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem, all of which appeared already in his thesis. Newman also used it to study abelian and solvable subgroups of one-relator groups with torsion; we detail this further in §
1.7.3
. Furthermore, it would come to play a particularly prominent role in the subsequent work by S. J. Pride on one-relator groups with torsion, which we will detail more in §
1.6.3
. We mention one of his results here, as its proof heavily relies on the Spelling Theorem. B. B. Newman and Pride once thought that
every
finitely generated subgroup of a one-relator group would either be free or have torsion
[
Pri77b
, p. 483]
. That this is not true can be seen either from Theorem
1.6.8
