together with the fact that not every one-relator group with torsion is free, or else from the example (
1.27
) below. Nevertheless, much can be said of the
two
-generator subgroups of one-relator groups with torsion:

Theorem 1.6.6

(Pride, 1977
[
Pri77b
]
)
.

Every two-generator subgroup of a one-relator group with torsion is either a free product of cyclic groups, or a one-relator group with torsion.

Further results on subgroups of one-relator groups is the subject of §
1.7
. We now conclude this present section with some brief remarks on the Spelling Theorem. First, a strengthening of the theorem was obtained by Gurevich
[
Gur72
,
Gur73
]
, see also Schupp
[
Sch76a
]
for a complete proof and further strengthening. As noted by Pride
[
Pri74
, p. 57]
, a proof of this stronger result can be obtained by the same method as the proof of Newman’s result given by McCool & Schupp
[
MS73
]
. Furthermore, one of the consequences, indeed one of the reformulations, of the Spelling Theorem is that one-relator groups with torsion admit
Dehn presentations
. This is known to be equivalent to Gromov hyperbolicity, and hence
all one-relator groups with torsion are hyperbolic
. This places many of the above theorems in their proper context: for example, in any hyperbolic group the centralizer is virtually cyclic, yielding in particular Theorem
1.6.5
for one-relator groups with torsion. However, the proof of Theorem
1.6.5
predates the definition of hyperbolic groups by two decades.

1.6.2.
Residual and virtual properties

As mentioned before, today we know that all of Baumslag’s conjectures (Conjecture
1.6.1
) are true. However, there had been some partial progress on these before this resolution, including for residual finiteness. We now present some of these results. We remark first that a natural conjecture might be that all one-relator groups with torsion are residually (torsion-free) nilpotent. But this is not so, as indicated by the following example due to B. B. Newman
[
New68b
, p. 72]
: let

(1.27)

G=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid(a^{-1}b^{-1}ab^{2})^{2}=1\rangle

Then the index
2
subgroup of
G
generated by
\langle a,b^{2},bab^{-1}\rangle
admits the presentation
\textnormal{}\langle x,y,z\mid[y,z][y,x]=y\rangle
, and clearly
y
lies in the intersection of every term in the lower central series. Hence
G
is not residually torsion-free nilpotent. One of the first theorems on residual properties of one-relator groups with torsion was also due to B. B. Newman, in his thesis:

Theorem 1.6.7

(
[
New68b
, Corollary 2.1.7]
)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{k}\mid r^{n}=1\rangle
where
n>1
. Then
G
is residually a two-generator one-relator group with torsion.

The proof is not difficult, but uses the Spelling Theorem. A few years later, Fischer, Karrass & Solitar
[
FKS72
]
wrote a short but influential note giving structural information about one-relator groups with torsion. Two of their results are as follows:

Theorem 1.6.8

(Fischer, Karrass & Solitar, 1972
[
FKS72
]
)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r^{n}=1\rangle
with
n>1
be a one-relator group with torsion. Then: (1)
G
is virtually torsion-free; and (2)
G
is virtually free if and only if
r
is a primitive word in
F_{A}
.

The authors also prove that the elements of finite order in
G
generate a subgroup that is the free product of conjugates of the cyclic group generated by
r
, a result that appears implicitly already in work of Cohen & Lyndon
[
CL63
]
. Part (2) of Theorem
1.6.8
was reproved, among other results, by Karrass, Solitar & Pietrowski
[
KPS73
]
the next year. Part (1) of Theorem
1.6.8
was expanded on by Fischer
[
Fis77
]
, who proved that (with the same notation as in Theorem
1.6.8
) if
H
is a subgroup of
G
with
[G:H]=k<\infty
and
H
is torsion-free, then
n
divides
k
. Furthermore, if some generator in
r
has exponent sum
s\neq 0
, then
G
has a torsion-free
|s|
-relator subgroup of finite index; if further
\gcd(s,n)=1
then
G
has a one-relator subgroup
H
of finite index. As a corollary, he deduced that if
G
contains a torsion-free subgroup
H
with
[G:H]=kn
, then
H
is a
k
-relator group. Finally, while we cannot pass into the details of
ends
of groups here, instead referring the reader to
[
Sta68
]
, we note that another important result proved in
[
FKS72
]
is that any freely indecomposable one-relator group with torsion has at most one end. Thus, in this case, it follows from Theorem
1.6.8
that if the defining word is non-trivial and not a power of a primitive word, then the group has exactly one end.

We now list some families of one-relator groups with torsion which were known to be residually finite before the full resolution of Baumslag’s conjecture:

(1)

(Fischer, 1977
[
Fis77
]
) The groups
\textnormal{}\langle A\cup B\mid(uv)^{n}=1\rangle
,
n>1
, where
u\in F_{A}
and
v\in F_{B}
are non-trivial elements, and
A\cap B=\varnothing
.

(2)

(Fischer, 1977
[
Fis77
]
) The groups
\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid(ab^{k}a^{-1}y^{r})^{n}=1\rangle
, where
n>1
,
n
does not divide
r
or
k
, and
\gcd(r+k,n)=1
.

(3)

(B. Baumslag & Tretkoff, 1978
[
BT78
]
) The groups
\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid(a^{-1}b^{l}ab^{l})^{n}=1\rangle
where
n>1
and
l\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(4)

(B. Baumslag & Levin, 1979
[
BL79
]
) The groups
\textnormal{}\langle A,x\mid(xux^{-1}v)^{n}=1\rangle
where
n>1
and
u,v\in F_{A}
and either (i)
u,v
are words over disjoint sets of generators; or (ii)
u
and
v
are non-trivial powers of a single element, e.g.
\textnormal{}\langle x,y\mid(xy^{k}x^{-1}y^{l})^{n}=1\rangle
with
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(5)

(Allenby & Tang, 1981
[
AT81a
,
AT81b
]
) Many different sporadic classes of one-relator groups with torsion; a typical example is
[
AT81a
, Theorem 4.4]
, which states that
\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid([a^{\alpha},b^{\beta}][a^{-\gamma},b^{-\delta}])^{t}=1\rangle
,
n>1
and
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\mathbb{N}
.

After these sporadic results, Egorov
[
Ego81
]
(a student of Moldavanskii) proved the following remarkable result on positive one-relator groups with torsion:

Theorem 1.6.9

(Egorov, 1981
[
Ego81
]
)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r^{n}=1\rangle
with
n>1
and
r
a positive word in
F_{A}
, i.e. including no inverse of any generator. Then
G
is residually finite.

For extensions of this result, we refer the reader to Wise
[
Wis01
]
; for comparisons with positive torsion-free one-relator groups, see §
1.7.5
. Finally, we mention a connection with another “residual-like” property. Tang
[
Tan82
]
proved that the groups
\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid(a^{m}b^{n})^{k}=1\rangle
where
k>1
are all
conjugacy separable
, a stronger property than residual finiteness. Recall that a group
G
is conjugacy separable if for any
g,h\in G
which are not conjugate, there exists some finite quotient
Q
of
G
such that the images of
g
and
h
are not conjugate in
Q
. Thus conjugacy separability is to the conjugacy problem what residual finiteness is to the word problem. Further results on conjugacy separability in some classes of one-relator groups with torsion were obtained by Allenby & Tang
[
AT86
]
a few years later, see also
[
KMT95
]
. Today, like residual finiteness, we know that one-relator groups with torsion are conjugacy separable, a result due to Minasyan & Zalesski
[
MZ13
]
.

1.6.3.
The isomorphism problem for one-relator groups with torsion

By deep results of Dahmani & Guirardel
[
DG11
]
, we know today that the isomorphism problem for all hyperbolic groups is decidable. As mentioned in §
1.6.1
(Theorem
1.6.2
), all one-relator groups with torsion are hyperbolic. Hence, the isomorphism problem is decidable for one-relator groups with torsion. However, this problem was by no means a mystery before this point, and it was known to be decidable in a large number of cases. Thus, we will now go through some of this earlier literature on the subject, focussing particularly on the remarkable results by Pride.

First, in line with asking about isomorphisms of one-relator groups, it is natural to ask whether there is a converse to the isomorphism result from Theorem
1.5.2
. Such a converse would show that the isomorphism problem for one-relator groups would be somewhat more tractable, since it would show that it would be sufficient to solve it in the torsion case. However, B. B. Newman suggested to Pride that a counterexample to this converse might exist
[
Pri74
, p. 71]
, and Pride was able to construct such a counterexample:

Theorem 1.6.10

(Pride, 1974
[
Pri74
, Theorem 4.7]
)
.

Let
n\geq 1
and consider the groups

B_{n}=\textnormal{}\langle a,t\mid(a^{-3}t^{-1}a^{2}t)^{n}=1\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad C_{n}=\textnormal{}\langle a,t\mid(a^{-1}[t,a]^{2})^{n}=1\rangle.

Then
B_{n}\cong C_{n}
if and only if
n=1
.

Thus, this theorem provided a counterexample to the converse of Theorem
1.5.2
. Indeed, it also provided another counterexample to an old conjecture by Magnus (Conjecture
1.3.5
), since it proves that there is an isomorphism

B_{1}=\textnormal{}\langle a,t\mid t^{-1}a^{2}t=a^{3}\rangle\cong\textnormal{}\langle a,t\mid[t,a]^{2}=a\rangle=C_{1}

but it is readily checked that
t^{-1}a^{2}ta^{-3}
and
[t,a]^{2}a^{-1}
are not related by a free group automorphism (this example was also obtained by Brunner
[
Bru74
]
, cf.
[
Bru76
]
).

The above negative result is, in hindsight, not surprising: the isomorphism problem for one-relator groups with torsion turns out to be significantly easier than in the torsion-free case. To state some of the positive results, we recall the notion of a
T
-system
(Ger.
Transitivitätssystem
, i.e. transitivity system) of a group, following
[
Pri75
]
. Let
G
be an
n
-generated group, and let

\mathfrak{g}=(g_{1},\dots,g_{n}),\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=(g^{\prime}_{1},\dots,g^{\prime}_{n})

be two generating tuples for
G
. We say that
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
are
Nielsen equivalent
if there is some automorphism

x_{i}\mapsto Y_{i}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}),\quad i=1,2,\dots,n

of the free group

F_{n}
on
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
such that
g_{i}^{\prime}=Y_{i}(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})
. Thus, for example, the tuples
(g_{1},g_{2})
and
(g_{2}g_{1}^{-1},g_{1})
are always Nielsen equivalent. We say that
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
lie in the same
T
-system
if there is some automorphism
\varrho\in\Aut(G)
such that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is Nielsen equivalent to
\varrho(\mathfrak{g})
, where

\varrho(\mathfrak{g})=(\varrho(g_{1}),\dots,\varrho(g_{n})).

This equivalence relation was defined by B. H. Neumann & H. Neumann
[
NN51
]
in 1951. For our purposes, the importance of
T
-systems comes via Magnus’ Conjugacy Theorem (Theorem
1.3.3
). First, continuing the above notation, let

\textnormal{}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid r_{1}=1,\dots,r_{m}=1\rangle

is a presentation of
G
with respect to the generating tuple
\mathfrak{g}
(i.e. the kernel of the map
x_{i}\mapsto g_{i}
coincides with the normal closure of the
r_{j}
). Then
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
lies in the same
T
-system as
\mathfrak{g}
if and only if there is some
\phi\in\Aut(F_{n})
such that

\textnormal{}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mid\phi(r_{1})=1,\dots,\phi(r_{m})=1\rangle

is a presentation of
G
with respect to the generating tuple
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
. In general,
G
may have many different
m
-relator presentations with respect to
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
, and one of the difficulties (indeed undecidability) of the isomorphism problem in full generality arises from this issue. However, when
m=1
, i.e. the one-relator case, Magnus’ Conjugacy Theorem (Theorem
1.3.3
) tells us that two generating sets lying in the same
T
-system gives very strict control over the defining relator:

Proposition 1.6.11
.

Two one-relator presentations
\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
and
\textnormal{}\langle A\mid s=1\rangle
of a group
G
are associated with elements from the same
T
-system in
G
if and only if
s
is equal in
F_{A}
to the image of
r^{\pm 1}
under some automorphism of
F_{A}
.

In particular, if a one-relator group
G
only has one
T
-system of generating tuples, then the isomorphism problem is decidable for the class of one-relator groups with respect to that one-relator group; in other words, it is decidable whether a given one-relator group is isomorphic to
G
or not. Brunner’s example of
\BS(2,3)
above shows that in general a one-relator group may have infinitely many
T
-systems of generating tuples. In the case of one-relator groups with torsion, however, more can be said.

First, for example, consider the class of groups
S_{n}=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid[a,b]^{n}=1\rangle
, where
n>1
. These groups
S_{n}
are virtually surface groups
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
, indeed it is not hard to show that
\ker(S_{n}\twoheadrightarrow D_{2n})
is isomorphic to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2n-1})
(see e.g.
[
BT08
]
). The fact that
S_{n}
is Fuchsian was observed also by Rosenberger
[
Ros73
]
, who studied these groups in 1973. He furthermore proved that the only generating sets
\{u,v\}
of
S_{n}
are those of the form where
[u,v]
is conjugate to
[a,b]^{\varepsilon}
, where
\varepsilon=\pm 1
(see also Purzitsky & Rosenberger
[
PR72
,
PR73
]
and Pride
[
Pri73
]
, cf.
[
Pri74
, p. 72]
). By Nielsen’s result (Proposition
1.2.4
), this implies that
\{u,v\}
is a basis of the underlying free group, so in particular
S_{n}
has only one
T
-system of generators. In 1972, Rosenberger
[
Ros72
]
also considered the “higher genus”
S_{n,g}
variants of the groups
S_{n}
, defined by

(1.28)

\textnormal{}\langle a_{1},b_{1},\dots,a_{g},b_{g}\mid([a_{1},b_{1}][a_{2},b_{2}]\cdots[a_{g},b_{g}])^{n}=1\rangle

with
n,g\geq 1
, and proved that the isomorphism problem for one-relator groups relative to this class is decidable. For other related examples, and a slight generalization, see
[
RK76
,
Ros76
]
.

However, a few years later, in 1977 Pride
[
Pri77a
]
was able to clarify the situation of generating tuples of all two-generator one-relator groups with torsion. He proved that if
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r^{n}=1\rangle
is a one-relator group with torsion, with
n>1
and
r
not a proper power, then
G
has only one
T
-system of generating tuples (when
n=2
) or
\frac{1}{2}\phi(n)
such systems when
n>2
. Here
\phi
denotes Euler’s totient function. He moreover showed that these
T
-systems can be effectively found, and hence deduced the following remarkable theorem:

Theorem 1.6.12

(Pride, 1977
[
Pri77a
]
)
.

The isomorphism problem is decidable for two-generator one-relator groups with torsion.

The method of proof involves much use of the B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem and, of course, induction on the length of the Magnus–Moldavanskii hierarchy. Beyond the isomorphism problem, other structural information can also be deduced from a one-relator group having only one
T
-system of generating tuples.

Theorem 1.6.13

(Pride, 1977
[
Pri77a
]
)
.

Let
G
be a two-generator one-relator group with torsion. Then:

(1)

G
is a hopfian group.

(2)

The automorphism group of
G
is finitely generated;

We remark that Rosenberger was able to extend these methods to a slightly more general setting; we do not recount all such extensions here, but mention a typical example. Let
X_{1}\cap X_{2}\neq\varnothing
, let
w_{i}\in F_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
be two words. Then Rosenberger
[
Ros77
]
gave conditions under which the group
\textnormal{}\langle X_{1},X_{2}\mid(w_{1}w_{2})^{n}=1\rangle
with
n>1
only has one
T
-system of generating tuples, and hence the isomorphism problem for one-relator groups is decidable with respect to this class.

1.7.
Subgroups of one-relator groups

Subgroups of zero-relator groups, i.e. free groups, are themselves zero-relator groups; this is the Nielsen–Schreier Theorem, as presented in §
1.2.3
. Similarly, many subgroups of one-relator groups are free, as the
Freiheitssatz
tells us. Thus, it seems natural to ask: what are the subgroups of one-relator groups? In particular, is every subgroup of a one-relator group a one-relator group? The answer to this latter question is emphatically
no
. The subgroup structure of one-relator group carries a great richness, and although we today know that all one-relator groups are coherent, much was known already before this was proved. In this section, we will present several different flavours of such results about subgroups of one-relator groups.

We first remark that there is, in general, an abundance of subgroups in one-relator groups. Baumslag & Miller
[
BM86
]
prove that certain one-relator groups contain many non-isomorphic subgroups. Indeed, they prove, using a simple argument that as soon as a group
G
contains a countably infinite number of non-isomorphic, freely indecomposable subgroups, then
G\ast\mathbb{Z}
contains continuously many non-isomorphic subgroups. For example, one can take the solvable Baumslag–Solitar group

\BS(1,2)=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid b^{-1}ab=a^{2}\rangle

in which the subgroup
E_{i}=\langle a,b^{2^{i}}\rangle
is solvable, and
E_{i}\not\cong E_{j}
if
i\neq j
since
H_{1}(E_{i},\mathbb{Z})
clearly grows in
i
. Thus
\BS(1,2)\ast\mathbb{Z}
contains continuously many non-isomorphic subgroups. Note also that every one-relator group can be embedded into a
2
-generator one-relator group (see e.g.
[
MKS66
, p. 259]
), and hence there are
2
-generator one-relator groups containing continuously many non-isomorphic subgroups, e.g. the Baumslag–Gersten group

(1.29)

\operatorname{BG}=\textnormal{}\langle a,t\mid ta^{-1}t^{-1}atat^{-1}=a^{2}\rangle

which is clearly an HNN-extension of
\BS(1,2)
. By the above results, it contains continuously many non-isomorphic subgroups. Thus it also contains countable subgroups which are not recursively presentable. Nevertheless, in general, much can be said about particular classes of subgroups of one-relator groups, particularly their
finitely generated
subgroups. In this section, we will see many examples of this.

We caution the reader that because of the often simultaneous and overlapping developments at this time, unlike previous sections, the subsections of this section (and the next) can be read more or less independently of one another, and will frequently pass from one subject to another with only some connective tissue.

1.7.1.
Magnus subgroups and malnormality

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
, with
r
cyclically reduced. Then recall (from §
1.3.1
) that a
Magnus subgroup
of
G
is any subgroup generated by a proper subset of
A
. The
Freiheitssatz
, being the first result about one-relator groups, was a result about these subgroups: they are free. More can be said about Magnus subgroups, however. One particular classical theme which we now expand on is that of
malnormality
. For an arbitrary group
G
, a non-trivial subgroup
H\leq G
is said to be
malnormal
if
gHg^{-1}\cap H=\{1\}
for all
g\in G\setminus H
. The terminology
malnormal
for this property was introduced by B. Baumslag in his 1965 PhD thesis
[
Bau65a
]
, see also
[
Bau68
, p. 601]
. The structure of a
finite
group with a malnormal subgroup is rather restricted. A finite group
G
has a (non-trivial) malnormal subgroup
H
if and only if it is a
Frobenius group
, i.e. it acts transitively on some finite set where no non-trivial element fixes more than one point, and some non-trivial element fixes some point. Then
H
being malnormal is a restatement of
H
being a
Frobenius complement
, and
G
splits as a semidirect product
G=K\rtimes H
where
K
is the (normal) subgroup consisting of all elements not in any conjugate of
H

[
Fro01
]
.

In general, in infinite groups malnormal subgroups are more complicated. Indeed, even the problem of deciding whether a subgroup of a
free
group is malnormal or not is non-trivial, albeit decidable
[
BMR99
]
. Early work on malnormal subgroups, other than that by B. Baumslag
[
Bau65a
,
Bau68
]
, are four other PhD theses, all of which were by students of G. Baumslag: the first is by Driscoll
[
Dri67
]
, who used it in her study of the conjugacy problem is some amalgamated free products; the second was Whittemore
[
Whi67
]
, who used malnormal subgroups in her study of the Frattini subgroups of amalgamated free products; and third by Taylor
[
Tay63
,
Lew67
]
38
38

38

Here, a possible source of confusion regarding surnames arises. Tekla Taylor and Jacques Lewin were the first two PhD students of G. Baumslag’s, and they married in 1965. Tekla took her husband’s surname, and the two would publish together frequently, including the article containing their famous Lewin–Lewin construction for the group ring of a torsion-free one-relator group
[
LL78
]
. Nevertheless, possibly because of delays in publishing, Tekla’s surname still appears as
Taylor
in 1968 in her joint article with Baumslag on the centre of one-relator groups
[
BT68
]
, see §
1.7.2
.
in her study of groups with unique roots. The fourth, and for us the most important, was by B. B. Newman, who proved the following remarkable result in his thesis:

Theorem 1.7.1

(B. B. Newman, 1968
[
New68b
, Lemma 2.3.1(I)]
)
.

Any Magnus subgroup of a one-relator group with torsion is malnormal.

This result, and a strengthening of it using the notion of
strong malnormality
, was used by Newman in his solution to the conjugacy problem in all one-relator groups with torsion. Newman’s result would also, many decades later, come to play a key role in Wise’s recent proof of the residual finiteness of one-relator groups with torsion
[
Wis09
]
. We remark that Moldavanskii
[
Mol68
]
further proved that the intersection of Magnus subgroups generated by disjoint subsets of the generators is always trivial in a one-relator group with torsion.

Outside the torsion case, Bagherzadeh
[
Bag76b
,
Bag76a
]
proved in 1976 that for any one-relator group
G
, if
H<G
is a Magnus subgroup, then
H\cap g^{-1}Hg
is cyclic for
g\not\in H
. Thus any Magnus subgroup of a one-relator group is what Bagherzadeh calls
cyclonormal
(a term which later appears also in work by Wise
[
Wis01
]
). It is somewhat surprising that new results are still proved about Magnus subgroups in general one-relator groups, even using mostly classical methods. Moldavanskii
[
Mol68
]
proved that if
G
is a one-relator group
\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
with
X_{1},X_{2}\subseteq A
and
X_{1}\cap X_{2}=\varnothing
, then
\langle X_{1}\rangle\cap\langle X_{2}\rangle
is either trivial or isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
. Collins
[
Col04
]
extended this result in 2004 by proving that if
X_{1},X_{2}\subset A
generate two Magnus subgroups
H_{1}=\langle X_{1}\rangle
and
H_{2}=\langle X_{2}\rangle
, then
H_{1}\cap H_{2}
is either free on
X_{1}\cap X_{2}
, or else is a free product of
\mathbb{Z}
with this group.

1.7.2.
The centre of one-relator groups

For any group
G
, it is natural to ask about its centre
Z(G)
. This is also true of one-relator groups. Indeed, Levi, in his 1933 investigation
[
Lev33
]
on free groups 1933, includes as an example the group
\BS(2,2)=\textnormal{}\langle x,y\mid x^{-1}y^{2}x=y^{2}\rangle
(see §
1.5.3
), and notes that its centre is infinite cyclic, and generated by the element
y^{2}
. Generalizing such examples, in 1935 Kalashnikov & Kurosh
[
KK35
]
studied subgroups of amalgamated free products in which the amalgamated subgroups are all subgroups of the centres of the factors, a precursor to the tree products which we shall encounter below. Computing the centre of finitely presented groups is in general an undecidable problem
39
39

39

This is not a direct consequence of the Adian–Rabin theorem
[
Adi55
,
Rab58
]
, but follows from a 1959 extension of it by Baumslag, Boone & Neumann
[
BBN59
]
.
, and Remeslennikov proved that there are finitely presented groups whose centre is not finitely generated
[
Rem74
]
(answering a question of Baumslag
[
Bau67a
, Question 1]
). However, it turns out that for one-relator groups, there is a complete answer. We give a brief exposition of these results in this section.

Perhaps the first general result on the centres of one-relator groups is that the centre of a one-relator group with torsion is necessarily trivial, proved in 1960
[
KMS60
, Corollary]
. The real impetus for the study of the centre of one-relator groups, however, came in 1964, when Murasugi
[
Mur64
]
proved several beautiful theorems. Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\mid R=1\rangle
be a one-relator group. Then Murasugi proved: first, if
n>2
, then
Z(G)=1
.
40
40

40

In a sequel article, Murasugi
[
Mur65
]
conjectured that any group with deficiency
d\geq 2
has trivial centre, which remains an open problem.
Furthermore, if we suppose that
n=2
,
G
is non-abelian, and
Z(G)
is non-trivial, then
Z(G)
is infinite cyclic. Thus the centre of any one-relator group
G
is either trivial, infinite cyclic, or
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
– and the last case happens if and only if
G\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
(and there is only one cyclically reduced one-relator presentation for
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, see §
1.3.2
). The proofs of this pass via the
Alexander polynomial
of
G
, as defined by Fox
[
Fox54
]
, and the proofs also give sufficient conditions for
Z(G)
to be trivial. However, the question of completely characterizing when
Z(G)
is trivial was left open. In 1968, this problem was solved by Baumslag & Taylor
[
BT68
]
, who proved that there exists an algorithm for deciding whether a one-relator group has trivial centre or not. This was the starting point for a natural line of investigation: what is the structure of one-relator groups with a non-trivial centre?

Some partial results in this line were obtained by Meskin, Pietrowski & Steinberg
[
MPS73
]
. The question was fully answered by Pietrowski in 1974 (although the results already appear in his 1972 PhD thesis
[
Pie72
]
) using subgroup theorems of Karrass & Solitar. Recall that a
tree product
of groups is the fundamental group of a graph of groups, where the underlying graph is a simple tree. A
stem
product is a tree product in which the underlying tree is a path, introduced in
[
KS70
]
.

Theorem 1.7.2

(Pietrowski, 1974
[
Pie74
]
)
.

Any one-relator group with non-trivial centre is a (finite) stem product of cyclic groups or an HNN-extension of such a group with cyclic associated subgroups.

Associated to any finite stem product of cyclic groups is a finite sequence of pairs of integers, corresponding to the amalgamated subgroups. This sequence can, it turns out, be used to essentially uniquely determine the one-relator group up to isomorphism. As a consequence, we have the following corollary:

Corollary 1.7.3

(Pietrowski, 1974
[
Pie74
]
)
.

The isomorphism problem is decidable for one-relator groups with non-trivial centre.

The problem of deciding precisely when a stem product of cyclic groups is a one-relator group is non-trivial and was left open by Pietrowski. Some partial results were obtained by, among others, McCool
[
McC91
]
, and it was eventually solved in 1999 by Metaftsis
[
Met99
]
. As pointed out by Meskin, the above structural result (Theorem
1.7.2
) makes no essential use of the fact that the group is a one-relator group, but applies equally well to any two-generator infinite (f.g. free)-by-cyclic groups with a non-trivial center. This was expanded on by Karrass, Pietrowski & Solitar
[
KPS73
]
, where they completely characterize the structure of infinite (f.g. free)-by-cyclic groups with a non-trivial centre as a tree product of infinite cyclic groups.

We mention another decidability result for one-relator groups with a non-trivial centre, proved by Moldavanskii & Timofeeva in 1987. Recall that a finitely generated subgroup
H\leq G
is said to be
separable
if for every
g\in G\setminus H
, there is some subgroup
K_{g}\in G
such that
H\leq K_{g}
and
g\not\in K_{g}
. In particular, separability of the trivial subgroup is precisely residual finiteness. We say that
G
is
subgroup separable
if all its finitely generated subgroups are separable.

Theorem 1.7.4

(Moldavanskii & Timofeeva, 1987
[
MT87
]
)
.

All one-relator groups with non-trivial centre are subgroup separable. In particular, the subgroup membership problem is decidable in one-relator groups with non-trivial centre.

We conclude our discussion on the centre by mentioning the centre of
subgroups
of one-relator groups. The subgroups of one-relator groups can be complicated in general, but their centres can be completely understood, analogously to Murasugi’s theorem.

Theorem 1.7.5

(Mahimovski, 1971
[
Mah71
]
/ Karrass, Pietrowski & Solitar, 1974
[
KPS74
]
)
.

Let
G
be a torsion-free one-relator group and
H<G
be a subgroup with non-trivial centre
1\nleq Z(H)\nleq H
. Then
Z(H)
is infinite cyclic.

Related to this theorem is a question posed by Baumslag
[
Bau71b
]
in 1974: if
G
is a one-relator group with at least
3
generators, and
N\vartriangleleft G
is such that
G/N
is infinite, does
G/N^{\prime}
have trivial centre? Here
N^{\prime}
is the derived subgroup of
N
. Fischer
[
Fis76
]
gave a negative answer to this question shortly thereafter.

Just as for many other theorems about subgroups of one-relator groups, there is an important (co)homological point of view. Swan
[
Swa69
, p. 156]
proved in 1969 that for any finitely generated non-abelian group
G
with
\cd_{\mathbb{Z}}(G)<\infty
, we have
\cd_{\mathbb{Z}}(Z(G))<\cd_{\mathbb{Z}}(Z(G))
. In particular, Swan’s theorem can be used to prove Theorem
1.7.5
, as the cohomological dimension of any torsion-free one-relator group is
\leq 2
(see §
1.4.2
), and any abelian group of cohomological dimension
\leq 1
is of course either
1
or
\mathbb{Z}
. In 1976, Bieri
[
Bie76
, Theorem C]
extended Swan’s theorem by dropping the hypothesis of finite generation. In the same article, Bieri
[
Bie76
, Theorem D]
also gave a homological generalization of many of the above results on the centre, by proving the following: let
G
be a group of type
\FP_{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})
with
\cd_{\mathbb{Z}}(G)=n
with
Z(G)
free abelian of rank
n-1
. Then
G
is a treed HNN-extension over a finite tree with all edges and all vertices
\cong Z(G)
. In particular, when
G
is a torsion-free one-relator group with non-trivial centre, we obtain the following consequences:

(1)

every finitely generated subgroup of
G
is finitely presented and of type
\FP_{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})
,

(2)

[G,G]\cap Z(G)=1
,

(3)

[G,G]
is free.

We refer the reader to
[
Bie76
, Corollary 6.6]
for more details. In this line there are also earlier results by Baumslag & Gruenberg
[
BG67
]
, e.g. that for any two-generated subgroup
H\leq G
of a one-relator group, if
Z(H)
is cyclic and
H/Z(H)
is periodic, then
H
is itself cyclic.

For a final note on one-relator groups with non-trivial centre, we mention that their (outer) automorphism groups are also known. As a classical example, recall that Schreier (see §
1.2.2
) determined the automorphism group of
G_{m,n}=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid a^{m}b^{n}=1\rangle
, and it is easy to see that the corresponding group is virtually free (note that Pettet
[
Pet95
]
has also proved a structural result for all finitely generated groups with virtually free automorphism group). Extending this result, Gilbert, Howie, Metaftsis & Raptis
[
GHMR00
]
proved that the outer automorphism group of a one-relator group
G
with non-trivial centre falls into two cases: first, if
\rk_{\mathbb{Z}}G^{\operatorname{ab}}=1
, then either
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
, or else
G\cong\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid a^{m}=b^{m}\rangle
for some
m\geq 2
, and in the latter case
\Out(G)\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{2}
, as proved by Schreier in 1924, see §
1.2.2
. If instead
\rk_{\mathbb{Z}}G^{\operatorname{ab}}=2
, then either
G\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, or
\Out(G)\cong D_{\infty}
, or
\Out(G)\cong D_{\infty}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
.

1.7.3.
Commutativity in one-relator groups

As we mentioned earlier (in §
1.5.4
), in his 1964 survey article Baumslag asked which abelian groups arise as subgroups of one-relator groups. In particular, he
[
Bau64
, p. 391]
conjectured that the additive group of rationals is not a subgroup of any one-relator group. He also says “one might however ask just which abelian groups are subgroups of a group with one defining relator”. We have already seen that Moldavanskii
[
Mol67
]
gave a nearly complete answer to this question in 1967, see §
1.5.4
, but that he left unresolved the question of whether
(\mathbb{Q},+)
embeds into any one-relator group. This would be done by B. B. Newman in 1968, again in his thesis, who gave an independent proof of Moldavanskii’s result (Theorem
1.5.9
), and clarified the situation of the locally cyclic subgroups as follows:

Theorem 1.7.6

(B. B. Newman, 1968
[
New68b
, Corollary 1.2.4]
)
.

Let
G
be a torsion-free one-relator group. Then no non-trivial element of
G
has more than finitely many prime divisors. Moreover, a non-trivial element is not divisible by more than finitely many powers of a prime
p
if
p
is greater than the length of the relator.

As an immediate consequence of this theorem, we see that:

Corollary 1.7.7

(B. B. Newman, 1968
[
New68b
, Corollary 1.2.4]
)
.

The additive group of rationals is not a subgroup of any one-relator group.

This completed the classification of the abelian subgroups of one-relator groups. New proofs of these results, particularly characterizing the abelian subgroups of amalgamated free products, were given by Wright
[
Wri79
]
using Bass–Serre theory, and Nagata
[
Nag80
]
.

We now turn to
solvable
subgroups of one-relator groups. This is a more difficult question, but again it becomes easier to answer in the case of one-relator groups with torsion. Unlike the proof of the Spelling Theorem, the proof of this result (which was also announced in
[
New68a
]
) was published in the literature, and the proof of Theorem
1.7.8
is the subject of the 1973 article
[
New73
]
.

Theorem 1.7.8

(B. B. Newman, 1968
[
New68b
, Corollary 2.3.3]
)
.

The solvable subgroups of a one-relator group with torsion are cyclic.

Karrass & Solitar
[
KS69
]
determined which one-relator groups contain abelian normal subgroups. In particular, the only one-relator group, other than
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
itself, containing a normal subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is
\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid abab^{-1}=1\rangle
, the fundamental group of the Klein bottle. Another student of Greendlinger in Tula was A. A. Chebotar, who proved several remarkable theorems in the 1970s, extending Moldavanskii’s work. First, in 1971
[
Che71
]
, he extended Theorem
1.5.10
to all subgroups of one-relator groups, proving that if
H\leq\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
is non-abelian and such that
H
does not contain a free subgroup of rank
2
, then either
H\cong C_{2}\times C_{2}
or
G\cong\BS(1,k)
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Thus, we also get a full classification of the solvable subgroups of one-relator groups, and a “Tits alternative” for one-relator groups. Next, in 1975 he extended Karrass & Solitar’s aforementioned result on abelian normal subgroups to all subgroups of one-relator groups
[
Che75
]
. But perhaps the most important result proved by Chebotar was the following, which was also given a proof by Bagherzadeh around the same time:

Theorem 1.7.9

(Bagherzadeh, 1976
[
Bag76b
]
/ Chebotar, 1978
[
Che78
]
)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
be a one-relator group, and let
H_{1}\times H_{2}\leq G
be a non-trivial direct product. Then one of
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
, and the other is locally cyclic.

In particular, the direct product
F_{2}\times F_{2}
of two non-abelian free groups does not embed into any one-relator group. This direct product has a range of bad properties, e.g. undecidable membership problem
[
Mik58
]
and incoherence
[
Sta77
]
. Today, we know that one-relator groups are coherent, giving another (significantly more difficult) proof of Chebotar’s theorem. Finally, Chebotar notes that his results give an answer to Question 6 from Baumslag’s 1974 survey
[
Bau71b
]
, published only four years earlier. This showcases the efficiency of transferring results and open problems across the Iron Curtain even during this time, and particularly the remarkable work by Greendlinger at making Tula a leading centre of combinatorial group theory.

1.7.4.
Coherence and subgroup separability

The first property we will consider is
coherence
. Recall that a group is said to be coherent if all of its finitely generated subgroups are finitely presented. As mentioned at the beginning of §
1.6
, Baumslag conjectured that all one-relator groups are coherent, which we today know is the case. The coherence of all one-relator groups was also said to be “conceivable” by Bieri
[
Bie76
, p. 36]
. Some classical results yield coherence in small classes of one-relator groups: for example, it follows from Theorem
1.2.3
that surface groups
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
are coherent for all
g\geq 1
. Perhaps the first large contribution to the coherence of one-relator groups came in 1976. As we have seen in §
1.7.2
, one-relator groups
with non-trivial centre
are in general significantly better behaved than their centreless counterparts, and general theorems easier to come by. This is true also for coherence:

Theorem 1.7.10

(Bieri, 1976
[
Bie76
, Corollary 6.6]
)
.

One-relator groups with non-trivial centre are coherent.

Beyond this result, however, few truly general results were known regarding coherence. More was known on the structure of particular finitely presented subgroups of one-relator groups. For example, one can ask what the finitely presented
normal
subgroups of one-relator groups are. In this line, we have the following theorem, which can be obtained by combining two closely related results due to Bieri and Karrass & Solitar.

Theorem 1.7.11

(Bieri
[
Bie76
, Theorem B]
/ Karrass & Solitar
[
KS78
]
)
.

Let
G
be a one-relator group and let
H\trianglelefteq G
be a non-trivial finitely presented normal subgroup. If
H
has infinite index in
G
, then
H
is free, and
G
is torsion-free and has two generators. Furthermore,
G
is an infinite cyclic or infinite dihedral extension of a finitely generated free group.

In fact, Bieri proved a stronger statement: the above theorem holds for all finitely generated groups of cohomological dimension
\leq 2
, which includes all torsion-free one-relator groups (see §
1.4.2
). Notably, the proof of both the results of Bieri and Karrass & Solitar use cohomological methods, including the rational Euler characteristic, cf.
[
Wal61
,
Chi76
]
.

Similarly, one can also ask about when finitely generated normal subgroups have infinite index in one-relator groups. Recall that Schreier (§
1.2.3
) proved that any finitely generated normal subgroup of a free group has finite index. The analogous theorem fails already for some very basic one-relator groups: for example, in the torus knot group
G=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid a^{2}=b^{3}\rangle
the commutator subgroup is finitely generated and free (cf. Theorem
1.5.11
), but and hence has infinite index in
G
as the defining word
a^{2}b^{-3}
is not primitive (see Theorem
1.4.2
). Note that
G\cong\mathbb{Z}\ast_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}
, where the amalgamated subgroup of course has finite index in both factors; this turns out to one source of obstructions to extending Schreier’s result. Karrass & Solitar
[
KS73
]
proved a number of results on amalgams with no such obstructions, and we state one of the relevant corollaries of their results:

Theorem 1.7.12

(Karrass & Solitar
[
KPS73
, p. 23]
)
.

Let
G=A\ast_{U}B
be an amalgamated free product where
U
has infinite index in
A
, the subgroups of
U
are all finitely generated, and every finitely generated normal subgroup of
A
has finite index in
A
. Then every finitely generated normal subgroup of
G
has finite index in
G
.

Thus, for example, the group
G^{\prime}=\textnormal{}\langle a,b,c\mid a^{2}=b^{3}\rangle

does
have the property that every finitely generated normal subgroup of
G^{\prime}
has finite index in
G^{\prime}
.

Other than coherence, another finiteness property for groups in relation to their finitely generated subgroups is
subgroup separability
, which we have mentioned above (see Theorem
1.7.4
). Since not every one-relator group is residually finite, neither is every one-relator group subgroup separable. However, in general subgroup separability is a much stronger condition even for one-relator groups. Indeed, Burns, Karrass & Solitar
[
BKS87
]
proved that the one-relator group defined by

(1.30)

\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid[a,bab^{-1}]=1\rangle\cong\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid[ab,ba]=1\rangle

is not subgroup separable. However, the group (
1.30
) is residually finite, indeed (f.g. free)-by-cyclic, by Theorem
1.7.15
.

Finally, we mention the
Howson
property. We say that a group
G
has the Howson property if the intersection of any two finitely generated subgroups of
G
is again finitely generated. Howson
[
How54
]
proved that free groups have the Howson property (see
[
Imr77
]
for a simple proof), and it is known that free products of Howson groups are Howson
[
Bau66
]
, also reproved by Bezverkhnii & Rollov
[
BR74
]
; cf. also
[
Bur72
,
Kap97
]
. It is thus natural to ask to what extent the Howson property holds in one-relator groups. Greenberg
[
Gre60a
, Theorem 2]
proved in 1960 that all surface groups are Howson. Moldavanskii
[
Mol68
]
proved that
\BS(1,n)
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
have the Howson property. However, Moldavanskii
[
Mol68
]
also proved that any one-relator group with a non-trivial centre fails to have the Howson property. Kapovich
[
Kap99
]
gave an example of a hyperbolic torsion-free one-relator group which is not Howson. Kapovich
[
Kap96
]
also proved that if
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r^{n}=1\rangle
is a finitely generated one-relator group with torsion such that
n\geq 5
, then the intersection of any two
2
-generated subgroups of
G
is again finitely generated.

1.7.5.
Quotients and residual properties of one-relator groups

We discuss some results on quotients of one-relator groups, beginning with their finite quotients and some results on residual solvability and nilpotency. Of course, Baumslag–Solitar groups (§
1.5.3
) give examples of non-residually finite one-relator groups, but many other examples of residually finite one-relator groups are known.

In 1963, G. Baumslag
[
Bau63b
]
proved several fundamental results regarding residual finiteness and nilpotent groups: for example, he proved that the free product of two residually finite groups, amalgamated in a finite subgroup, is always residually finite (see also
[
Tre73
]
for a simple proof of this fact). One of his results concerns one-relator groups directly, and we have already seen it in §
1.5.2
: the free product of two free groups, amalgamated in a cyclic subgroup, is residually finite
[
Bau63b
, Theorem 7]
(cf. also
[
Eva73
,
Weh73
,
Shi88
]
in this line). Such amalgams, called
cyclically pinched one-relator groups
, provide a rich source of residually finite one-relator groups (including the surface group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})
, and hence also all
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
for
g>1
, see §
1.2.2
).

Theorem 1.7.13

(Baumslag, 1963
[
Bau63b
]
)
.

Cyclically pinched one-relator groups are residually finite.

Strengthening this, in 1981 Allenby
[
All81
]
proved that cyclically pinched one-relator groups are
potent
, a stronger form of residual finiteness (for every non-trivial element
g
and every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a homomorphism onto a finite group which maps
g
onto an element of order exactly
n
).

We turn to
positive
one-relator groups and related classes of groups. A one-relator group
G
is said to be positive if it admits some presentation
\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
where
r\in F_{A}
contains no inverse letters. Note that this condition has some subtlety: for example
G=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid bab^{-1}a=1\rangle
is, in spite of its disguise, a positive one-relator group, since
G\cong\textnormal{}\langle x,y\mid x^{2}y^{2}=1\rangle
. Indeed, the isomorphism problem of one-relator groups with respect to the class of positive one-relator groups is still an open problem, i.e. it is unknown whether we can decide if a given one-relator group is positive or not. Note that e.g. the group
\mathbb{Z}^{2}=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid[a,b]=1\rangle
is not positive, by Proposition
1.3.4
. Positive one-relator groups enjoy some particular properties. The first, and perhaps most striking, theorem in this line is the following:

Theorem 1.7.14

(Baumslag, 1971
[
Bau
]
)
.

Positive one-relator groups are residually solvable.

In particular, every perfect subgroup of a positive one-relator group is trivial. It follows essentially from the local indicability of one-relator groups (see below) that all
finitely generated
perfect subgroups of one-relator groups are trivial; if one drops the assumption of finite generation, then the answer is negative, as evidenced by the Baumslag–Gersten group
\operatorname{BG}(1,2)
defined in (
1.31
).

When the defining relator is positive
and
a proper power, i.e. we are considering a positive one-relator group with torsion
G
, then
G
is residually finite by Theorem
1.6.9
in §
1.6.2
. Of course, solvable groups are not in general residually finite, indeed they can have undecidable word problem, by Kharlampovich
[
Kha81
]
. Furthermore, positive one-relator groups are not residually finite in general. Indeed, any Baumslag–Solitar group
\BS(m,-n)
with
m,n\geq 0
admits a positive presentation, as noticed by Higman
[
Bau
, p. 166]
and, independently, Perrin & Schupp
[
PS84
]
.

One-relator groups are, in general, not residually solvable, as e.g. the Baumslag–Gersten group
G=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid[a,bab^{-1}]=a\rangle
has
a
lie in every term of the derived series. It is not hard to see that
G\cong\textnormal{}\langle x,y\mid x^{2}yxy=yx\rangle
, and so in general one-relator groups with a defining relation of the form
uv^{-1}=1
for two positive words is not residually solvable. However, with some additional assumptions, more can be said also in this case. This was the subject of a 1983 article by Baumslag, some of the results of which we now present. First, recall that for a word
w\in F_{A}
and a letter
a\in A
, we let
\sigma_{a}(w)
be the
exponent sum
of
a
in
w
.

Theorem 1.7.15

(Baumslag, 1983
[
Bau83
]
)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid u=v\rangle
where
u,v\in F_{A}
are positive words, and where
\sigma_{a}(u)=\sigma_{a}(v)
for all
a\in A
. Then
G
is free-by-cyclic.

Note that one particular case of groups in the class from Theorem
1.7.15
is the case of a commutator
[u,v]=1
, where
u,v\in F_{A}
are positive words. Not every one-relator group of the form
[u,v]=1
with general
u,v\in F_{A}
is free-by-cyclic (see
[
Bau83
, §4.3]
), but it is possible to add further conditions on
u
and
v
, other than positivity, to ensure residual finiteness.

Theorem 1.7.16

(Baumslag, 1983
[
Bau83
]
)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\cup B\mid[u,v]=1\rangle
where
A\cap B=\varnothing
and
u\in F_{A},v\in F_{B}
. Then some term in the lower central series of
G
is free; and
G
is residually finite.

Thus, for example, the group
\textnormal{}\langle a,b,c\mid[a^{m},cb^{n}c^{-1}]=1\rangle
is residually finite for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
. For extensions of Theorem
1.7.16
to a slightly more general setting, see Loginova
[
Log99
]
.

On the other hand, some one-relator groups have residual finiteness fail in a rather dramatic fashion. By basic linear algebra, every infinite one-relator group obviously surjects
\mathbb{Z}
, and hence also has all cyclic groups as a quotient. Baumslag
[
Bau69b
]
proved, remarkably, that there also exist non-cyclic one-relator groups all of whose finite quotients are cyclic. This can be seen as a very strong failure of residual finiteness. The group in question was generalized to a family of groups known as the
Baumslag–Gersten groups

(1.31)

\operatorname{BG}(m,n)=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid bab^{-1}a^{m}ba^{-1}b^{-1}=a^{n}\rangle

and the original example in
[
Bau69b
]
is the case of
(m,n)=(1,2)
. The groups have seen some study. Gersten
[
Ger92
]
proved that the Dehn function of
\operatorname{BG}(1,2)
grows faster than any finite tower of exponentials, later sharpened by Platonov
[
Pla04
]
. Brunner
[
Bru80
]
investigated the automorphism group of
\operatorname{BG}(m,n)
, and Kavutskii & Moldavanskii
[
KM88
]
proved that
\operatorname{BG}(m,n)
is residually finite if and only if
|m|=|n|
, and it is not hard to see that all of the finite quotients of
\operatorname{BG}(m,n)
are cyclic if and only if
m=n\pm 1
.

Recall that finitely generated nilpotent groups are residually finite (§
1.4.3
). In 1964, A. Steinberg
[
Ste63
,
Ste64
]
studied free nilpotent quotients of one-relator groups. Specifically, let
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid R=1\rangle
where
R\in[F_{A},F_{A}]
, i.e. the exponent sum of every generator in
R
is zero. Steinberg then proved that there exists an algorithm for deciding whether or not
G
has a rank
2
free nilpotent quotient. Shapiro & Sonn
[
SS74b
]
reproved his theorem in a cohomological setting and extended it to a somewhat broader class of one-relator groups, see also
[
Son74
]
. One of the corollaries of Steinberg’s results in
[
Ste64
]
is that the one-relator group
\textnormal{}\langle a,b,c\mid a^{2}b^{2}c^{3}=1\rangle
has free nilpotent quotient groups of rank
2
and all classes; however, it does not surject the free group of rank
2
(this follows e.g. by a theorem of Schützenberger
[
Sch59
]
). We shall see more of such groups in §
1.7.6
. Finally, we remark that although automorphism groups of one-relator groups remain in general somewhat mysterious, a very elegant and short argument due to Baumslag
[
Bau63a
]
shows that the automorphism group of any finitely generated residually finite group is again residually finite. Thus, automorphism groups of residually finite one-relator groups provide a rich source of residually finite groups.

Free groups are one-relator groups, and studying the residual properties of free groups often leads to interesting questions related to one-relator groups more general. First, note that by a simple argument,
F_{n}
is residually
F_{2}
for all
n\geq 2
, see
[
Pel66
]
. Peluso
[
Pel66
]
also proved that the free group of rank
n\geq 2
is residually
\PSL(2,p)
for any
p>3
. In relation to this result, we mention briefly that an old conjecture by Magnus was that for any infinite family of finite non-abelian simple groups
\mathcal{G}
, the free group
F_{2}
is residually
\mathcal{G}
. This led to many significant articles, e.g.
[
KM68
,
GL70
,
Pos70
,
Pri72
,
Wie77
,
Lub86
,
Wil91
]
, leading finally to a full resolution
[
Wei92b
,
Wei92a
,
Wei93
]
, cf. also
[
Val93
]
. Magnus
[
Mag69
, p. 309]
raises the point that non-abelian free groups are residually
\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid a^{n}\rangle
for
n>1
, as proved by
[
KM68
]
, and also “possibly residually
S
for a large number of other one-relator groups
S
”. This line of investigation was taken up by Pride
[
Pri72
]
, and especially in
[
Pri74
, Chapter 3]
, where he investigates when free groups are residually one-relator groups with torsion, making heavy use of the B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem. In particular, he proves:

Theorem 1.7.17

(Pride, 1974
[
Pri74
, §3.2]
)
.

Let
G=\textnormal{}\langle x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{k}\mid r^{n}=1\rangle
with
n>1
be an arbitrary one-relator group with torsion.

(1)

If
k>3
, then
F_{k}
is residually
G
.

(2)

If
k=2
, and
r\neq[x_{1},x_{2}]
, then
F_{2}
is residually
G
.

(3)

If
k=2
, and
r=[x_{1},x_{2}]
, then
F_{2}
is not residually
G
.

For more on the class of groups
S_{n}=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid[a,b]^{n}=1\rangle
with
n>1
, see §
1.6.3
.

We now turn to more general quotients of one-relator groups. There is in general no shortage of quotients of one-relator groups. Recall that a countable group
G
is
SQ-universal
if every countable group embeds in some quotient of
G
. In 1973, P. Neumann
[
Neu73
]
conjectured that any one-relator group is either solvable (and hence either
\BS(1,n)
or cyclic) or else is SQ-universal, which remains an open problem. In 1974, Sacerdote & Schupp
[
SS74a
]
proved that every one-relator group with at least three generators is SQ-universal. B. Baumslag & Pride
[
BP78
]
generalized this to prove that if
G
is a group with
n
generators and
r
relators, such that
n-r\geq 2
, then
G
has a finite index subgroup which surjects
F_{2}
. Finally, Stöhr proved the following general result in 1983:

Theorem 1.7.18

(Stöhr, 1983
[
Stö83
]
)
.

Every one-relator group with torsion is SQ-universal.

In looking at quotients of one-relator groups, it is natural to ask the following question: given two one-relator groups which are homomorphic images of one another, are they necessarily isomorphic? This question was first posed by Moldavanskii in 1969 (see Question 3.33 in the Kourovka Notebook
[
KM24
]
). The question was given a negative answer only 40 years later by Borshchev & Moldavanskii
[
BM06
]
, indicating the difficulty of even such simple-sounding problems.

Finally, we briefly mention a very important result on quotients of one-relator groups. A group is said to be
indicable
if it surjects
\mathbb{Z}
, and
locally indicable
if all of its finitely generated subgroups also surject
\mathbb{Z}
. The notion of an indicable group was introduced by Higman
[
Hig40
, p. 242]
, who proved that if
G
is locally indicable and
R
is an integral domain, then
RG
has no zero divisors and no non-trivial units
[
Hig40
, Theorem 12]
. Baumslag
[
Bau71b
]
conjectured that every torsion-free one-relator group is locally indicable. Brodskii
[
Bro80
]
announced in 1980 the theorem that this conjecture is true, i.e. all torsion-free one-relator groups are locally indicable. Brodskii
[
Bro84
]
only published the full proofs of these facts four years later; in the meantime, the theorem was also given topological proof by Howie
[
How82
]
. The subject of local indicability and its many fascinating links with many broader topics reaches far outside the scope of this survey, however.

1.7.6.
Lower central series and parafree groups

Let
G
be any group. Recall that the
lower central series

\gamma_{1}(G),\gamma_{2}(G),\dots
of
G
is defined inductively by
\gamma_{1}(G)=G
and
\gamma_{n+1}(G)=[G,\gamma_{n}(G)]
. This determines a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded Lie (
\mathbb{Z}-
)algebra
\gr(G):=\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}\gamma_{n}(G)/\gamma_{n+1}(G)
, where the factor
\gamma_{n}(G)/\gamma_{n+1}(G)
is termed the
n
th
lower central factor
of
G
. The sequence of lower central factors is sometimes called the
lower central sequence
of
G
. The lower central factors of any finitely presented group are finitely generated abelian groups, and are furthermore computable from the presentation (by a commutator collection process
[
Hal34
]
, see e.g.
[
AP90
,
Bau99
]
) and thus form a particularly convenient set of invariants of groups. This is in stark contrast to the derived series of a group: if
G^{\prime\prime}
denotes the second derived subgroup of
G
, then
G/G^{\prime\prime}
need not be finitely presented, even if
G
is a one-relator group. Indeed, as proved by Baumslag & Strebel
[
BS76a
]
: if
G_{m,n,r}=\textnormal{}\langle a,t\mid t^{r}a^{m}t^{-r}=a^{n}\rangle
, where
mnr\neq 0
and
\gcd(m,n)>1
or
|r|>1
and
mn=1
, then
H_{2}(G/G^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{Z})
is not finitely generated. On the other hand, if
\gcd(m,n)=1
and
|m|\neq 1\neq|n|
, then
G/G^{\prime\prime}
is not finitely presented, even though
H_{2}(G/G^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{Z})
is finitely generated.

The structure of the Lie algebra
\gr(G)
has seen some study in the case of one-relator groups. We mention some results briefly. First, if
G=F_{n}
, a free group on
n
generators, then
\gr(F_{n})
is the free Lie algebra on
n
generators (see
[
MKS66
, Chapter 5]
).
If
G=F/R
, then there is a canonical surjection
\gr(F)\twoheadrightarrow\gr(G)
. This kernel is quite non-trivial already for some very simple examples of one-relator groups. For example, it was determined when
G=\textnormal{}\langle x,y\mid x^{p}=1\rangle
with
p
a prime by Labute
[
Lab77
]
. In the case that the defining word is not a proper power, more can be said. Indeed, Labute
[
Lab70
]
found an explicit formula for the rank of
\gamma_{n}(G)/\gamma_{n+1}(G)
when
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
, as long as
r
is not an
n
th power modulo certain terms of the lower central series of the free group
F=F(A)
, and furthermore that in this case the associated Lie algebra
\gr(G)
is itself definable by a single defining relation (as a Lie algebra), cf. also
[
Lab67
]
. This, for example, gives a closed formula for the rank of
\gamma_{n}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2}))
, where
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2})
is the fundamental group of a genus
2
surface. If
g_{n}
denotes the
\mathbb{Z}
-rank of the free abelian group
\gamma_{n}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{2}))
, then

(1.32)

g_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{d\mid n}\mu(\frac{n}{d})\left(\sum_{0\leq i\leq\lfloor d/2\rfloor}(-1)^{i}\frac{d}{d-i}\binom{d-i}{i}4^{d-2i}\right)

where
\mu
denotes the Möbius function. One can use (
1.32
) to show that
g_{n}
grows as
(2+\sqrt{3})^{n}
, and more generally the exponential growth rate of the ranks in the case of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
with genus
g>1
is given by
g+\sqrt{g^{2}-1}
. It also follows from the aforementioned result by Labute that
\gr(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g}))
is a one-relator Lie algebra (in particular, it has decidable word problem
[
Shi62
]
). By contrast, it is not hard to show that
\gr(\BS(1,3))
is not finitely presented as a Lie algebra.

We now turn to a very important aspect of the lower central series. This goes back to the 1939 theorem by Magnus (see Theorem
1.4.3
(3)) that any
n
-generator group with the same sequence of lower central factors as the
n
-generator free group is itself free. This prompted H. Neumann to ask whether any residually nilpotent group with the same sequence of lower central factors as a free group is necessarily free. This leads to the following definition. A group
G
is said to be
parafree
if it is residually nilpotent and has the same sequence of lower central factors as some free group. Thus H. Neumann’s conjecture simply asked: do non-free parafree groups exist? G. Baumslag
[
Bau64
,
Bau65b
,
Bau67b
,
Bau69a
]
would approach this question in a series of papers (cf. also
[
BS64
]
), and prove:

Theorem 1.7.19

(G. Baumslag, 1964
[
Bau64
]
)
.

Non-free parafree groups exist.

One such example given in
[
Bau64
]
, with proof going via a careful study of amalgamated free products to be found in
[
Bau65b
]
, is
\textnormal{}\langle a,b,c\mid a^{2}b^{3}c^{5}=1\rangle
. We note the reader that the terminology used in
[
Bau64
]
is
pseudofree
, rather than
parafree
. Other examples of non-free parafree groups given in 1969 by Baumslag
[
Bau69a
]
are the groups
\textnormal{}\langle a,b,c\mid a=[c^{i},a][c^{j},b]\rangle
which are parafree, but not free, for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
ij\neq 0
. Of course, the free group with which they share their sequence of lower central factors is the free group of rank
2
. This family belongs to the broader class of parafree-by-cyclic groups, studied by Wong
[
Won78
,
Won80
]
in his PhD thesis, supervised by G. Baumslag. Parafree groups share a great deal of properties with free groups. Recall (§
1.7.5
) that any non-cyclic free group is residually free of rank two. Similarly, one can show that any non-cyclic parafree group is also residually parafree of rank two
[
Bau69a
, Theorem 5.2]
. We also have the following curious result, showing that the local behaviour of parafree groups is remarkably like that of free groups:

Theorem 1.7.20

(G. Baumslag
[
Bau69a
, Theorem 4.2]
)
.

Any two-generator subgroup of a parafree group is free.

Baumslag & Stammbach
[
BS76b
]
have also given an example of a non-free parafree group all of whose countable subgroups are free. We remark that for a residually nilpotent group
G
, we have that
G
is a parafree group if and only if
\gr(G)
is a free Lie algebra. Using the Nielsen–Schreier theorem for free Lie algebras due to Witt
[
Wit37
]
, it is possible to give another proof of Theorem
1.7.20
. This final part of the section is only intended to serve as a small sampler of parafree groups. Very much more can be written, particularly on parafree one-relator groups. Many of the techniques and results involved in the study of such groups go far beyond the classical, and is better left for a separate survey.

We conclude this section by noting that one can go beyond residual nilpotence in the following way. Let
G
be any group. Then its transfinite lower central series is defined as in the finite case by
\gamma_{1}(G)=G,\gamma_{\alpha+1}=[G,\gamma_{\alpha}(G)]
, and
\gamma_{\lambda}(G)=\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}\gamma_{\alpha}(G)
for limit ordinals
\lambda
. Then the nilpotency class of
G
is the minimal
\alpha
such that
\gamma_{1+\alpha}(G)=1
. Thus, for example, a residually nilpotent group has nilpotency class
\leq\omega
. Baumslag
[
Bau71b
]
asked whether every one-relator group has nilpotency class
<\omega^{2}
. Indeed it was open for some time whether there were finitely presented groups of nilpotency class
>\omega
, until Levine
[
Lev91
]
gave an example of such groups in 1991. Baumslag’s question was only answered in 2016, when Mikhailov
[
Mik16
]
proved that
\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid ab^{-1}a^{3}=b^{-2}ab\rangle
has nilpotency class
\omega^{2}
.

1.8.
More on decision problems

Even though the word problem was solved for one-relator groups almost a century ago, as presented in §
1.3.3
, remarkably both the conjugacy problem and isomorphism problem – the other two of Dehn’s three fundamental problems – remain open for one-relator groups. In this section, we will discuss some partial progress made on these problems, and the general approaches to the problems. Finally, we will discuss the word problem in some greater depth, including some open problems, and the possibility of extending the solution of the word problem to all
two
-relator groups.

1.8.1.
The conjugacy problem

We begin with a general remark regarding the difficulty of the conjugacy problem. Decidability of the conjugacy problem implies decidability of the word problem, and hence the conjugacy problem is for general finitely presented groups undecidable. However, P. S. Novikov
[
Nov54
]
proved in 1955 the existence of finitely presented groups
A_{p_{1},p_{2}}
with undecidable conjugacy problem, the construction of which is significantly simpler than his group with undecidable word problem (see §
1.4.4
). He left the question of the decidability of the word problem in
A_{p_{1},p_{2}}
open. In 1960, A. A. Fridman (a student of Novikov) proved that the groups
A_{p_{1},p_{2}}
have decidable word problem, thus showing that the conjugacy problem is in general strictly harder than the word problem. We note that in 1967, Bokut
[
Bok67
]
simplified both Novikov’s argument and Fridman’s argument, and Collins
[
Col69c
]
in 1969, proved that the conjugacy problem can be arbitrarily difficult – i.e. of any recursively enumerable degree of unsolvability – even in groups with decidable word problem.

More specifically to one-relator groups, recall that Magnus’ solution to the word problem (§
1.3.3
) in all one-relator groups depends, as clarified by Moldavanskii (§
1.5.4
), in large part on properties of HNN-extensions and amalgamated free products. Summarizing this argument, heavy appeal is made to the fact that if the word problem is decidable in two groups
G_{1},G_{2}
, and
H\leq G_{1},G_{2}
is a common subgroup in which membership is decidable for both
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, then the amalgamated free product
G_{1}\ast_{H}G_{2}
has decidable word problem; the analogous statement also holds for HNN-extensions,
mutatis mutandis
. Thus the word problem (more specifically membership in Magnus subgroups) can be passed down from a one-relator group to the next one-relator group in the Magnus–Moldavanskii hierarchy, possibly embedding the group into a larger one-relator group along the way, until a free group is reached, and the word problem is decidable in all one-relator groups by induction. In seeking to extend this line of argument to the conjugacy problem, we are faced with two problems:

(1)

Decidability of the conjugacy problem is a much more delicate property to preserve when taking HNN-extensions or amalgamated free products; and

(2)

Decidability of the conjugacy problem is not, in general, preserved by taking subgroups, even of finite index.

We elaborate on these two points. For the first part, there is the following general undecidability result which,
a priori
, shows that additional ingredients must be added to the Magnus–Moldavanskii hierarchy to adapt it to the conjugacy problem:

Theorem 1.8.1

(Miller, 1971
[
Mil71
, Theorem III.10]
)
.

There exists a finitely presented HNN-extension of a free group with undecidable conjugacy problem. There exists an amalgam
F_{n}\ast_{F_{k}}F_{m}
of two finitely generated free groups over a finitely generated free subgroup with undecidable conjugacy problem.

Note that the groups in Theorem
1.8.1
all have decidable word problem, since the subgroup membership problem is decidable in free groups. We refer the reader to
[
BMR07a
,
BMR07b
]
for more in-depth investigations into the complexity of the conjugacy problem in such amalgams of free groups. We also note that the conjugacy problem in HNN-extensions, and thus in some one-relator groups, has a very combinatorial flavour, and can thus often be phrased in terms of semigroup-theoretic problems. For example, for certain HNN-extensions the conjugacy problem reduces to reachability problems of vector addition systems, see
[
Ans76c
,
Ans76b
,
Ans78
]
, and sometimes further reduced to the word problem for commutative semigroups; see
[
Ans76a
]
. Similarly, Horadam & Farr
[
HF94
]
have reduced the conjugacy problem in some HNN-extensions to membership problems in inverse semigroups.

For the second part, i.e. closure under taking subgroups, it was probably first recognized by Collins
[
Col69a
]
that there exists a finitely presented group
G
with a finitely presented subgroup
H\leq G
such that
G
has decidable conjugacy problem, but
H
has undecidable conjugacy problem. In 1971, C. F. Miller
[
Mil71
]
proved that there exist subgroups of
F_{2}\times F_{2}
with undecidable conjugacy problem, even though the conjugacy problem in
F_{2}\times F_{2}
is almost trivial. His example is based on the undecidability of the membership problem in
F_{2}\times F_{2}
due to K. A. Mikhailova
[
Mik58
]
(a PhD student of P. S. Novikov). In 1977, Collins & Miller
[
CM77
]
proved that in general neither passing to index
2
subgroups nor index
2
extensions preserve decidability of the conjugacy problem. In this line, see also
[
GK75
]
. A natural question that arises in this context is the following:

Problem 1.8.2
.

Do subgroups of one-relator groups have decidable conjugacy problem?

We emphasize that it is possible that Problem
1.8.2
has a negative answer, even if all one-relator groups have decidable conjugacy problem.

In spite of all the above negative results, in the setting of one-relator groups there is nevertheless a case to be made for optimism. Collins
[
Col69d
]
proved that the conjugacy problem can be undecidable in 11-relator groups, but the problem remains open for
k
-relator groups for all
1\leq k\leq 10
. Furthermore, as mentioned in §
1.2
, Dehn gave already in 1911 a solution for the conjugacy problem in the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
of surface groups of genus
g>1
. In 1966, Lipschutz
[
Lip66
]
generalized this to all cyclically pinched one-relator groups, i.e. amalgamated free products
F_{n}\ast_{\mathbb{Z}}F_{m}
of free groups amalgamating a cyclic subgroup (of which surface groups are central examples), to give the following theorem:

Theorem 1.8.3

(Lipschutz, 1966
[
Lip66
]
)
.

Cyclically pinched one-relator groups have decidable conjugacy problem.

Lipschutz later extended these results to other cyclic amalgams of non-free groups, see
[
Lip69
,
Lip75
]
. For a different solution to the conjugacy problem in
\BS(m,n)
with
\gcd(m,n)=1
, see
[
AS74
]
. In 1980, Dyer
[
Dye80
]
extended Theorem
1.8.3
by proving that cyclically pinched one-relator groups are conjugacy separable. Larsen
[
Lar77
]
also proved that any one-relator group with non-trivial centre has decidable conjugacy problem, using the structure theorem for one-relator groups (see §
1.7.2
) with non-trivial centre. In this line, see also
[
Hur80
]
. Finally, we have already mentioned that B. B. Newman proved (see §
1.6.1
) that every one-relator group with torsion has decidable conjugacy problem.

We remark that it might be tempting to consider the well-behaved class of residually nilpotent one-relator groups as natural targets, since finitely generated nilpotent groups have decidable conjugacy problem; indeed they are even conjugacy separable, as proved in 1965 by Blackburn
[
Bla65
]
. However, unlike for the word problem, it turns out that residually nilpotent groups need not have decidable conjugacy problem, as proved in 1999 by G. Baumslag
[
Bau99
]
. Whether residually nilpotent one-relator groups have decidable conjugacy problem also seems to be an open problem.

1.8.2.
The isomorphism problem for torsion-free one-relator groups

The isomorphism problem is perhaps the most important as well as the most intractable problem in the theory of one-relator groups.

—G. Baumslag, 1971
[
Bau71b
, p. 75]

We have already outlined some of the results about the isomorphism problem for one-relator groups with torsion in §
1.6.3
. In particular, we know that in this case, the problem is decidable. By contrast, the isomorphism problem for torsion-free one-relator groups remains wide open. In this section, we will discuss some partial results, as well as some particularly difficult instances of the problem. Before beginning, we mention that by far the two largest classes of one-relator groups for which the isomorphism problem is decidable are one-relator groups with torsion (see §
1.6.3
) and one-relator groups with non-trivial centre (see §
1.7.2
). Beyond this, few truly general results are known: the quotation by G. Baumslag above remains as true today as it was 50 years ago.

Nevertheless, we can say some generalities. First, recall that two one-relator groups
\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
and
\textnormal{}\langle A\mid s=1\rangle
are said to be (
Nielsen
)
equivalent
if there is an automorphism of
F_{A}
mapping
r
to
s
. Of course, any two Nielsen equivalent one-relator groups are isomorphic, but whether the converse holds was for some time an open problem (Conjecture
1.3.5
, sometimes called the Magnus Conjecture, cf. e.g.
[
MKS66
, p. 401]
). We have seen that Whitehead proved the conjecture true in 1936 in the case that the one-relator group is free (Theorem
1.4.2
. However, for general one-relator groups the conjecture was disproved in 1970 by Zieschang
[
Zie70
]
and independently in 1971 by McCool & Pietrowski
[
MP71
]
. The examples by McCool & Pietrowski come from torus knot groups. Let
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
with
|p|,|q|>1
, and let
T_{p,q}=\textnormal{}\langle x,y\mid x^{p}=y^{q}\rangle
. If
p=st+1
for some
s,t\in\mathbb{N}
with
t\neq 1
, then the authors proved that
T_{p,q}\cong\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid b=(a^{p}b^{-s})^{t}\rangle
, but that these presentations are not equivalent. Zieschang’s examples also come from
T_{p,q}
. Such counterexamples produce finite classes of isomorphic but non-equivalent one-relator groups. This was extended in 1976 by Brunner
[
Bru76
]
(cf. also
[
Bru80
]
), who proved that if for
r\geq 0
we define

(1.33)

G_{r}=\textnormal{}\langle x,y\mid y^{-1}x^{-2^{r}}yxy^{-1}x^{2^{r}}y=x^{2}\rangle,

then
G_{r}\cong G_{s}
for all
r,s\geq 0
, but no two such groups are Nielsen equivalent for
r\neq s
. This gives an even stronger failure of the Magnus Conjecture. Thus, it would be interesting to know whether it is decidable if a given one-relator group has infinitely many pairwise non-Nielsen-equivalent one-relator presentations. In principle this can be decidable even if the isomorphism problem is undecidable, and vice versa.

The isomorphism problem, and the above failure of Magnus’ Conjecture, is closely related to finding non-equivalent generating sets of one-relator groups, i.e. generating sets lying in different
T
-systems
. We discussed this subject in §
1.6.3
, and note that there is also some literature on this subject in the torsion-free case (beyond what is cited above). For example, Zieschang’s article
[
Zie70
]
is primarily concerned with extending Nielsen’s method of finding a basis for a subgroup in free groups to amalgamated free products. This is not possible in general, and Zieschang was able to point out several obstructions to this together with sufficient conditions on the amalgam for the method to work; such obstructions are also the source of the failure of Magnus’ Conjecture. Similar conditions were given in 1978 by Peczynski & Reiwer
[
PR78
]
for HNN-extensions. The Nielsen method in this broader context of amalgams and HNN-extensions was surveyed in
[
Ros79
]
, in which it is among other things used to give a proof of the Tits alternative for
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Nielsen’s method was extended to all Bass–Serre theory in
[
Wei02
]
; see also
[
FRS95
]
for a survey. We note that even Baumslag–Solitar groups
\BS(m,n)
(see §
1.5.3
) can also be elusive from the point of view of their generating sets. In particular, the group
\BS(2,3)=\textnormal{}\langle x,y\mid x^{-1}y^{2}x=y^{3}\rangle
requires more than one relation to define it in terms of the generators
x
and
y^{4}
(in this connection, see also
[
Pic71
]
). Dunwoody & Pietrowski
[
DP73
]
gave infinitely many non-equivalent generating sets of the trefoil knot group
\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid a^{2}=b^{3}\rangle
. These generating sets do not, however, have one-relator presentations.

One can also consider the isomorphism problem with respect to particular classes. For example, Whitehead’s Theorem (Theorem
1.4.2
) implies that it is decidable whether a one-relator group is free or not; thus, the isomorphism problem is decidable for one-relator groups relative to free groups. Similarly, one can prove using classical methods that the isomorphism problem is decidable for one-relator groups relative to the class of cyclically pinched one-relator groups, i.e. cyclic amalgams of free groups
F_{n}\ast_{\mathbb{Z}}F_{m}
. This was proved for
n=m=2
and some constraints on the amalgam by Rosenberger
[
Ros82
]
, and finally in general by Rosenberger
[
Ros94a
]
in 1994 (cf. also
[
Zie77
]
and
[
Col78a
]
). The strategy was to prove that such groups have only finitely many
T
-systems of generators (as in §
1.6.3
), yielding in particular the following result:

Theorem 1.8.4

(Rosenberger 1994,
[
Ros82
,
Ros94a
]
)
.

The isomorphism problem for one-relator groups is decidable relative to the class of cyclically pinched one-relator groups.

Another reason for the difficulty of the isomorphism problem in torsion-free one-relator groups comes from parafree groups (discussed in §
1.7.6
). In this direction, Chandler & Magnus
[
CM82
]
mention a particular family of parafree one-relator groups:

(1.34)

G_{i,j}=\textnormal{}\langle a,b,c\mid a=[c^{i},a][c^{j},b]\rangle\quad(i,j\in\mathbb{N})

as being particularly difficult. Finding necessary and sufficient conditions on the parameters
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
for pairs of groups in the family
G_{i,j}
to be isomorphic is particularly difficult, and for decades, only sporadic isomorphism results were proved for these groups (see e.g.
[
FRS95
,
FRS95
]
, and
[
BCH04
]
for an overview). Finally, the isomorphism problem for the family of groups
\{G_{i,j}\mid i,j\in\mathbb{N}\}
was solved in 2021 by Chen
[
Che21
]
, using character varieties: it turns out that the group
G_{i,j}
is uniquely determined by the pair
(i,j)
when
i,j>0
. In a similar vein, Meskin
[
Mes68
,
Mes69
]
in his PhD thesis studied the isomorphism problem for the one-relator groups

\textnormal{}\langle x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{t}\mid x_{1}^{m_{1}}x_{2}^{m_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{m_{t}}=1\rangle

where
t\geq 2
and
m_{1},\dots,m_{t}
are arbitrary integers. These groups are parafree when
t>3
and
\gcd(m_{1},\dots,m_{t})=1
. Already
within
this class of one-relator groups the isomorphism problem is difficult, but was eventually fully solved in 1975 by Meskin
[
Mes75
]
: the integers
m_{1},\dots,m_{t}
, up to permutation, determine the group uniquely.

In practice, there are many useful group invariants that can distinguish a given pair of torsion-free one-relator groups. The most basic invariant comes from the abelianization. The second homology group also provides some information: it is a consequence of Lyndon’s Identity Theorem (§
1.4.2
) that if
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r=1\rangle
, then
H_{2}(G,\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}
if and only if
r\in[F_{A},F_{A}]
. In this case, when the second homology group is non-trivial, there is much more to be said. This was explored by Horadam
[
Hor81a
,
Hor81b
]
in 1981, who provides a simple and straightforward test for non-isomorphism (later expanded on in
[
Hor82a
,
Hor82b
]
). For example, as in
[
Hor81b
, p. 197]
, if we let
A=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{4}\}
, and take

\displaystyle r_{1}

\displaystyle=[x_{3},x_{4}][x_{1},x_{2}][x_{1},x_{4}^{2}][x_{3},x_{2}][x_{2},x_{4}][x_{3},x_{4}]^{3},

\displaystyle r_{2}

\displaystyle=[x_{3},x_{4}][x_{1},x_{2}][x_{1},x_{4}^{2}][x_{3},x_{2}][x_{2},x_{4}][x_{3},x_{4}]^{2},

the only difference being the final exponent, and set
G_{i}=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid r_{i}=1\rangle
for
i=1,2
, then her methods prove that
G_{1}\not\cong G_{2}
. The technique can also be used to show that there exist infinitely many pairwise non-isomorphic one-relator groups of the form
\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid r=1\rangle
where
r\in[F_{2},F_{2}]
, which is not trivial to demonstrate otherwise. We note that her methods were also strengthened later by Gromadzki
[
Gro85
]
.

There is more to be said on using finite quotients, and whether the set of all finite quotients of a residually finite one-relator group uniquely determines the group up to isomorphism (
profinite rigidity
of one-relator groups). In this direction, we mention only
[
MS95
]
, who prove that the groups
\BS(1,k)
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
are profinitely rigid among all one-relator groups, i.e. if a residually finite one-relator group has the same finite images as
\BS(1,k)
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
G\cong\BS(1,k)
.

1.8.3.
More on the word problem

The first major result on one-relator groups was the
Freiheitssatz
, and one of the first application of this result was the decidability of the word problem in all one-relator groups. It is therefore remarkable that since these results, proved almost a century ago, few general results on the word problem in one-relator groups have appeared. For example, it remains an open problem whether it can be decided in polynomial time. There also exist examples of one-relator groups with very rapidly growing Dehn functions, but nevertheless having word problem decidable in polynomial time
[
MUW11
]
. In this section, we will discuss some open problems closely related to the word problem in one-relator groups.

One approach to better understand time complexity of the word problem in one-relator groups is via
finite complete rewriting systems
. We give brief definitions here, referring the reader to the monographs
[
Jan88
,
OZ91
]
for significantly more thorough treatments. Let
A
be a finite set,
A^{\ast}
the free monoid on
A
, and let
\mathcal{R}\subseteq A^{\ast}\times A^{\ast}
be a set of pairs of words, which we call
rules
. The set
\mathcal{R}
of rules induces a relation
\xrightarrow{}_{\mathcal{R}}
on
A^{\ast}
, where
w_{1}\xrightarrow{}_{\mathcal{R}}w_{2}
if and only if there exist words
x,y\in A^{\ast}
and some rule
r=(u,v)\in\mathcal{R}
such that
w_{1}\equiv xuy
and
w_{2}\equiv xvy
