. The reflexive and transitive closure of
\xrightarrow{}_{\mathcal{R}}
is denoted
\xrightarrow{}_{\mathcal{R}}^{\ast}
. If there is no infinite chain
u_{1}\xrightarrow{}_{\mathcal{R}}u_{2}\xrightarrow{}_{\mathcal{R}}\cdots
, then we say that
\mathcal{R}
is
terminating
. For
u\in A^{\ast}
, if there exists some
v\in A^{\ast}
such that
u\xrightarrow{}_{\mathcal{R}}v
, then we say that
u
is reducible, and otherwise
irreducible
. If
\mathcal{R}
is a terminating rewriting system in which for every word
w\in A^{\ast}
there exists a unique irreducible
w^{\prime}\in A^{\ast}
such that
w\xrightarrow{}^{\ast}_{\mathcal{R}}w^{\prime}
, then we say that
\mathcal{R}
is
complete
(cf.
[
Jan88
, Prop. 1.1.25]
). Every rewriting system
\mathcal{R}
induces an equivalence relation
\leftrightarrow^{\ast}_{\mathcal{R}}
, being the least equivalence relation containing
\mathcal{R}
. In fact, this is a congruence, and
A^{\ast}/\leftrightarrow^{\ast}_{\mathcal{R}}
, which we call the monoid
associated
to
\mathcal{R}
, is simply the monoid presented by
\textnormal{Mon}\langle A\mid R\rangle
. If a group
G
is isomorphic to the monoid associated to a finite complete rewriting system, then we say that
G

admits
a finite complete rewriting system. Of course, any group admitting a finite complete rewriting system has decidable word problem: simply rewrite any word to an irreducible one by successively applying the rules of the system.

Thus a finite complete rewriting system for a group is a particularly nice type of monoid presentation for the group, giving rise to a simple solution to the word problem. This leads to the following natural question:

Problem 1.8.5

(Otto & Kobayashi, 1997
[
OK97
, Q3]
)
.

Does every one-relator group admit a finite complete rewriting system?

If Problem
1.8.5
has a positive answer, then if the rules are concrete enough, it may be possible to gain upper bounds on the complexity of the word problem in any one-relator group. On the other hand, the property of admitting a finite complete rewriting system does not, in general, entail any upper bound (beyond recursiveness) on the complexity of the word problem
[
BO84
]
. Regarding a
negative
answer to Problem
1.8.5
, we first note that there exists a finitely presented monoid
\textnormal{}\langle A\mid R\rangle
which does not admit a finite complete rewriting system
\mathcal{R}\subseteq A^{\ast}\times A^{\ast}
, but which does admit a finite complete rewriting system over some other generating set
[
Ott84
]
. Thus Problem
1.8.5
is intricately linked with the problem of generating sets and alternate presentations of one-relator groups. Second, it seems difficult to even approach the question of how one might give a negative answer to Problem
1.8.5
. However, it turns out that finite complete rewriting systems are closely related to homological finiteness properties. Squier
[
Squ87
]
proved in 1987 that every monoid admitting a finite complete rewriting system has the homological finiteness property
\FP_{3}(\mathbb{Z})
, later strengthened to
\FP_{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})
by Kobayashi
[
Kob90
]
, cf. also
[
Ani86
,
Gro90
,
SOK94
]
. Since there are finitely presented groups with decidable word problem but which are not
\FP_{3}(\mathbb{Z})

[
Sta63
]
, there are finitely presented groups with decidable word problem but not admitting any finite complete rewriting system. Whether the same is true for one-relator groups is thus the crux of Problem
1.8.5
. Lyndon’s Identity Theorem (Theorem
1.4.4
) shows that all one-relator groups are
\FP_{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})
, showing that any negative answer to Problem
1.8.5
would have to involve some fundamentally new technique. We also remark here that there exist finitely presented groups of cohomological dimension
2
with undecidable word problem
[
CM99
]
, and thus not admitting a finite complete rewriting system.

Another angle on the word problem comes from embedding one-relator groups into finitely presented simple groups: any finitely presented simple group has decidable word problem by Kuznetsov’s elegant algorithm
[
Kuz58
]
.
41
41

41

If
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid R\rangle
is a finitely presented simple group and
w\in F_{A}
, then to decide if
w=1
in
G
, run the following two procedures in parallel: (1) enumerate all words equal to
1
in
G
; (2) enumerate the elements of
G_{w}=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid R,w=1\rangle
. Then (1) terminates if
w=1
, and (2) terminates if
w\neq 1
in
G
, since
G
being simple then implies that
G_{w}
is trivial.
Recall that the classical Boone–Higman Theorem
[
BH74
]
from 1974 states that a finitely generated group
G
has decidable word problem if and only if there exists a simple group
S
and a finitely presented group
K
such that
G\leq K\leq S
. The Boone–Higman Conjecture asks whether there is furthermore some such
S
and
K
exist with
K=S
, i.e. whether every finitely generated group with decidable word problem embeds in some finitely presented simple group. This conjecture remains open, but it has recently seen remarkable progress: Belk & Bleak
[
BB63
]
proved in 2023 that every finitely generated hyperbolic group embeds in some finitely presented simple group. Thus, for example, by combining this with the B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem (§
1.6.1
) we have that
every one-relator group with torsion embeds into a finitely presented simple group
. Whether this can be extended to all one-relator groups seems a next natural step for the Boone–Higman Conjecture.

Problem 1.8.6
.

Can every one-relator group be embedded into a finitely presented simple group?

Extensions of the Boone–Higman Theorem to other decision problems also exist, including for the order problem
[
BH75
]
and the conjugacy problem
[
Sac77
]
, giving rise to analogous questions for one-relator groups. Dual to the above problem is the following classical problem:

Problem 1.8.7

(Baumslag, 1964
[
Bau64
, p. 391]
)
.

Can a one-relator group contain a non-abelian simple subgroup?

Finally, we make a brief remark on membership problems, being in some ways a natural step following the word problem. The
subgroup membership problem
for a group
G=\textnormal{}\langle A\mid R\rangle
asks, on input a set of words
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\in F_{A}
and a word
w\in F_{A}
, whether or not
w
belongs to the subgroup of
G
generated by
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
. Thus the word problem is simply the membership problem for the trivial subgroup. Recall that Magnus (§
1.3.3
) proved that membership in Magnus subgroups of one-relator groups is decidable. However, for arbitrary finitely generated subgroups, deciding membership remains an unsolved problem in general:

Problem 1.8.8
.

Is the subgroup membership problem decidable in every one-relator group?

Any subgroup separable one-relator group has decidable subgroup membership problem. Thus a large class of one-relator groups with decidable subgroup membership problem is formed by the one-relator groups with non-trivial centre (see §
1.7.2
and particularly Theorem
1.7.4
). However, there are non-subgroup separable one-relator groups which nevertheless have decidable subgroup membership problem. e.g. the groups
H_{m,k}=\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid a^{-m}ba^{m}=b^{k}\rangle
, with
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, of Novikova
[
Nov98
]
. Problem
1.8.8
can also be extended to instances of the sub
monoid
membership problem, defined analogously. Here undecidability appears rather quickly, and many one-relator groups have undecidable submonoid membership problem, e.g.
\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid[ab,ba]=1\rangle
or
\textnormal{}\langle a,b\mid[a^{2},b^{2}]=1\rangle
. There are also positive one-relator groups with undecidable submonoid membership problem
[
FGNB24
]
. However, all currently known such examples arise from embedding the right-angled Artin group
A(P_{4})
, which has undecidable submonoid membership problem (see
[
LS08
]
). Such subgroups obviously cannot appear as subgroups of hyperbolic groups. Thus whether there is some hyperbolic one-relator group with undecidable submonoid membership problem, and whether there is a one-relator group with torsion with undecidable submonoid membership problem, both remain open problems, being Problems 20.68 & 20.69, respectively, in the Kourovka Notebook
[
KM24
]
.

1.8.4.
Two-relator groups

So my survey ends fittingly, as it began, with the work of Wilhelm Magnus.

—G. Baumslag
[
Bau86
, p. 55]

This survey has told the story of one defining relation. What question could now be more natural than: what about two-relator groups? Unfortunately, here much less is known. The question of extending the methods of one-relator groups to two relators dates back to the very foundations of the former subject, being present already in an appendix to Magnus’ 1930 article
[
Mag30
, §8]
. Very little has been produced in way of results for two-relator groups
qua
two-relator groups. Even the problem of classifying all finite two-relator groups is still a mystery. Certainly, any finite two-relator group must by basic linear algebra also be generated by two elements, and thus admit a balanced (i.e. deficiency zero) presentation. It follows that any finite two-relator group
G
must satisfy
H_{2}(G,\mathbb{Z})=0
. Beyond such generalities, the class of finite two-relator groups seems, at present, far too broad to understand in its entirety, and contains groups such as
\SL(2,5)\times\SL(2,25)
, as proved in
[
CRK+86
]
, and much computational work has been directed towards finding two-generator, two-relator presentations for similar groups, see e.g.
[
CRK+86
,
RW89
]
. Even the finiteness problem for two-relator groups, i.e. the isomorphism problem for two-relator groups with respect to the class of finite two-relator groups, is at present wide open.

The word problem, conjugacy problem, and isomorphism problem are all open for two-relator groups. The latter two are less surprising, since they also remain open for one-relator group, and currently known methods for encoding undecidable problems in groups tend to produce a relatively large number of relators. Indeed, as for the word problem, as discussed in §
1.4.4
the currently smallest known number of defining relators for a group with undecidable word problem is 12, as proved by Borisov
[
Bor69
]
. His proof (simplified by Collins
[
Col72
]
) uses the fact that the word problem in any
n
-relation monoid can be encoded into an
(n+9)
-relator group, and Matiyasevich
[
Mat67
]
gave an example of a three-relation monoid with undecidable word problem. It seems very plausible, but remains an open problem, that there is a two-relation monoid with undecidable word problem; and recent trends (see e.g.
[
Gra20b
,
FGNB24
]
) have indicated that there may even be a
one
-relation monoid with undecidable word problem (see
[
NB21b
]
for an introduction to this problem). Nevertheless, we see that Borisov’s method would only yield a 11 resp. 10-relator group with undecidable word problem if these undecidability results would hold, respectively. Thus, if the word problem is undecidable in two-relator groups, then to prove it would require some fundamentally new way of encoding undecidability into groups. If it is decidable, then some fundamentally new way of solving word problems must be developed, perhaps by extending the Magnus hierarchy to this setting. In either case, much exciting mathematics will surely follow!

Chapter 2
Recent progress

2.1.
Introduction

The origins of combinatorial group theory are often identified with Dehn’s solutions to the word and conjugacy problem for fundamental groups of closed hyperbolic surfaces. Dehn presented two approaches, one utilising the geometry of the hyperbolic plane
\mathbb{H}^{2}

[
Deh11
]
and another abstracting the geometric properties to purely combinatorial properties of their one-relator presentations
[
Deh12
]
:

\Sigma_{g}=\left\langle a_{1},b_{1},\ldots,a_{g},b_{g}\,\middle|\,\prod_{i=1}^{g}[a_{i},b_{i}]\right\rangle,\quad g\geqslant 2.

Generalisations of Dehn’s ideas would eventually lead to the very fruitful study of small cancellation groups and, later, hyperbolic groups.

The theory of amalgamated free products, developed by Schreier in the decade that followed
[
Sch27
]
, provided a third method of solving the word problem for surface groups. This algebraic method was picked up by Dehn’s doctoral student Magnus who used it to investigate the much more general class of
one-relator groups
; that is, groups admitting a presentation

G=\langle S\mid w\rangle

with a single defining relation. Magnus made two fundamental initial contributions to the theory by proving the Frieheitssatz in 1930
[
Mag30
]
and solving the word problem for all one-relator groups in 1932
[
Mag32
]
. These results initiated the systematic study of one-relator groups which has continued to develop and mature over the last century. After Magnus’ foundational work, one-relator groups quickly became a test-piece for new conjectures in group theory. Indeed, one-relator groups exhibit a wide variety of behaviours so that few properties hold for the whole class and characterising one-relator groups with a certain property is often a nuanced problem. This has the corollary that even fundamental problems like Dehn’s conjugacy and isomorphism problems are very difficult to solve for all one-relator groups and in fact remain wide open. On top of this, not a single theorem is known to hold for all two-relator groups that does not already hold for all finitely presented groups. It is therefore remarkable that one-relator groups admit a cohesive theory at all and even more so that it is sufficiently deep and developed that one can hope to approach most difficult problems.

Until the 80s, group theory remained a mostly algebraic and combinatorial subject. The focus in group theory then shifted towards more geometrically flavoured problems when Gromov introduced hyperbolic groups in
[
Gro87
]
. By the very nature of their definition, one-relator groups do not admit any obvious intrinsic geometry and so this shift initially had little impact on the theory of one-relator groups. Indeed, the geometry of one-relator groups has proven to be surprisingly complicated, resisting any form of decisive classification for some time.

On the other hand, one-relator groups have turned out to be very amenable for study via topological and homological means. The recent developments in
\cat(0)
cube complexes, 2-complexes with non-positive immersions and
L^{2}
-invariants have all had a significant impact on one-relator groups, resulting in new insights and resolutions of old conjectures that had seen little progress for many years. This includes, but is not limited to, the residual finiteness conjecture (posed by Baumslag in
[
Bau67c
]
and solved by Wise in
[
Wis21
]
), the coherence conjecture (posed by Baumslag in
[
Bau74b
]
and solved by Jaikin-Zapirain and the first author in
[
JZL23
]
) and the virtually free-by-cyclic conjecture (posed by Baumslag in
[
Bau86
]
and solved by Kielak and the first author in
[
KL24
]
).

The purpose of this chapter is to survey the current state of the art in the theory of one-relator groups with a particular emphasis on homological, topological and geometric aspects of one-relator groups, topics which, as mentioned above, have seen considerable advancement in the last decade. Some of the content of this survey is based on work of the first author, but the majority is based on recent work of Andrei Jaikin-Zapirain, Dawid Kielak, Lars Louder, Henry Wilton, Daniel Wise and many others, without whom this article would not warrant writing.

We aim to complement other pre-existing treatments of one-relator groups which are more classical and combinatorial in nature, see
[
Bau64
,
Bau74b
,
Bau86
,
LS01
,
MKS04
,
BFR19
]
for example. We include proofs of a few selected results, but mostly content ourselves with sketch proofs and ideas. Throughout, we also present open problems and directions for future research. As such, we hope this document can serve both as an invitation to the theory of one-relator groups and as a point of reference for anyone wishing to contribute and expand on the topics presented here.

Many results and questions we discuss here in reality apply (or can be generalised) to broader classes of groups which include:

•

One-relator products of groups (possibly with extra conditions on the factors),

•

Fundamental groups of 2-complexes with non-positive immersions,

•

Groups of cohomological dimension two with vanishing second
L^{2}
-Betti number.

Indeed, often results for these classes of groups arise from direct generalisations of arguments used to prove the same result for the simplest non-trivial case in the corresponding class, one-relator groups. For the sake of simplicity, in this article we mostly only state the versions of results for one-relator groups, pointing the reader towards references for further information.

2.1.1.
A brief description of the content

In
Section

2.2
we begin by discussing Magnus’ main tool for studying one-relator groups, the Magnus hierarchy. We discuss subsequent improvements to the hierarchy and cover a topological version using two-complexes in detail, as developed by the first author in
[
Lin22a
]
. The treatment includes several examples and a somewhat complete proof of the Freiheitssatz.

In
Section

2.3
we cover what is known about splittings of one-relator groups. We begin with an in depth analysis of the action of a one-relator group on the Bass–Serre tree associated with the splittings arising from its hierarchy. We then provide a sufficient condition for when a one-relator group is free-by-cyclic which we conjecture to also be necessary. We then consider the more specific case of when a one-relator group is {finitely generated free}-by-cyclic, which was completely determined by Moldavanskiĭ and later by Brown. Finally, we cover the work of Friedl–Tillmann and Henneke–Kielak on polytopes and splitting complexities for one-relator groups, finishing with some open problems on JSJ and amalgam decompositions.

In
Section

2.4
we cover (co)homological topics and properties of the group ring of one-relator groups. We discuss Lyndon’s identity theorem and the implications this has for the (co)homology of a one-relator group. We then cover recent results on groups with cyclic relation module and connections with the relation gap and relation lifting problems. We also cover
L^{2}
-invariants, the resolution of the strong Atiyah conjecture for one-relator groups by Jaikin-Zapirain–López-Álvarez and embeddings of group rings of torsion-free one-relator groups into division rings. Finally, we sketch a proof of the fact that the rational group ring of a one-relator group is coherent.

In
Section

2.5
we cover properties of subgroups of one-relator groups. We begin by covering the non-positive immersions property as introduced by Wise. We then cover work of Louder–Wilton who characterise when a one-relator group has the stronger property of negative immersions. This then leads us to connections between the
k
-freeness property and primitivity rank of elements of the free group. Combining these results with some homological properties, we then sketch a proof of the fact that all one-relator groups are coherent, covering also a strengthening of this theorem for the subclass of 2-free one-relator groups. Finally, we cover what is known about when a one-relator group is virtually free-by-cyclic, Mel’nikov’s conjecture, surface subgroups and subgroups of infinite index.

In
Section

2.6
we cover geometric properties of one-relator groups. We discuss Gersten’s conjecture on the hyperbolicity of one-relator groups, discuss a method of proving a one-relator group is hyperbolic which leads to a reduction of Gersten’s conjecture to the case of primitive extension groups and mention results that are known to hold for hyperbolic one-relator groups. Then, we cover
\cat(0)
and cubulated one-relator groups, demonstrating further the difficulty of geometric characterisations of one-relator groups with some pathological examples due to Gardam–Woodhouse. Finally, we discuss Wise’s quasi-convex hierarchies, Wise’s solution of the residual finiteness conjecture and an extension to the class of 2-free one-relator groups.

In
Section

2.7
we cover residual properties such as residual solvability, residual nilpotence, parafree groups and profinite completions.

In
Section

2.8
we cover the work that has been carried out on the complexity of the word problem for one-relator groups, the conjugacy problem, various membership problems, the normal root problem, generating sets of one-relator groups and the isomorphism problem.

2.2.
One-relator splittings and the hierarchy

2.2.1.
The Magnus–Moldavanskiĭ–Masters hierarchy

If
S
is a set, we shall write
S^{\pm 1}
to denote
S\sqcup S^{-1}
, the union of
S
with the set of its formal inverses and by
F(S)
the free group freely generated by the set
S
. A
one-relator group
is a group of the form

G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle

where
F
is a free group and where
\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
denotes the normal closure of the single element
w\in F
. We will always be assuming that
w\neq 1
. The most general tool used to study one relator groups is known as the
Magnus hierarchy
, developed by Magnus in his thesis
[
Mag30
]
in order to prove the Freiheitssatz.

Theorem 2.2.1

(Freiheitssatz)
.

Let
F
be a free group over a generating set
S
and let
w\in F
be a cyclically reduced element. If
T\subset S
is a subset such that
w\notin\langle T\rangle
, then the natural map

F(T)\to G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle

is injective.

A free subgroup of a one-relator group arising from
Theorem

2.2.1
is called a
Magnus subgroup
. The reader is directed to
Section

1.3
for more on the Freiheitssatz.

The idea of the original proof of
Theorem

2.2.1
is to find a suitable epimorphism
G\to\mathbb{Z}
in which the kernel can be decomposed as an infinite amalgamated free product of one-relator groups with strictly shorter relator length and then use the explicit structure of this decomposition, together with induction, to prove that the subset of generators we chose freely generate a free subgroup. There is a caveat with this sketch; such an epimorphism exists only when a generator appears with exponent sum zero in the relator, and this cannot always be the case. To get around this, Magnus embeds the one-relator group into another suitably chosen one-relator group in which some generator does appear with exponent sum zero. Then some technical arguments allows one to show that if the Freiheitssatz holds for the larger one-relator group, then it holds for the original one-relator group. Several proofs of
Theorem

2.2.1
have appeared that do not (explicitly) make use of Magnus’ hierarchy. See, for instance,
[
Lyn72
]
and
[
LW22
]
.

The ideas in Magnus’ proof are extremely powerful as they subsequently allowed Magnus to prove several strong results on the structure of one-relator groups, such as the decidability of the word problem
[
Mag32
]
. As is often the case, in order to make an inductive argument work, one needs to prove something stronger than the desired statement. Indeed, in order to solve the word problem in HNN-extensions or amalgamated free products, one needs both decidability of the word problem in the base groups as well as decidability of the membership problem in the associated subgroups. Thus, the actual statement that Magnus proved inductively in order to solve the word problem for one-relator groups was that the membership problem for any Magnus subgroup is decidable. Most other results on one-relator groups follow a similar structure, usually one proves that the result is true ‘relative’ (in an appropriate sense) to Magnus subgroups by induction on the length of a hierarchy, highlighting the importance of Magnus subgroups.

Over the many years that have passed since Magnus’ first paper, there have been several improvements to Magnus’ original version of the hierarchy, the most well known being due to Moldavanskiĭ
[
Mol67
]
which utilises HNN-extensions. Explicitly, Moldavanskiĭ noticed that the amalgamated product decompositions of the kernel of some epimorphism
G\to\mathbb{Z}
that arose in Magnus’ work could be bundled together to obtain a HNN-decomposition
G\cong H*_{\psi}
, with the original epimorphism being recoverd by quotienting by the normal closure of
H
. In particular, if
G
is a one-relator group, then Moldavanskiĭ proved that there is a diagram of one-relator groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G=G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{N}}

where
G_{N}
is a free product of a free group and a (possibly trivial) finite cyclic group and where
G_{i-1}^{\prime}\cong G_{i}*_{\psi_{i}}
where
\psi_{i}
identifies a pair of Magnus subgroups of
G_{i}
. All embeddings and splittings can also be described very explicitly in terms of the presentations. We shall not do this here, we direct the reader to
[
LS01
]
for more details. We remark that HNN-extensions had been introduced almost two decades after
[
Mag32
]
. A variation of this version of the hierarchy was also proven by Mihalik–Tschantz
[
MT92
]
who proved that all the embeddings above can be replaced with embeddings as (proper) free factors.

Expressing the Magnus hierarchy with HNN-extensions simplifies many of Magnus’ original arguments; indeed, McCool–Schupp then used Moldavanskiĭ’s improvement to reprove many of Magnus’ original results on one-relator groups in a short paper
[
MS73
]
.

Another version of the hierarchy that is worth mentioning is due to Masters. In an unpublished prepreint, Masters
[
Mas06
]
showed that one can do away with the embedding step in the hierarchy and obtained the following theorem.

Theorem 2.2.2

(The Algebraic Hierarchy)
.

Let
F
be a finitely free generated group,
w\in F
cyclically reduced and consider the one-relator group
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
. There is sequence of finitely generated subgroups
F_{N}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant F_{1}\leqslant F_{0}=F
containing
w
and isomorphisms
\psi_{i}\colon A_{i}\to B_{i}
between Magnus subgroups of
G_{i}=F_{i}/\langle\!\langle w_{i}\rangle\!\rangle
for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant N
, such that
F_{N}\cong\mathbb{Z}
and

G_{i-1}\cong G_{i}*_{\psi_{i}}

for each
0<i\leqslant N
.

The splittings that arise in this version are the same as those from the Magnus–Moldavanskiĭ hierarchy. However, in order to find them, Masters modifies the original presentation via Nielsen moves until a presentation with relator having exponent sum zero on some generator appears. This idea was already present in previous articles, the issue in using it to obtain a hierarchy had previously been that there was no argument to overcome the fact that the relator might get longer under this modification. The novelty of Masters proof is to show that, regardless, continuing to split one eventually reaches a free product of a free group with a finite cyclic group.

Some classical results follow directly from this version of the Magnus hierarchy, combined with general theory of HNN-extensions. However, the splittings are not given so explicitly in terms of the original presentation of the one-relator group making it more difficult to apply than the Magnus–Moldavanskiĭ hierarchy.

Yet another version of the hierarchy is provided by the first author in
[
Lin22a
]
. This hierarchy is phrased and proven in terms of 2-complexes. It both eliminates the embedding step from the Magnus–Moldavanskiĭ hierarchy and expresses the HNN-splittings very explicitly. Given that in this article we shall mostly be studying one-relator groups via 2-complexes, we shall cover this hierarchy in much more detail now. Moreover, this version implies
Theorem

2.2.2
.

2.2.2.
The topological hierarchy and some classical results

Before reaching the topological hierarchy statement, let us first set up some notation and definitions.

A
graph
is a 1-dimensional CW-complex. A combinatorial map of graphs is one that sends 0-cells to 0-cells and open 1-cells homeomorphically to open 1-cells. An
immersion
of graphs is a combinatorial map that is locally injective, which we denote by
\looparrowright
. If
\lambda\colon S^{1}\looparrowright\Lambda
is an immersion of a cycle, denote by
\deg(\lambda)
the maximal degree of a covering map
S^{1}\looparrowright S^{1}
that
\lambda
factors through. Note that
\deg(\lambda)=1
if and only if
\lambda_{*}(1)
is not a proper power in
\pi_{1}(\Lambda)
, where here
1\in\mathbb{Z}=\pi_{1}(S^{1})
is a generator. If
\lambda=\sqcup_{i}\lambda_{i}
where
\lambda_{i}\colon S^{1}\looparrowright\Lambda
, then write
\deg(\lambda)=\sum_{i}\deg(\lambda_{i})
. Here we are implicitly putting a combinatorial structure on
S^{1}
. When
\lambda
is an immersion of cycles
\mathbb{S}=\bigsqcup S^{1}
, we will write
|\lambda|
to denote the number of 1-cells in
\mathbb{S}
.

A
2-complex
will be used throughout to mean a two-dimensional CW-complex
X
in which all attaching maps of 2-cells are combinatorial immersions. We will usually write
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
where
\Lambda=X^{(1)}
is the 1-skeleton and where
\lambda\colon\mathbb{S}=\bigsqcup S^{1}\looparrowright\Lambda
is the attaching map for the 2-cells in
X
.

Our maps between 2-complexes will always be assumed to be
combinatorial
, that is, they will send open
n
-cells homeomorphically to
n
-cells. An
immersion
of 2-complexes is a locally injective combinatorial map which we shall usually use the arrow
\looparrowright
to denote. If
Z\looparrowright X
is an immersion of 2-complexes, it will be useful to bear in mind that there is an induced diagram of immersions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}_{\Lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
\looparrowleft
\looparrowleft
\looparrowleft
\looparrowleft

where the map
\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}\looparrowright\mathbb{S}_{\Lambda}
is a homeomorphism on each component.

A natural 2-complex to associate to a one-relator group is its presentation complex. That is, the 2-complex with a single 0-cell, a 1-cell for each generator and a single 2-cell whose attaching map spells out the relator. However, for the statement and proofs of results in this article it will be more useful to consider arbitrary 2-complexes with a single 2-cell.

Definition 2.2.3
.

A connected 2-complex
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
is a
one-relator complex
if
\lambda
is an immersion of a single cycle.

In direct analogy with the definition of Magnus subgroups, we define Magnus subgraphs.

Definition 2.2.4
.

If
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
is a one-relator complex, say a connected subgraph
\Gamma\subset\Lambda
is a
Magnus subgraph
if
\Gamma
does not support
\lambda
.

The aim of the next sections is to sketch a proof of topological versions of Magnus’ original results. The Freiheitssatz has an obvious translation.

Theorem 2.2.5

(Topological Freiheitssatz)
.

If
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
is a one-relator complex and
\Gamma\subset\Lambda
is a Magnus subgraph, then the map on
\pi_{1}
induced by inclusion

\pi_{1}(\Gamma)\to\pi_{1}(X)

is injective.

The Magnus hierarchy and variations do not have an immediate useful translation to the setting of 2-complexes. However, in
[
Lin22a
]
, the following theorem is proven. The statement is heavily influenced by Howie’s towers
[
How81
]
.

Theorem 2.2.6

(The Topological Hierarchy)
.

If
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
is a finite one-relator complex, then there exists a sequence of immersions

X_{N}\looparrowright\ldots\looparrowright X_{1}\looparrowright X_{0}=X.

such that

(1)

For all
0\leqslant i\leqslant N
,
X_{i}
is a finite one-relator complex.

(2)

\pi_{1}(X_{N})\cong\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
where
n=\deg(\lambda)
.

(3)

For all
1\leqslant i\leqslant N
, we have

\pi_{1}(X_{i-1})\cong\pi_{1}(X_{i})*_{\psi_{i}}

where
\psi_{i}
is induced by an isomorphism between Magnus subgraphs of
X_{i}
.

Remark 2.2.7
.

One could drop the assumption that
X
be finite in
Theorem

2.2.6
at the expense of removing the word ‘finite’ from the first conclusion and modifying the second conclusion to ‘
\pi_{1}(X_{N})\cong F*\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
for some free group
F
and where
n=\deg(\lambda)
’. However, if
X
is a one-relator complex, all the interesting information about
\pi_{1}(X)
can be derived by only looking at the smallest one-relator subcomplex, which is necessarily finite.

We now note some classical results which were proved using Magnus original hierarchy for one-relator groups. The proofs can all be simplified somewhat by using
Theorem

2.2.6
and the explicit description of the splittings provided by
Theorem

2.2.14
.

The first result is known as the
Magnus property
. A group
G
has the Magnus property if for any two elements
w,v\in G
, we have
\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle=\langle\!\langle v\rangle\!\rangle
if and only if
w
is conjugate to
v
or
v^{-1}
. Magnus proved in
[
Mag30
]
that free groups have the Magnus property. Below is the restatement in terms of one-relator complexes.

Theorem 2.2.8

(Magnus property)
.

Let
X=(\Lambda,\lambda_{1})
and
Y=(\Lambda,\lambda_{2})
be two one-relator complexes such that the identity map on
\pi_{1}(\Lambda)
induces an isomorphism
\pi_{1}(X)\to\pi_{1}(Y)
. Then the identity map on
\Lambda
extends to an isomorphism of one-relator complexes
X\to Y
.

Cockcroft characterised when a one-relator complex is aspherical in
[
Coc54
]
. His proof made use of Lyndon’s identity theorem
[
Lyn50
]
, which we shall cover in
Section

2.4.1
.

Theorem 2.2.9

(Asphericity)
.

If
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
is a one-relator complex, then
X
is aspherical if and only if
\deg(\lambda)=1
.

Karrass–Magnus–Solitar characterised which elements in a one-relator group have finite order in
[
KMS60
]
. The criterion is very easily checked by inspecting the relator.

Theorem 2.2.10

(Torsion)
.

If
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
is a one-relator complex, then
\pi_{1}(X)
has torsion if and only if
\deg(\lambda)\geqslant 2
. Moreover, every element of finite order in
\pi_{1}(X)
is conjugate to a root of
\lambda_{*}(1)
.

Theorem

2.2.10
follows from
Theorem

2.2.6
by noting that an element of finite order acting on a tree (the Bass–Serre tree of the splittings) must have a fixed point.

Theorem

2.2.10
can be stated in terms of presentations as follows. Let
F
be a free group,
w\in F
not a proper power and
n\geqslant 1
an integer. Then
G=F/\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle
has torsion if and only if
n\geqslant 2
. Moreover, all elements of finite order in
G
are conjugate into the subgroup
\langle w\rangle
.

To conclude this section, we state a result due to Howie
[
How82
]
and, independently, Brodskiĭ
[
Bro84
]
. Recall that a group is
locally indicable
if every non-trivial finitely generated subgroup admits an epimorphism to
\mathbb{Z}
. The proof of this theorem is not carried out using the Magnus hierarchy, rather using the method of towers.

Theorem 2.2.11

(Local indicability)
.

If
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
is a one-relator complex with
\deg(\lambda)=1
, then
\pi_{1}(X)
is locally indicable.

2.2.3.
One-relator splittings

Before diving into the details of
Theorem

2.2.6
, we now explain further where exactly these splittings come from. Firstly, we need a notion of complexity. This notion is related to the relator length as used by Magnus, and is also closely related to a notion of complexity used by Howie in
[
How82
]
. We are following
[
Lin22a
]
in this section and those that follow.

Definition 2.2.12
.

If
\lambda\colon S^{1}\looparrowright\Lambda
is an immersed cycle, denote by

c(\lambda)=\frac{|\lambda|}{\deg(\lambda)}-\left|\Ima(\lambda)^{(0)}\right|

the
complexity of
\lambda
. If
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
is a one-relator complex, the
complexity of
X
is the tuple

c(X)=(c(\lambda),-\chi(X))

which is given the dictionary order.

Remark 2.2.13
.

If
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
is a one-relator complex, then
c(\lambda)=0
if and only if
\lambda
factors through an embedded cycle
S^{1}\hookrightarrow\Lambda
. In particular, if
c(\lambda)=0
, then
\pi_{1}(X)\cong F*\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
where
F
is a free group and
n=\deg(\lambda)
. Moreover,
c(X)
reaches its minimal value
(0,0)
if and only if
\pi_{1}(X)\cong\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
.

The following theorem explicitly identifies the splittings that arise in
Theorem

2.2.6
. The statement is rather technical, but we will show with some examples how exactly one can apply it to compute hierarchies. Recall that a
\mathbb{Z}
-cover is a covering space
\rho\colon Y\to X
such that
\deck(\rho)\cong\mathbb{Z}
. We shall be using the notion of graphs of spaces, as introduced by Scott–Wall
[
SW79
]
.

Theorem 2.2.14

(One-relator splittings)
.

Let
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
be a finite one-relator complex and let
\rho\colon Y\to X
be a connected
\mathbb{Z}
-cover with
t\in\deck(\rho)
a generator.

•

(Existence of a one-relator
\mathbb{Z}
-domain) There exists a finite connected subcomplex
Z\subset Y
, called a
\mathbb{Z}
-domain
, with the following properties:

(1)

Z
is one-relator and
Z^{(1)}
supports the attaching map of precisely one 2-cell in
Y
.

(2)

Y=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}t^{i}(Z)
.

(3)

Z_{0}=t^{-1}(Z)\cap Z
and
Z_{1}=t(Z_{0})=Z\cap t(Z)
are Magnus subgraphs of
Z
.

(4)

For any
i>0
, we have
Z\cap t^{i}(Z)\subset Z\cap t^{i-1}(Z)
.

•

(Graph of spaces/groups decomposition) If
Z\subset Y
is any one-relator
\mathbb{Z}
-domain as above, then, denoting by
\iota\colon Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z
the inclusion and by

\mathcal{X}=Z\sqcup\left(Z_{0}\times[-1,1]\right)/\{\iota(z)\sim(z,-1),\,(t\circ\iota)(z)\sim(z,1),\,z\in Z_{0}\},

the map

h\colon\mathcal{X}\to X

given by
\rho
when restricted to
Z
or
Z_{0}\times\{i\}
for all
i\in(-1,1)
, is a homotopy equivalence. This homotopy equivalence induces an isomorphism:

\pi_{1}(X)\cong\pi_{1}(Z)*_{\psi}

where
\psi\colon\pi_{1}(Z_{0})\to\pi_{1}(Z_{1})
is induced by
t\mid_{Z_{0}}\colon Z_{0}\to Z_{1}
.

•

(Decreasing complexity) There exists some one-relator
\mathbb{Z}
-domain
Z\subset Y
as above with

c(Z)<c(X).

The first bullet point is
[
Lin22a
, Proposition 4.9]
, the second is
[
Lin22a
, Proposition 4.3]
and the third is
[
Lin22a
, Proposition 4.11]
.

Definition 2.2.15
.

If
X
is a one-relator complex, we will call a splitting
\pi_{1}(X)\cong\pi_{1}(Z)*_{\psi}
as arising from
Theorem

2.2.14
a
one-relator splitting
of
\pi_{1}(X)
.

Definition 2.2.16
.

If
X
is a one-relator complex, a
one-relator tower
for
X
is a sequence of immersions of one-relator complexes

X_{N}\looparrowright\ldots\looparrowright X_{1}\looparrowright X_{0}=X

such that for each
i
,
X_{i+1}
is obtained from
X_{i}
as in
Theorem

2.2.14
.

If
X
is a 2-complex, the
\mathbb{Z}
-covers of
X
are in bijection with non-trivial homomorphisms
\pi_{1}(X)\to\mathbb{Z}
, or rather, with non-zero elements of
H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. The connected
\mathbb{Z}
-covers are in bijection with epimorphisms
\pi_{1}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
, or rather, with basis elements of
H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. We will make this bijection more explicit in
Section

2.2.6
. When
X
is a one-relator complex, such epimorphisms exist if and only if
\pi_{1}(X)
is not isomorphic to a finite cyclic group. In particular, at least one epimorphism exists precisely when
c(X)>(0,0)
.
Theorem

2.2.6
now follows by
Theorem

2.2.14
and induction.

2.2.4.
Explicit examples of the topological hierarchy

Our first example will be the example which was the inspiration for
Theorem

2.2.14
. The prototypical one-relator complex is a closed surface. So let
X
be a closed surface, let
C\subset X
be a non-separating simple closed curve and consider the epimorphism
\pi_{1}(X)\to H_{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{Z}\cdot[C]
, where here
[C]
denotes the homology class of
C
in
H_{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. We can always modify the 2-complex structure of
X
so that
C
actually lies in the 1-skeleton of
X
. Then if
\rho\colon Y\to X
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-cover associated with this homomorphism, one can take
Z\subset Y
from
Theorem

2.2.14
to be the closure of
\rho^{-1}(X-C)
. Here
Z
is a surface with two boundary components corresponding to two distinct lifts of
C
. If
t\in\deck(\rho)
is a generator, then
Z_{0}=t^{-1}(Z)\cap Z
and
Z_{1}=Z\cap t(Z)
are precisely these two lifts. The space
\mathcal{X}
from
Theorem

2.2.14
is then obtained from
Z
by attaching a cylinder connecting these two boundary components and expresses
\pi_{1}(X)
as a HNN-extension over an infinite cyclic group.
Fig.

2.1
illustrates this example explicitly in the case
X
is a genus two surface and
C
is a simple closed curve going around one of the genus.

Figure 2.1.
A simple illustration of
Theorem

2.2.14
in the case of a genus two surface.

Our next example will be the presentation complex
X
of the following one-relator group

G=\langle a,b\mid a^{2}=b^{3}\rangle.

A simple computation shows that
H_{1}(G,\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
and so there is only one epimorphism
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}
(up to change of sign) given by

\phi(a)=3,\quad\phi(b)=2.

Denote by
\rho\colon Y\looparrowright X
the associated
\mathbb{Z}
-cover. Choose some lift
v_{0}\in Y
of the 0-cell in
X
and lifts
a_{0},b_{0}\subset Y
of the two 1-cells in
X
such that they lead out of
v_{0}
. Denote by
v_{i}=t^{i}(v)
and
a_{i}=t^{i}(a_{0})
,
b_{i}=t^{i}(b_{0})
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Reading out the relator
a^{2}b^{-3}
, we see that for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
there is a 2-cell in
Y
whose attaching map is given by the path

a_{i}a_{i+3}b_{i+4}^{-1}b_{i+2}^{-1}b_{i}^{-1}.

Hence, we see that there is a single attaching map (the one corresponding to
i=0
) of a 2-cell of
Y
supported in the subgraph

\Gamma=\left(\bigcup_{i=0}^{6}v_{i}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{i=0}^{3}a_{i}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{i=0}^{4}b_{i}\right)

and no attaching map of a 2-cell is supported in
t^{-1}(\Gamma)\cap\Gamma
or
\Gamma\cap t(\Gamma)
. Letting
Z\subset Y
be the one-relator subcomplex with 1-skeleton
\Gamma
, we see that
Z
satisfies all the hypotheses of
Theorem

2.2.14
. This is depicted in
Fig.

2.2
.

Figure 2.2.
A one-relator
\mathbb{Z}
-domain.

In order to obtain the one-relator splitting explicitly we need to choose generators for
\pi_{1}(Z)
. The elements

c_{1}=a_{0}b_{1}^{-1}a_{1}b_{2}^{-1}b_{0}^{-1},\quad c_{2}=b_{0}a_{2}b_{3}^{-1}a_{0}^{-1},\quad c_{3}=a_{0}a_{3}b_{4}^{-1}b_{2}^{-1}b_{0}^{-1}

are seen to generate
\pi_{1}(Z,v_{0})
, with the first two generating
\pi_{1}(Z_{0},v_{0})
and the last being trivial. They come from choosing the spanning tree
T\subset\Gamma
such that
\Gamma-T
contains the 1-cells
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
. Denoting by

d=a_{0}b_{1}^{-1},

we have

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(X)\cong

\displaystyle\,\pi_{1}(Z)*_{\psi}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\,\langle c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\mid c_{3}=1\rangle*_{\psi}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\,\langle c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},d\mid c_{3}=1,dc_{1}d^{-1}=c_{2}c_{3},dc_{2}d^{-1}=c_{3}c_{1}^{-1}\rangle

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\,\langle c_{1},c_{2},d\mid dc_{1}d^{-1}=c_{2},dc_{2}d^{-1}=c_{1}^{-1}\rangle

where the last isomorphism is obtained by applying a Tietze transformation removing the redundant generator
c_{3}
and relator
c_{3}=1
.

The last presentation above expresses
\pi_{1}(X)
as an
F_{2}
-by-cyclic group with monodromy an order four automorphism. One can see the fact that
\pi_{1}(X)
is free-by-cyclic geometrically: the 2-cell in
Z
traverses the bottom edges and the top edges each precisely once and so
Z
collapses to
Z_{0}=t^{-1}(Z)\cap Z
and to
Z\cap t(Z)
. Thus, the one-relator splitting expresses
\pi_{1}(X)
as a HNN-extension where the identifying isomorphism is defined on all of
\pi_{1}(Z)
- the fundamental group of a graph.

We remark here that our presentation for
\pi_{1}(X)
depended on our chosen spanning tree, however it is not hard to see that we also could have obtained the following presentation:

\pi_{1}(X)\cong\langle c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},d\mid c_{3}c_{1},d^{-1}c_{1}d=c_{2},d^{-1}c_{2}d=c_{3}\rangle

which is perhaps more what those familiar with the Magnus–Moldavanskiĭ method might expect.

Remark 2.2.17
.

If
G=F(S)/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group in which some generator
t\in S
appears with exponent sum zero, there is an epimorphism
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}
defined by sending each generator different to
t
to zero and by sending
t
to one. Taking
X
to be the presentation complex for
G
, the
\mathbb{Z}
-cover associated with
\phi
has 1-skeleton a biinfinite line consisting of the lifts of 1-cells corresponding to
t
and at each 0-cell there is a loop for every generator in
S-\{t\}
. The one-relator splitting arising from a
\mathbb{Z}
-domain of this cover as in
Theorem

2.2.14
is precisely the type of HNN-splitting that Moldavanskiĭ finds in
[
Mol67
]
.

2.2.5.
Sketch of the proof of
Theorem

2.2.14

We now sketch out the main ideas of the proof of
Theorem

2.2.14
. There are essentially three parts to the proof with each part corresponding to the three points in
Theorem

2.2.14
.

Part one: HNN-decompositions from
\mathbb{Z}
-domains

The first step involves showing that certain kinds of subcomplexes of
\mathbb{Z}
-covers give rise to HNN-splittings.

Definition 2.2.18
.

Let
\rho\colon Y\to X
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-cover of 2-complexes and let
t\colon Y\to Y
be a generator of
\deck(\rho)=\mathbb{Z}
. A subcomplex
Z\subset Y
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-domain
if the following holds:

(1)

The
\mathbb{Z}
-translates of
Z
cover all of
Y
. That is,
Y=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}t^{i}(Z)
.

(2)

Z\cap t(Z)
is connected and non-empty.

(3)

For each
i>0
, we have that
Z\cap t^{i}(Z)\subset Z\cap t^{i-1}(Z)
.

The following result is the motivation behind the definition of
\mathbb{Z}
-domains.

Proposition 2.2.19
.

Let
\rho\colon Y\to X
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-cover of 2-complexes, let
t\colon Y\to Y
be a generator of
\deck(\rho)
, let
Z\subset Y
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-domain and denote by
Z_{0}=t^{-1}(Z)\cap Z
and
\iota\colon Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z
the inclusion. If

\mathcal{X}=Z\sqcup\left(Z_{0}\times[-1,1]\right)/\{\iota(z)\sim(z,-1),\,(t\circ\iota)(z)\sim(z,1),\,z\in Z_{0}\},

then the map

h\colon\mathcal{X}\to X

given by
\rho
when restricted to
Z
or
Z_{0}\times\{i\}
for all
i\in(-1,1)
, is a homotopy equivalence.

Moreover, if
\iota_{*}
and
(t\circ\iota)_{*}
are injective, then

\pi_{1}(X)\cong\pi_{1}(Z)*_{\psi}

where

\psi=(\iota\circ t\circ\iota^{-1})_{*}\colon\iota_{*}(\pi_{1}(Z_{0}))\to(t\circ\iota)_{*}(\pi_{1}(Z_{0})).

Proof.

The map
h
is surjective by property (
1
) of
\mathbb{Z}
-domains. Moreover, property
2
says that
Z_{0}
is connected and non-empty. Finally, property (
3
) implies that the fibers
h^{-1}(x)
are all connected (possibly infinite) lines. In particular, this implies that
h
is a homotopy equivalence. See
[
Lin22a
, Proposition 3.2]
for further details and a more general result.

If the homomorphisms
\iota_{*},(t\circ\iota)_{*}
are injective, then
\mathcal{X}
is a graph of spaces with a single vertex space
Z
and a single edge space
Z_{0}\times[-1,1]
, attached to
Z
via
\iota
and
t\circ\iota
. Hence, we have
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{X})\cong\pi_{1}(Z)*_{\psi}
. Since
h
is a homotopy equivalence, we also have
\pi_{1}(X)\cong\pi_{1}(Z)*_{\psi}
as claimed.
∎

Part two: existence of one-relator
\mathbb{Z}
-domains

The second step involves proving that
\mathbb{Z}
-domains always exist for
\mathbb{Z}
-covers of one-relator complexes and that they may additionally be chosen so that they only support a single attaching map of a 2-cell. Then the Freiheitssatz combined with
Proposition

2.2.19
will allow us to find the desired splitting.

Proposition 2.2.20
.

[
Lin22a
, Lemma 4.7]

If
\rho\colon Y\to\Lambda
is a connected
\mathbb{Z}
-cover of 1-complexes, then there exists a
\mathbb{Z}
-domain
Z\subset Y
. Moreover, if
Z
is minimal under inclusion of
\mathbb{Z}
-domains, then
Z\cap t(Z)
is a tree and, if
\Lambda
is finite,
Z
is finite.

From
Proposition

2.2.20
it is not hard to prove the following.

Corollary 2.2.21
.

If
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
is a one-relator complex and
\rho\colon Y\to X
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-cover, then there exists a
\mathbb{Z}
-domain
Z\subset Y
, finite if
X
is finite, which supports precisely one lift of
\lambda
.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset Y^{(1)}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-domain for the
\mathbb{Z}
-cover
Y^{(1)}\to X^{(1)}
. Let
k\geqslant 0
be the smallest integer such that

\Gamma=\bigcup_{i=0}^{k}t^{k}(\Gamma^{\prime})

supports some lift of
\lambda
. Let
Z\subset Y
be the one-relator subcomplex obtained from
\Gamma
by attaching a 2-cell along a lift of
\lambda
. Since
\Gamma^{\prime}
was a
\mathbb{Z}
-domain for
Y^{(1)}\to X^{(1)}
, the three properties (
1
), (
2
) and (
3
) required of a
\mathbb{Z}
-domain holds for
Z
. Since
\Gamma^{\prime}
is finite when
\Lambda
is,
Z
is finite when
X
is. Furthermore, if
Z
does not contain precisely one lift of an attaching map of some 2-cell in
X
, this would imply that
t^{-1}(Z)\cap Z
supports a lift of
\lambda
, contradicting our choice of
\Gamma
. Thus,
Z
supports precisely one lift of
\lambda
.
∎

It will sometimes be useful to have a refinement of
Corollary

2.2.21
specifically for one-relator complexes. This statement is
[
Lin24b
, Proposition 4.4]
.

Proposition 2.2.22
.

Let
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
be a one-relator complex with
c(\lambda)>0
and let
e\subset X
be a 1-cell traversed by
\lambda
. There exists a
\mathbb{Z}
-cover
\rho\colon Y\to X
and a one-relator
\mathbb{Z}
-domain
(\Lambda_{Z},\lambda_{Z})=Z\subset Y
with
c(\lambda_{Z})<c(\lambda)
such that
\rho^{-1}(\Lambda-e)\subset Z
and the following holds. If
\tilde{e}\subset Z
is a lift of
e
to
Z
and
m,M\in\mathbb{Z}
are, respectively, the minimal and maximal integers such that
t^{m}(\tilde{e})\subset Z
and
t^{M}(\tilde{e})\subset Z
, then
\lambda_{Z}
traverses both
t^{m}(\tilde{e})
and
t^{M}(\tilde{e})
.

For an arbitrary
\mathbb{Z}
-cover of a one-relator complex
\rho\colon Y\to X
, by
Corollary

2.2.21
we know that there is
some
1-cell
e\subset X
and a one-relator
\mathbb{Z}
-domain
Z\subset Y
such that the attaching map for the 2-cell in
Z
traverses the minimal lift of
e
and the maximal lift. The advantage of
Proposition

2.2.22
is that it allows us to first choose a 1-cell in
X
and then procure a
\mathbb{Z}
-cover and a
\mathbb{Z}
-domain with these properties. This version is particularly useful when generalising results on one-relator groups to the setting of one-relator products of locally indicable groups.

Remark 2.2.23
.

One should compare
Proposition

2.2.22
with the usual method of finding a splitting of a one-relator group over a one-relator subgroup, as described, for example, in
[
LS01
]
.

Remark 2.2.24
.

With almost the same proof,
Corollary

2.2.21
can easily be generalised to arbitrary finite 2-complexes
X
. Then using
Proposition

2.2.19
one can prove that for any finitely presented group
G
and any epimorphism
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}
, there is an element
t\in G
such that
\phi(t)=1
, a finitely generated subgroup
H\leqslant\ker(\phi)
and two finitely generated subgroups
A=H^{t^{-1}}\cap H
,
B=H\cap H^{t}
of
H
such that

G\cong H*_{\psi}

where
\psi\colon A\to B
is the isomorphism induced by conjugation by
t
. In fact, one only needs
G
to have type
\fp_{2}(R)
for some ring
R
. This is proven by Bieri–Strebel in
[
BS78
, Theorem A]
. We will state a much more general, but much less explicit, result in
Section

2.5.4
.

Part three: decrease of complexity

The final step involves showing that the complexity of the one-relator
\mathbb{Z}
-domain from
Corollary

2.2.21
goes down.

Lemma 2.2.25
.

If
Z=(\Lambda_{Z},\lambda_{Z})
and
X=(\Lambda_{X},\lambda_{X})
are one-relator complexes and
\sigma\colon Z\looparrowright X
is an immersion, then

c(\lambda_{Z})\leqslant c(\lambda_{X})

with equality precisely when
\sigma\mid\Ima(\lambda_{Z})
is injective.

Proof.

If
\lambda_{X}
is the attaching map for
X
and
\lambda_{Z}
is the attaching map for
Z
, we have that

\frac{|\lambda_{Z}|}{\deg(\lambda_{X})}=\frac{|\lambda_{Z}|}{\deg(\lambda_{Z})}.

Moreover, we have

\left|\Ima(\lambda_{X})^{(0)}\right|\leqslant\left|\Ima(\lambda_{Z})^{(0)}\right|

with equality precisely when
\sigma\mid\Ima(\lambda_{Z})^{(0)}
is a bijection. Since
\sigma
is an immersion, this would force
\sigma
to be injective on all of
\Ima(\lambda_{Z})
.
∎

Corollary 2.2.26
.

Let
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
be a one-relator complex and let
\rho\colon Y\looparrowright X
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-cover. If
Z\subset Y
is a one-relator
\mathbb{Z}
-domain, minimal under inclusion amongst one-relator
\mathbb{Z}
-domains, then

c(Z)<c(X).

Proof.

Let
\lambda_{Z}
denote the attaching map of the 2-cell in
Z
. The cover
\rho
sends
\Ima(\lambda_{Z})
surjectively to
\Ima(\lambda)
. If
c(\lambda_{Z})\geqslant c(\lambda)
, then
c(\lambda_{Z})=c(\lambda)
by
Lemma

2.2.25
and
\Ima(\lambda_{Z})
maps isomorphically to
\Ima(\lambda)
. By collapsing each 2-cell to a point in
X
, we obtain an induced commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{\prime}}

where vertical maps are collapse maps. In particular,
Z^{\prime},Y^{\prime},X^{\prime}
are all 1-complexes. Since
Z^{\prime}-t^{-1}(Z^{\prime})\cap Z^{\prime}
contains precisely one lift of each cell in
X^{\prime}
, we have
-\chi(Z^{\prime})=-\chi(X^{\prime})-\chi(t^{-1}(Z^{\prime})\cap Z^{\prime})
. Hence, by
Proposition

2.2.20
, since
Z
was minimal, it follows that
Z^{\prime}\cap t(Z^{\prime})
is a tree and so
-\chi(Z^{\prime})=-\chi(X^{\prime})-1
. This implies that
-\chi(Z)<\chi(X)
.
∎

Completing the proof: the Freiheitssatz

Combining
Proposition

2.2.19
,
Corollary

2.2.21
and
Corollary

2.2.26
, we have proven
Theorem

2.2.14
under the assumption that the Freiheitssatz holds. This is because in order to apply
Proposition

2.2.19
, we needed
\pi_{1}(Z_{0}),\pi_{1}(Z_{1})\to\pi_{1}(Z)
to be injective.

In order to prove the Freiheitssatz for
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
, we assume it holds for all one-relator complexes
Z
with
c(Z)<c(X)
(the base case
c(X)=0
is clear). Let
e\subset\Lambda
be a 1-cell traversed by
\lambda
and denote by
\Gamma=\Lambda-e
. By
Proposition

2.2.22
, there is a
\mathbb{Z}
-cover
\rho\colon Y\to\mathbb{Z}
and a one-relator
\mathbb{Z}
-domain
Z\subset Y
with
c(Z)<c(X)
and such that
Z\cap t(Z)
does not support any attaching map of a 2-cell and such that
\rho^{-1}(\Gamma)\subset Z
. Thus, the homomorphisms
\pi_{1}(Z_{0}),\pi_{1}(Z_{1})\to\pi_{1}(Z)
are both injective by the inductive hypothesis and we may apply
Proposition

2.2.19
to conclude that

\pi_{1}(X)\cong\pi_{1}(Z)*_{\psi}.

In particular,
\pi_{1}(Z)
injects into
\pi_{1}(X)
. This implies that two paths in
X
that lift to
Z
are path homotopic if and only if they are path homotopic in
Z
. Since
\rho^{-1}(\Gamma)\subset Z
, any pair of paths in
\Gamma
lift to
Z
. Since each component of
\rho^{-1}(\Gamma)
is a Magnus subgraph of
Z
, by induction it follows that any pair of paths in
\rho^{-1}(\Gamma)
are path homotopic in
Z
if and only if they are path homotopic in
\rho^{-1}(\Gamma)
. Thus, any pair of paths in
\Gamma
that are path homotopic in
X
are path homotopic in
\Gamma
. Thus, the map

\pi_{1}(\Gamma)\to\pi_{1}(X)

induced by inclusion is injective. We have proven the Freiheitssatz and hence,
Theorem

2.2.14
.

2.2.6.
The character sphere and describing all one-relator splittings

If
G
is a group, the
character sphere

S(G)
is the following space

S(G)=\left(H^{1}(G,\mathbb{R})-\{0\}\right)/\mathbb{R}_{>0}

of equivalence classes of homomorphisms
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{R}
, where
\phi\sim\phi^{\prime}
if
\phi=r\cdot\phi^{\prime}
for some
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

Let
S
be a finite set of cardinality
n
, let
F=F(S)
be a free group, let
w\in F
be a cyclically reduced word and let
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
be a one-relator group. Denote by
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}=H^{1}(F,\mathbb{R})
the Cayley graph for
F_{\ab}=\mathbb{Z}^{n}
over the generating set given by the image of
S
in
F_{\ab}
. Let
\lambda_{w}\colon I\to\Gamma
denote the path at the origin spelling out the word
w
and denote by
\Lambda_{w}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
its image. Let
p_{w}
denote the endpoint of
\lambda_{w}
. We have
p_{w}=0
if and only if
w\in[F,F]
. The path
\lambda_{w}
is called the
trace
of
w
. See
Fig.

2.3
for an example.

There is a natural correspondence:

S(G)\leftrightarrow\left\{\text{Directed lines $L\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}$ through the origin with $p_{w}\in L^{\bot}$}\right\}

where a line
L_{\phi}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is associated with a homomorphism
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{R}
(unique in the
S(G)
equivalence class) via the composition of the map
G\to H_{1}(G,\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the orthogonal projection of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
onto the line
L_{\phi}
. More explicitly, if
p_{\phi}\in L_{\phi}
is the point at (positive) unit distance from the origin, then each element in
r\cdot p_{\phi}+L_{\phi}^{\bot}
gets mapped to
r\in\mathbb{R}
.

Figure 2.3.
The trace of the word
a^{2}b^{3}ab^{-2}a^{-1}bab^{-1}a^{-2}bab^{-1}a^{-2}b^{-1}
.

Now let
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
be the presentation complex for
G=F(S)/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
and let
\phi\in H^{1}(G,\mathbb{R})
be a homomorphism
G\to\mathbb{R}
. Let
L_{\phi}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the corresponding line. The 1-skeleton of the abelian cover
Y\to X
associated with
\phi
is the quotient of
\Gamma
defined by identifying all
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
orbits of points that lie in the same plane
r\cdot p_{\phi}+L_{\phi}^{\bot}
for each
r\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that
Y^{(1)}
has a natural map
Y^{(1)}\to\mathbb{R}
given by sending the points in
r\cdot p_{\phi}+L_{\phi}^{\bot}
to
r\in\mathbb{R}
. The path
\lambda_{w}\colon I\to\Gamma
projects to a loop in
Y^{(1)}
. Denote by
\lambda_{\phi}\colon S^{1}\looparrowright Y^{(1)}
the cycle obtained from
I\xrightarrow{\lambda_{w}}\Gamma\xrightarrow{}Y^{(1)}
by identifying the endpoints of
I
. The abelian cover
Y
is then obtained from
Y^{(1)}
by attaching cycles along the
G
-orbit of
\lambda_{\phi}
. Denote also by
\Lambda_{\phi}\subset Y^{(1)}
the image of
\Lambda_{w}
so that
(\Lambda_{\phi},\lambda_{\phi})=Z_{\phi}\subset Y
is a one-relator subcomplex. This construction can also be generalised for arbitrary one-relator complexes by contracting and expanding a spanning tree, we leave the details to the reader. Call
Z_{\phi}

the one-relator complex associated with
\phi
. Note that
Z_{\phi}\cong Z_{\psi}
for all
\psi\in\pm[\phi]
.

Example 2.2.27
.

Let us illustrate how to obtain the one-relator complex
Z_{\phi}
associated with
\phi
through an example. In
Fig.

2.3
we have depicted the trace of the word
w=a^{2}b^{3}ab^{-2}a^{-1}bab^{-1}a^{-2}bab^{-1}a^{-2}b^{-1}
. Since
w\in[F(a,b),F(a,b)]
we have
p_{w}=0
and so any directed line through the origin gives us a homomorphism
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{R}
where
G=F(a,b)/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
. Let
L_{\phi}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the directed line passing through the origin and through the point
(1,2)
. Then the induced surjective map
\Lambda_{w}\to Z^{(1)}_{\phi}
identifies two pairs of vertices, the pairs
(0,1),(2,0)
and
(1,2),(3,1)
, and does not identify any edges. See
Fig.

2.4
for a depiction of
Z_{\phi}^{(1)}
. Then
Z_{\phi}
is obtained from
Z_{\phi}^{(1)}
by attaching a 2-cell along the loop
I\to\Lambda_{w}\to Z_{\phi}^{(1)}
.

Note that by adding the missing
b
-edge and the missing
a
-edge to
Z_{\phi}
(that is, the
b
-edge connecting
\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}
with
\frac{3}{\sqrt{5}}
and the
a
-edge connecting
\frac{7}{\sqrt{5}}
with
\frac{8}{\sqrt{5}}
), then we obtain a
\mathbb{Z}
-domain for the
\mathbb{Z}
-cover of the presentation complex induced by
\phi
.

Figure 2.4.
The 1-skeleton of
Z_{\phi}
, obtained by projecting
\Lambda_{w}
onto
L_{\phi}
.

We have the following which follows from the definition of one-relator splittings and the fact that every 1-cell in
\Lambda_{\phi}
is traversed by the attaching map
\lambda_{\phi}
.

Lemma 2.2.28
.

Let
X
be a one-relator complex, let
\pi_{1}(X)\cong\pi_{1}(Z)*_{\psi}
be a one-relator splitting with
\phi\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\pi_{1}(X)/\langle\!\langle\pi_{1}(Z)\rangle\!\rangle=\mathbb{Z}
the associated epimorphism. Then, after possibly replacing
Z
by a translate, the one-relator complex
Z_{\phi}
associated with
\phi
is a subcomplex of
Z
. In particular,
\pi_{1}(Z)
splits as a free product of a free group and
\pi_{1}(Z_{\phi})
.

In this way we may see all base groups of all one-relator splittings of
\pi_{1}(X)
by looking at appropriate quotients of
\Lambda_{w},\lambda_{w}
. In fact, there is always a maximal such quotient, in the sense that this quotient one-relator complex quotients onto all other
Z_{\phi}
. Indeed, we may always choose a homomorphism
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{R}
which factors through an epimorphism
G\to\mathbb{Z}
such that two points
p,q\in\Lambda_{w}\subset\Gamma
in the same
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
-orbit are identified under the quotient map
\Lambda_{w}\to\Lambda_{\phi}
if and only if
p+\mathbb{R}\cdot p_{w}=q+\mathbb{R}\cdot p_{w}
. Let
\Lambda_{\max}
denote this maximal quotient and let
\lambda_{\max}\colon S^{1}\looparrowright\Lambda_{\max}
be the induced cycle. Then by construction, the one-relator complex
Z_{\max}=(\Lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\max})
admits a unique immersion
Z_{\max}\looparrowright Z_{\phi^{\prime}}
for all epimorphisms
\phi^{\prime}\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}
. We have proven the following lemma in the case
X
has a single 0-cell, the more general case is left as an easy exercise to the reader.

Lemma 2.2.29
.

If
X
is a one-relator complex with
\pi_{1}(X)
infinite, then there is a unique immersion of a one-relator complex
Z_{\max}\looparrowright X
such that
Z_{\max}\looparrowright X
factors through all immersions of
\mathbb{Z}
-domains of all
\mathbb{Z}
-covers of
X
, with at least one
\mathbb{Z}
-domain containing
Z_{\max}
as a subcomplex.

Another way to see
Z_{\max}
is as a minimal one-relator subcomplex of the maximal torsion-free abelian cover of
X
. This is explained in more detail in
[
Lin22b
]
.

As an illustration of how to obtain
Z_{\max}
, in
2.2.27
we may take the line
L_{\phi}
to pass through the origin and
(1,3)
instead of
(1,2)
. In this way, no vertices are identified under the projection map
\Lambda_{w}\to Z_{\phi}
.

2.3.
Splittings of one-relator groups

2.3.1.
Magnus subgroups and the Bass–Serre theory of one-relator splittings

In this subsection, we discuss some properties of the action of a one-relator group on the Bass–Serre tree associated with a one-relator splitting. In order to understand the action, we need to understand the interaction between the edge groups and their conjugates. Thus, in the first part of this subsection we shall look at intersections of Magnus subgroups.

Intersections of Magnus subgroups

Two results due to Collins are particularly important for this topic. The first involves exceptional intersections. A pair of Magnus subgraphs
A,B\subset X
of a one-relator complex with
A\cap B
are said to have
exceptional intersection
if
\pi_{1}(A)\cap\pi_{1}(B)\neq\pi_{1}(A\cap B)
. In other words, if the intersection is not the obvious one. Collins characterised exceptional intersections in
[
Col04
]
. Howie also characterised exceptional intersections for one-relator products and presented an algorithm to compute intersections of Magnus subgroups in
[
How05
]
.

Theorem 2.3.1
.

Let
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
be a one-relator complex and let
A,B\subset X
be Magnus subgraphs such that
A\cap B
is connected. One of the following holds:

•

\pi_{1}(A)\cap\pi_{1}(B)=\pi_{1}(A\cap B)
.

•

\pi_{1}(A)\cap\pi_{1}(B)=\pi_{1}(A\cap B)*\langle c\rangle
for some
c\in\pi_{1}(X)
.

Moreover, if
\deg(\lambda)\geqslant 2
, then only the first situation occurs.

Howie’s result
[
How05
]
provides further information: if
\pi_{1}(A)\cap\pi_{1}(B)
have an exceptional intersection, then there is an immersion of a one-relator complex
Z\looparrowright X
which expresses this intersection in an explicit way. This extra information was exploited heavily in
[
Lin24a
]
.

The second result involves intersections between conjugates of Magnus subgroups and was proved by Collins in
[
Col08
]
. These turn out to be much more controlled.

Theorem 2.3.2
.

Let
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
be a one-relator complex and let
A,B\subset X
be Magnus subgraphs such that
A\cap B
is connected. If
g\in\pi_{1}(X)
, one of the following holds:

•

g\in\pi_{1}(A)\cdot\pi_{1}(B)
, in which case
\pi_{1}(A)^{g}\cap\pi_{1}(B)=(\pi_{1}(A)\cap\pi_{1}(B))^{b}
for some
b\in\pi_{1}(B)
.

•

\pi_{1}(A)^{g}\cap\pi_{1}(B)=1
.

•

\pi_{1}(A)^{g}\cap\pi_{1}(B)=\langle c\rangle
for some
c\in\pi_{1}(X)
.

Moreover, if
\deg(\lambda)\geqslant 2
, then only the first two situations occur.

The
rank

\rk(G)
of a group
G
is the smallest cardinality of a generating set for
G
. The
reduced rank
is then defined as:

\rr(G)=\min{\{0,\rk(G)-1\}}.

A much stronger, but less precise result on intersections of Magnus subgroups of one-relator groups was proven by Jaikin-Zapirain and the first author in
[
JZL23
]
.

Theorem 2.3.3
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group and
A\leqslant G
is a Magnus subgroup, then for any finitely generated subgroup
H\leqslant G
, we have

\sum_{HgA}\rr(A\cap H^{g})\leqslant\rr(H).

Dicks–Ventura introduced the notion of being inert in
[
DV96
]
: a subgroup
A\leqslant G
is
inert
if for all finitely generated subgroups
H\leqslant G
we have
\rk(A\cap H)\leqslant\rk(H)
.
Theorem

2.3.3
says that Magnus subgroups of one-relator groups are
strongly inert
.

The action on the Bass–Serre tree

Recall that if
G\cong H*_{\psi}
is a HNN-extension, where
\psi\colon A\to B
is the identifying isomorphism and
t\in G
is the stable letter, then the
Bass–Serre tree
associated with the splitting is the tree
T
with vertices and edges:

\displaystyle V(T)

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{gH\in G/H}gH

\displaystyle E(T)

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{gA\in G/A}gA

and adjacency given by:

\displaystyle o(gA)

\displaystyle=gH

\displaystyle t(gA)

\displaystyle=gtH.

A priori it is not obvious why this is a tree: the fundamental theorem of Bass–Serre theory states that it is, see Serre’s monograph
[
Ser03
]
for details. The Bass–Serre tree has a natural left
G
action with a single orbit of vertices and a single orbit of edges.

If
gH\in V(T)
is a vertex at distance
n
from the vertex
H\in V(T)
, then we have

gH=h_{1}t^{\epsilon_{1}}h_{1}\ldots h_{n}t^{\epsilon_{n}}H

for some sequence of elements
h_{i}\in H
and
\epsilon_{i}=\pm 1
.

If
g_{1}H,g_{2}H\in V(T)
, denote by
S_{g_{1}H,g_{2}H}\subset T
the unique geodesic segment connecting
g_{1}H
with
g_{2}H
. Then we have

\displaystyle\stab\left(S_{g_{1}H,g_{2}H}\right)

\displaystyle=\stab(g_{1}H)\cap\stab(g_{2}H)

\displaystyle=H^{g_{1}^{-1}}\cap H^{g_{2}^{-1}}

\displaystyle=(H\cap H^{g_{2}^{-1}g_{1}})^{g_{1}^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\stab\left(S_{H,g_{1}^{-1}g_{2}H}\right)^{g_{1}^{-1}}

where
\stab(-)
denotes the pointwise stabiliser. Note that the length of
S_{g_{1}H,g_{2}H}
is precisely the minimal number of
t
-letters which appear in the reduced form of
g_{1}^{-1}g_{2}
. Call this the
t
-length
. More precisely, the
t
-length of an element
g\in H*_{\psi}
, denoted by
|g|_{t}
, is the smallest number of
t^{\pm 1}
-letters that appear in a word over
H
and
t^{\pm 1}
that equals
g
in
H*_{\psi}
.

If
n\geqslant 0
is any integer, denote by

W_{n}=\{g\in G\mid|g|_{t}=n\}.

By definition, we have
W_{0}=H
. More generally, we have:

d(H,gH)=n\iff g\in W_{n}.

Using Collin’s results, in
[
Lin22a
]
the following result is shown.

Theorem 2.3.4
.

If
G\cong H*_{\psi}
is a one-relator splitting,
\psi\colon A\to B
is the identifying isomorphism,
t\in G
is the stable letter and
n\geqslant 1
is an integer, then

\sum_{g\in HW_{n}H}\rr(H\cap H^{g})\leqslant\rr(A)+\rr(B).

If
A\cap B
has no exceptional intersection, then for some
k\geqslant 1
, we have

\rr(H\cap H^{g})=0,

for all
g\in HW_{n}H,n\geqslant k
.

If
k\geqslant 0
is an integer, an action of a group
G
on a tree
T
is
k
-acylindrical
if for every segment
S\subset T
of length at least
k
, we have
\stab(S)=1
. Say the action is
acylindrical
if it is
k
-acylindrical for some
k
. The following theorem was proven by Wise for one-relator groups with torsion in
[
Wis21
]
. The full statement is one of the main theorems of
[
Lin22a
]
.

Theorem 2.3.5
.

Let
G
be a one-relator group and let
G\cong H*_{\psi}
be a one-relator splitting. If
G
has torsion or is 2-free, then

\sum_{HgH}\rk(H\cap H^{g})<\infty.

In particular, the action of
G
on the Bass–Serre tree is acylindrical.

Recall that a group
G
is
k
-free
if all
k
-generated subgroups of
G
are free. We will revisit 2-free one-relator groups in detail in
Section

2.5.3
and again in
Section

2.5.4
. Importantly, the property of being 2-free is, in a precise sense, generic and there is a straightforward algorithm which decides whether a one-relator group is 2-free. Both of these results can be found in
[
LW22
]
.

2.3.2.
Free co-abelian subgroups

A
co-abelian
subgroup of a group
G
is a normal subgroup
N\triangleleft G
such that
G/N
is torsion-free abelian. If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group, the aim of this section is to provide a simple criterion for when a co-abelian subgroup of
G
is a free group. In particular, if
G=H*_{\psi}
is a one-relator splitting, we have a natural epimorphism

G\to G/\langle\!\langle H\rangle\!\rangle=\mathbb{Z}

and we want to determine when the co-abelian subgroup
\langle\!\langle H\rangle\!\rangle
is a free group.

Let
X
be the presentation complex for
G
. Any co-abelian subgroup of
G
can be obtained as
\ker(\phi)
for some
\phi\in H^{1}(G,\mathbb{R})\cong H^{1}(X,\mathbb{R})
. Let
\phi\in H_{1}(X,\mathbb{R})
and let
Y\to X
be the induced abelian cover. Let
\mu\colon Y^{(1)}\to\mathbb{R}
be the map given by projection to
L_{\phi}
, as described in
Section

2.2.6
. Since
\ker(\phi)=\pi_{1}(Y)
, we want to understand the structure of
Y
. For this we describe two conditions that depend only on the homotopy type of
Z_{\phi}\subset Y
.

Recall that a subcomplex of a combinatorial complex is
full
if every cell whose attaching map is supported in the subcomplex, also lies in the subcomplex. If
J\subset\mathbb{R}
is a connected subset, denote by
Y_{J}
the full subcomplex of
Y
containing all vertices that map within
J\subset\mathbb{R}
via
\mu
. Let
a_{\min},a_{\max}\in\mathbb{R}
be the minimal and maximal values that 0-cells in
Z_{\phi}
map to. Consider the following two conditions:

(1)

(
\phi
is freely descending
) There is a 1-cell

e_{L}\subset Z_{\phi}-\left(Z_{\phi}\cap Y_{(a_{\min},a_{\max}]}\right)

such that,
Z_{\phi}
is homotopic to

Z_{\phi}^{(1)}-e_{L}

relative to
Z_{\phi}\cap Y_{(a_{\min},a_{\max}]}
.

(2)

(
\phi
is freely ascending
) There is a 1-cell

e_{U}\subset Z_{\phi}-\left(Z_{\phi}\cap Y_{[a_{\min},a_{\max})}\right)

such that,
Z_{\phi}
is homotopic to

Z_{\phi}^{(1)}-e_{U}

relative to
Z_{\phi}\cap Y_{[a_{\min},a_{\max})}
.

Remark 2.3.6
.

A homomorphism
\phi\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{R})
is freely descending if, for instance, some minimal edge in
Z_{\phi}
is a free edge in the sense that it is traversed precisely once by the attaching map of the 2-cell in
Z_{\phi}
. By symmetry, the same can be said about when
\phi
is freely descending with minimal replaced with maximal.

Sapir–Špakulová show in
[
Sv11
]
that a random one-relator group on three or more generators has some epimorphism
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}
such that
Z_{\phi}
has a topmost or bottommost edge that is free.

Theorem 2.3.7
.

Let
X
be a one-relator complex with a single 0-cell. If
\phi\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{R})
is freely descending and ascending then
\ker(\phi)
is a free group.

Example 2.3.8
.

Let us return to
2.2.27
. In this example, both the topmost
b
-edge and the bottommost
b
-edge in
Z_{\phi}
are traversed precisely once by the attaching map of the 2-cell. Hence, we may collapse the 2-cell through these free edges to obtain a homotopy equivalence as required by the definitions of
\phi
being freely descending and ascending. Hence, the homomorphism
\phi
from
2.2.27
is both freely descending and ascending so that
\ker(\phi)
is a free group by
Theorem

2.3.7
. In fact,
\ker(\phi)
is a finitely generated free group.

Example 2.3.9
.

For another type of example, consider the one-relator group

G=\langle a,b\mid a^{2}babca^{3}a^{2}bababc^{-1}\rangle.

Let
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}
be the epimorphism defined by sending
c
to
1
and
a,b
to
0
. See
Fig.

2.5
for the one-relator complex
Z_{\phi}
. We see that
Z_{\phi}
does not have any free faces and so we cannot conclude that
\ker(\phi)
is free as in
2.3.8
. However, since
a_{0}^{2}b_{0}a_{0}b_{0}
is a primitive element of
F(a_{0},b_{0})
(we can see this by applying the automorphism
a_{0}\to a_{0}b_{0}^{-2}
,
b_{0}\to a_{0}^{-1}b_{0}
), we see that
Z_{\phi}
is homotopy equivalent to
Z_{\phi}^{(1)}-a_{0}
relative to
Z_{\phi}^{(1)}-(v_{0}\cup a_{0}\cup b_{0}\cup c_{0})=Z_{\phi}\cap Y_{(0,1]}
. Hence,
\phi
is freely descending. Since
a_{1}^{2}b_{1}a_{1}b_{1}a_{1}b_{1}
is a primitive element of
F(a_{1},b_{1})
, we may also conclude that
\phi
is freely ascending. Thus,
\ker(\phi)
is free by
Theorem

2.3.7
.

Figure 2.5.
An example of a one-relator complex
Z_{\phi}
with
\phi
freely descending and ascending.

Proposition 2.3.10
.

Let
X
be a one-relator complex with a single 0-cell and suppose that
\phi\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{R})
factors through
\mathbb{Z}
and is freely descending.
Let
t
be the generator of
\deck(Y\to X)\cong\mathbb{Z}
that translates in the positive direction. Let
\Delta_{L}\subset Y^{(1)}
be the subgraph consisting of the vertex mapping to
a_{\min}
and all outgoing edges and their endpoints except for
e_{L}
. Then
Y
is homotopy equivalent to

\left(\bigcup_{i<0}t^{i}(\Delta_{L})\right)\cup Y_{[a_{\min},+\infty)}

relative to
Y_{[a_{\min},+\infty)}
.

Proof.

Since
\phi
factors through
\mathbb{Z}
, after multiplying by a suitable real number, we may assume that
\phi
maps surjectively to
\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{R}
. After possibly replacing
Z_{\phi}
with a translate, we may assume that
a_{\min}=0
. If
k<0
is an integer, denote by

Y_{k}=t^{k}(\Delta_{L})\cup Y_{[k+1,+\infty)}.

Since we have

t^{k}(Z_{\phi})\cap Y_{[k+1,+\infty)}=t^{k}\left(Z_{\phi}\cap Y_{[a_{\min}+1,a_{\max}]}\right)=t^{k}\left(Z_{\phi}\cap Y_{(a_{\min},a_{\max}]}\right)

and since
Y_{[k,+\infty)}-Y_{[k+1,+\infty)}
contains no 2-cell, it follows from the definition of
\phi
being freely descending that
Y_{[k,+\infty)}
is homotopy equivalent to
t^{k}(\Delta_{L})\cup Y_{[k+1,+\infty)}
relative to
Y_{[k+1,+\infty)}
. Hence, for each
k<0
there is a map
f_{k}\colon Y_{[k,+\infty)}\to Y_{k}
that is a homotopy equivalence relative to
Y_{[k+1,+\infty)}
. Now define a map

Y\to\left(\bigcup_{i<0}t^{i}(\Delta_{L})\right)\cup Y_{[a_{\min},+\infty)}

as the identity on
Y_{[a_{\min},+\infty)}
and as
f_{k}
when restricted to
Y_{[k,+\infty)}-Y_{[k+1,+\infty)}
for all
k<0
. It can be checked that this is a homotopy equivalence relative to
Y_{[a_{\min},+\infty)}
, using the same idea for the homotopy inverse of each
f_{k}
.
∎

A symmetric proof yields the ascending version of
Proposition

2.3.10
.

Proposition 2.3.11
.

Let
X
be a one-relator complex with a single 0-cell and suppose that
\phi\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{R})
factors through
\mathbb{Z}
and is freely ascending.
Let
t
be the generator of
\deck(Y\to X)\cong\mathbb{Z}
that translates in the positive direction. Let
\Delta_{U}\subset Y^{(1)}
be the subgraph consisting of the vertex mapping to
a_{\max}
and all outgoing edges and their endpoints except for
e_{U}
. Then
Y
is homotopy equivalent to

\left(\bigcup_{i>0}t^{i}(\Delta_{U})\right)\cup Y_{(-\infty,a_{\max}]}

relative to
Y_{(-\infty,a_{\max}]}
.

Proposition 2.3.12
.

Let
X
be a one-relator complex with a single 0-cell and suppose that
\phi\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{R})
factors through
\mathbb{Z}
and is freely ascending.
Let
t
be the generator of
\deck(Y\to X)\cong\mathbb{Z}
that translates in the positive direction. Let
\Delta_{L},\Delta_{U}
be the subgraphs from
Propositions

2.3.10
and
2.3.11
. Then there is a homotopy equivalence

Y\to\left(\bigcup_{i\leqslant 0}t^{i}(\Delta_{L})\right)\cup Y_{(a_{\min},a_{\max}]}\cup\left(\bigcup_{i>0}t^{i}(\Delta_{U})\right)

fixing
Y_{(a_{\min},a_{\max}]}
. In particular,
Y
is homotopy equivalent to a graph.

Proof.

Combining
Proposition

2.3.10
and
Proposition

2.3.11
, we see that
Y
is homotopy equivalent to

Y\to\left(\bigcup_{i<0}t^{i}(\Delta_{L})\right)\cup Y_{[a_{\min},a_{\max}]}\cup\left(\bigcup_{i>0}t^{i}(\Delta_{U})\right).

Since
Y_{[a_{\min},a_{\max}]}
is homotopy equivalent to
\Delta_{L}\cup Y_{(a_{\min},a_{\max}]}
, the result follows.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

2.3.7
.

The image of
\pi_{1}(X)
under
\phi
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
for some
n
. Since
Z_{\phi}
is finite, we may find an appropriate epimorphism
\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to\mathbb{Z}
such that, if
\phi^{\prime}
denotes the composition of
\phi
with this map, then there is an isomorphism
Z_{\phi}\to Z_{\phi^{\prime}}
respecting the order on the vertices. This may be done for instance by choosing a line
L\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
(where
m=\rk(\pi_{1}(X^{(1)}))
) with rational slope sufficiently close to the line associated with
\phi
. In particular,
\phi^{\prime}
is freely descending and ascending because
\phi
is and so
\ker(\phi^{\prime})
is free by
Proposition

2.3.12
. Since
\ker(\phi)\leqslant\ker(\phi^{\prime})
, we have that
\ker(\phi)
is free as claimed.
∎

Conjecture 2.3.13
.

If
X
is a one-relator complex with a single 0-cell and if
\phi\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{R})
, then
\ker(\phi)
is free if and only if
\phi
is freely ascending and descending.

In the next section we discuss Brown’s criterion which states that Conjecture
2.3.13
is true when
\chi(X)=0
. In other words, when
\pi_{1}(X)
is a two-generator one-relator group.

Propositions

2.3.10
and
2.3.11
actually imply the following weakening of
Theorem

2.3.7
. Recall that a HNN-extension
H*_{\psi}
is
ascending
if the domain of
\psi
is all of
H
,
descending
if the target of
\psi
is all of
H
.

Theorem 2.3.14
.

Let
X
be a one-relator complex with a single 0-cell and let
\phi\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{R})
. If
\phi
is freely descending or freely ascending, then
\ker(\phi)
is locally free. If
\phi
also factors through
\mathbb{Z}
, then
\pi_{1}(X)
splits as a descending or ascending HNN-extension of a free group
\pi_{1}(X)\cong F*_{\psi}
such that
\langle\!\langle F\rangle\!\rangle=\ker(\phi)
.

Remark 2.3.15
.

A finitely generated ascending HNN-extension of a free group embeds into an ascending HNN-extension of a finitely generated free group by work of Chong–Wise
[
CW24
]
. Since an ascending HNN-extension of a finitely generated free group is residually finite by work of Borisov–Sapir
[
BS05
]
,
Theorem

2.3.14
also yields a criterion for residual finiteness of a one-relator group.

We may also state a variation of Conjecture
2.3.13
.

Conjecture 2.3.16
.

If
X
is a one-relator complex with a single 0-cell and if
\phi\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{R})
, then
\ker(\phi)
is locally free if and only if
\phi
is freely ascending or descending.

Baumslag asked in
[
Bau74b
]
whether a (one-relator) group with locally free derived subgroup is residually finite. Answering Conjecture
2.3.16
would solve this problem by
Remark

2.3.15
.

2.3.3.
Algebraic fibring, Brown’s criterion and the Friedl–Tillmann polytope

An
algebraic fibring
of a group
G
is an epimorphism
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}
with
\ker(\phi)
finitely generated. One can think of this as an algebraic analog of a fibration of a manifold over a circle. Algebraic fibrations of a group
G
