are encoded in its BNS-invariant, see Bieri–Neumann–Strebel
[
BNS87
]
. We shall not define what the BNS-invariant of a group
G
is, but we remark that it is an open subset of the character sphere
\Sigma^{1}(G)\subset S(G)
and that
\ker(\phi)
is finitely generated if and only if
[\phi],-[\phi]\in\Sigma^{1}(G)
. The reader is directed to
[
Str13
]
for further details.

Brown computed the BNS-invariant for all one-relator groups in
[
Bro87
]
and thus characterised when a one-relator group admits an algebraic fibring. Brown’s theorem, known as
Brown’s criterion
, completely determines which homomorphisms
G\to\mathbb{Z}
of a one-relator group have finitely generated kernel in terms of the relator. Below we state Brown’s criterion in the language of
Section

2.2.6
.

Theorem 2.3.17

(Brown’s criterion)
.

Let
G=F(S)/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
be a one-relator group with
w
cyclically reduced, let
X
be its presentation complex and let
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}
be an epimorphism. Then:

(1)

[\phi]\in\Sigma^{1}(G)
if and only if
|S|=2
and a bottommost edge in
Z_{\phi}=(\Lambda_{\phi},\lambda_{\phi})
is traversed once by
\lambda_{\phi}
.

(2)

-[\phi]\in\Sigma^{1}(G)
if and only if
|S|=2
and a topmost edge in
Z_{\phi}=(\Lambda_{\phi},\lambda_{\phi})
is traversed once by
\lambda_{\phi}
.

In particular,
\ker(\phi)
is finitely generated if and only if
|S|=2
and a topmost and a bottommost edge in
Z_{\phi}
is traversed once by
\lambda_{\phi}
.

Remark 2.3.18
.

Dunfield–Thurston investigated algebraic fibring of one-relator groups from a probabilistic perspective in
[
DT06
]
using Brown’s criterion. Specifically they showed that the probability that a random two-generator one-relator group algebraically fibres lies somewhere between
0.0006
and
0.975
. They also remark that computer experiments indicate the probability may be limiting towards
0.94
.

As mentioned in
Section

2.3.2
, Brown’s criterion is a converse to
Theorem

2.3.7
for two-generator one-relator groups. Let us provide a sketch proof of the last statement of
Theorem

2.3.17
. This part of the statement was proved first by Moldavanskiĭ
[
Mol67
]
.

Sketch proof.

Let us first assume that a topmost and bottommost edge in
Z_{\phi}
is traversed once by
\lambda_{\phi}
, then by
Proposition

2.3.12
, we have

\ker(\phi)=\pi_{1}\left(\left(\bigcup_{i\leqslant 0}t^{i}(\Delta_{L})\right)\cup Y_{(a_{\min},a_{\max}]}\cup\left(\bigcup_{i>0}t^{i}(\Delta_{U})\right)\right)

For each integer
k\geqslant 1
, denote by

Y_{k}=\left(\bigcup_{-k\leqslant i\leqslant 0}t^{i}(\Delta_{L})\right)\cup Y_{(a_{\min},a_{\max}]}\cup\left(\bigcup_{0<i\leqslant k}t^{i}(\Delta_{U})\right)

By definition of
\Delta_{L}
and
\Delta_{U}
,
Y_{k+1}-Y_{k}
contains a single vertex and
|S|-1
many edges. Thus,
\chi(Y_{k+1})=\chi(Y_{k})-|S|+2
. This implies that
\ker(\phi)
is finitely generated if and only if
|S|=2
. Note that
|S|=1
cannot hold since otherwise there would be no epimorphism to
\mathbb{Z}
. We have established one direction of the claim.

Now suppose that
\ker(\phi)
is finitely generated. By
Theorem

2.2.14
, if
\rho\colon Y\to X
denotes the cover induced by
\phi
and
t\in\deck(\rho)
is a generator, there is a one-relator
\mathbb{Z}
-domain
Z\subset Y
such that
\pi_{1}(X)\cong\pi_{1}(Z)*_{\psi}
where
\psi
is induced by an isomorphism between two Magnus subgraphs
Z_{0},Z_{1}\subset Z
, where
Z_{0}=t^{-1}(Z)\cap Z
and
Z_{1}=Z\cap t(Z)
. Importantly, we have that
Z_{0},Z_{1}
both have one more vertex and
|S|
many more edges than
Z
. We have

(2.1)

\displaystyle\ker(\phi)=\pi_{1}(Y)=\ldots\underset{\pi_{1}(Z_{0})}{*}\pi_{1}(Z)\underset{\pi_{1}(t(Z_{0}))}{*}\pi_{1}(t(Z))\underset{\pi_{1}(t^{2}(Z_{0}))}{*}\ldots

Hence, if
\pi_{1}(Y)
is finitely generated, then for some
k\geqslant 0
we must have

\pi_{1}(Y)=\langle\pi_{1}(t^{-k}(Z)),\ldots,\pi_{1}(Z),\pi_{1}(t(Z)),\ldots,\pi_{1}(t^{k}(Z))\rangle.

By the amalgam decomposition (
2.1
), we have that
\pi_{1}(t^{k+1}(Z))\leqslant\pi_{1}(t^{k}(Z))
which implies that
\pi_{1}(t^{k+1}(Z))=\pi_{1}(t^{k+1}(Z_{0}))
. Arguing in the same way for
\pi_{1}(t^{-k-1}(Z))
, we see that
\pi_{1}(t^{-k-1}(Z))=\pi_{1}(t^{-k-1}(Z_{1}))
also. Hence, we have

(2.2)

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(Z)

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}(Z_{0})

(2.3)

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}(Z_{1})

We shall now need a lemma. It is the topological translation of
[
Mol67
, Lemma 1]
.

Lemma 2.3.19
.

Let
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
be a one-relator complex and let
M\subset X
be a Magnus subgraph. The inclusion
\pi_{1}(M)\hookrightarrow\pi_{1}(X)
is an isomorphism if and only if there is a 1-cell
e\subset\Lambda-M
traversed by
\lambda
precisely once and
\Lambda-e
deformation retracts onto
M
.

In order to complete the proof, we may use (
2.2
) and (
2.3
) combined with
Lemma

2.3.19
to conclude that there are 1-cells
e_{L}\subset Z-Z_{1}
and
e_{U}\subset Z-Z_{0}
that are both traversed precisely once by
\lambda_{\phi}
. By the first part of the proof this also implies that
|S|=2
and so the proof is complete.
∎

The Friedl–Tillmann polytope

In this section we describe the Friedl–Tillmann of a two-generator one-relator group, as introduced in
[
FT20
]
. This is a marked polytope in
H_{1}(G,\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}^{2}
which encodes the BNS-invariant of a one-relator group via its marking. It can be constructed directly from a presentation and it is (up to translation) independent of the choice of one-relator presentation.

Let us fix a free group
F=F(a,b)
and a cyclically reduced word
w
contained in the commutator subgroup
[F(a,b),F(a,b)]
. Recall from
Section

2.2.6
the definition of
\Lambda_{w}
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{w}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the convex hull of
\Lambda_{w}
. Mark all vertices in
\mathcal{C}_{w}
that are traversed precisely once by
\lambda_{w}
, the trace of
w
. The
Friedl–Tillmann polytope

\mathcal{M}_{w}\subset H_{1}(G,\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}^{2}
for
F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is then defined as the convex hull of the midpoints of the squares contained in
\mathcal{C}_{w}
. A vertex of the polytope
\mathcal{M}_{w}
is marked if all the vertices of the corresponding square in
\mathcal{C}_{w}
are marked. See
Fig.

2.6
for an illustration of the marked polytope
\mathcal{M}_{w}
for the one-relator group from
2.2.27
.

Figure 2.6.
Friedl–Tillmann’s marked polytope
\mathcal{M}_{w}
for the one-relator group with relator
w=a^{2}b^{3}ab^{-2}a^{-1}bab^{-1}a^{-2}bab^{-1}a^{-2}b^{-1}
. The marked vertices are in blue.

An element
\phi\in H^{1}(G,\mathbb{R})

pairs maximally
with a vertex
v
of
\mathcal{M}_{w}
if
\phi(v)>\phi(w)
for all other vertices
w\neq v
in
\mathcal{M}_{w}
. Friedl–Tillmann prove the following result in
[
FT20
]
, relating their polytope to the BNS-invariant of a one-relator group.

Theorem 2.3.20
.

Let
F=F(a,b)
, let
w\in[F,F]
be a cyclically reduced word and let
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
. Then
\phi\in H^{1}(G,\mathbb{R})
lies in the BNS-invariant
\Sigma^{1}(G)
if and only if
\phi
pairs maximally with a marked vertex of
\mathcal{M}_{w}
.

In the same article, Friedl–Tillmann conjecture that
\mathcal{P}_{w}
is an invariant of the group
F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
, up to translation. They prove that it is the case for a large class of one-relator groups, but their conjecture was only confirmed in full by Henneke–Kielak in
[
HK20
, Theorem 5.12]
.

Theorem 2.3.21
.

Let
F=F(a,b)
, let
w\in[F,F]
be a cyclically reduced word and let
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
. Then the marked polytope
\mathcal{M}_{w}
is an invariant of
G
, up to translation.

See
Section

2.3.4
for a connection between the thickness of the Friedl–Tillmann polytope of a one-relator group and its splitting complexity.

2.3.4.
Splitting complexity, JSJ-decompositions and amalgamated free products

If
G
is a group and
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}
is an epimorphism, a
splitting of
(G,\phi)
is an HNN-extension decomposition
G\cong H*_{\psi}
such that
\langle\!\langle H\rangle\!\rangle=\ker(\phi)
. Recall that if
\psi
identifies
A\leqslant H
with
B\leqslant H
, then
A
and
B
are the
associated groups
. The
splitting complexity
of
(G,\phi)
is

c(G,\phi)=\min{\{\rk(A)\mid(G,\phi)\text{ splits with associated group $A$}\}}.

The
free splitting complexity
of
(G,\phi)
is

c_{f}(G,\phi)=\min{\{\rk(A)\mid(G,\phi)\text{ splits with free associated group $A$}\}}.

If
G
is a finitely presented group (or more generally, of type
\fp_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
) and
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}
is an epimorphism, then Bieri–Strebel proved
[
BS78
, Theorem A]
that
c(G,\phi)<\infty
. Although
c_{f}(G,\phi)
in general could be infinite, when
G
is a finitely generated one-relator group it is finite for all
\phi
by
Theorem

2.2.14
.

In
[
FT20
]
, Friedl–Tillmann related the splitting complexity of many pairs
(F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle,\phi)
with the
thickness
of the polytope
\mathcal{M}_{w}
with respect to
\phi
:

\thick_{\phi}(\mathcal{M}_{w})=\max{\{\phi(p)-\phi(q)\mid p,q\in\mathcal{M}_{w}\}}

where here
\phi
is defined on
\mathcal{M}_{w}
via its natural extension to a homomorphism
H_{1}(G,\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
. Henneke–Kielak then generalised their results to all two-generator one-relator groups in
[
HK20
, Theorem 6.4]
.

Theorem 2.3.22
.

Let
F=F(a,b)
, let
w\in[F,F]
be a cyclically reduced word that is not a proper power and let
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
. For any epimorphism
\phi\colon G\to\mathbb{Z}
, we have

c(G,\phi)=c_{f}(G,\phi)=\thick_{\phi}(\mathcal{M}_{w})+1.

Remark 2.3.23
.

The upper bound
c_{f}(G,\phi)\leqslant\thick_{\phi}(\mathcal{M}_{w})
from
Theorem

2.3.22
is
[
FT20
, Proposition 7.3]
. The proof shows that there is a one-relator splitting for
(G,\phi)
with associated Magnus subgroups of rank precisely
\thick_{\phi}(\mathcal{M}_{w})+1
. This can also be proved directly using
Theorem

2.2.14
and the description of one-relator splittings from
Section

2.2.6
.

Question 2.3.24
.

Is there an analogue to
Theorem

2.3.22
valid for all one-relator groups?

Let
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
be a one-relator group as in
Theorem

2.3.22
. One immediate consequence of
Theorem

2.3.22
is that
G
splits as a HNN-extension over
\mathbb{Z}
if and only if
\thick_{\phi}(G)=0
for some
\phi
, or, equivalently, if and only if the polytope
\mathcal{M}_{w}
is a line. In light of
Remark

2.3.23
,
G
then splits as a HNN-extension over an infinite cyclic group if and only if
G
admits a one-relator splitting with associated Magnus subgroups isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
.

Gardam–Kielak–Logan applied this to two-generator one-relator hyperbolic groups in
[
GKL24
]
to compute the JSJ-decomposition of
G
. Logan was able to understand the case of two-generator one-relator groups with torsion in even more detail and worked out a complete characterisation of all outer automorphism groups in
[
Log16
]
. The reader is directed towards Guirardel–Levitt
[
GL17
]
for more information on JSJ-decompositions.

Problem 2.3.25
.

Characterise the JSJ-decompositions of one-relator groups.

Although a lot is known about HNN-extension decompositions, as evidenced above and by
Section

2.2
, not much is known about amalgamated free product decompositions of one-relator groups. Call an amalgamated free product
G=A*_{C}B

proper
if
A,B,C
are finitely generated,
C
is a proper subgroup of
A
and
B
, and
C
has index at least three in
A
or
B
. Wall asked the following question in
[
Wa779
, Question F2]
.

Question 2.3.26

(Wall)
.

Which one-relator groups split as proper amalgamated free products?

It is not too hard to show that a proper amalgamated free product contains a free subgroup of rank two. Thus,
\bs(1,n)
is an infinite family of one-relator groups that cannot admit any proper amalgamated free product decomposition. Nevertheless, many one-relator groups do andmit such decompositions. Baumslag–Shalen showed in
[
BS90
]
that every group
G
of deficiency at least two admits a
proper
amalgamated free product decomposition. That is,
G=A*_{C}B
with
A,B,C
finitely generated,
C
a proper subgroup of
A
and
B
and with
C
of index greater than two in
A
or
B
. In particular, this applies to one-relator groups generated by at least three elements. Such decompositions were also shown to hold for all non-cyclic one-relator groups with torsion by Benyash-Krivets
[
BK98
]
. Wall’s question does not yet have an answer for torsion-free two-generator one-relator groups.

Theorem 2.3.27
.

A one-relator group
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
splits as a proper amalgamated free product unless
\rk(F)=2
and
G
is torsion-free.

Beyond existence of amalgamated free product decompositions of one-relator groups, it would also be interesting to know what structure such amalgamated free products can have. Fine–Peluso proposed several conjectures in
[
FP99
]
which became known as the Amalgam Conjectures. We state the weakest possible such conjecture.

Conjecture 2.3.28

(The Amalgam Conjecture)
.

If
G
is a one-relator group with at least three generators, then
G\cong A*_{C}B
with
C
a free group and
A,B
either one-relator groups or free groups.

Note that in general, if
A*_{C}B
is finitely presented and
A,B,C
are finitely generated, it is possible that
A,B,C
are not finitely presented, see
[
BS90
, Section 6]
for an example. In case
G=A*_{C}B
is a one-relator group, we know that
A,B,C
are finitely presented since
G
is coherent, see
Section

2.5.4
.

2.4.
Group algebra properties

Recall that if
G
is a group and
R
is a ring, then the
group algebra

RG
(or
R[G]
) is the
R
-algebra with free
R
-basis in correspondence with the elements of
G
and with multiplication of basis elements given by the multiplication from
G
. In other words, it is the ring with underlying set

RG=\left\{\sum_{g\in G}r_{g}g\,\middle|\,r_{g}\in R\text{ and }r_{g}=0\text{ for all but finitely many $g\in G$}\right\}.

and ring operations defined in the natural way. In this section we will be studying the properties of group rings of one-relator groups.

2.4.1.
(Co)homology and the relation module

Let
F
be a free group and let
N\triangleleft F
be a normal subgroup. Then
F
acts by conjugation on the abelian group

N_{\ab}=N/[N,N].

This action extends linearly to a (left) action of
\mathbb{Z}F
on
N_{\ab}
. Since
N
acts trivially on its abelianisation, this action descends to an action of
\mathbb{Z}G
, making
N_{\ab}
a (left)
\mathbb{Z}G
-module called the
relation module
. Another way to view the relation module of a group presentation
G=F(S)/N
is as the
\mathbb{Z}G
-module of cycles
N_{\ab}=Z_{1}(\cay(G,S))
of the Cayley graph with respect to the generating set
S
. See
Fig.

2.7
.

Figure 2.7.
The Cayley graph for
G=F(a,b)/\langle\!\langle[a,b]\rangle\!\rangle
. The cycle
(3a^{-1}+b-ab^{-1})\cdot[a,b][N,N]
in
Z_{1}(\cay(G,\{a,b\})
is depicted in red.

Lyndon computed the relation module of a one-relator group in
[
Lyn50
]
, a theorem which became known as Lyndon’s identity theorem.

Theorem 2.4.1

(Lyndon’s Identity Theorem)
.

Let
G=F/\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle
be a one-relator group with
w
not a proper power and with
n\geqslant 1
. Denoting by
N=\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle
, we have

N_{\ab}\cong\mathbb{Z}G/(w-1).

In particular, if
n=1
then

N_{\ab}\cong\mathbb{Z}G.

The simplest way to prove
Theorem

2.4.1
is to show that
\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle\triangleleft F
has the
Cohen–Lyndon property
: there is a set of
\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle,\langle w\rangle
double coset representatives
T\subset F
such that
\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle\cong*_{t\in T}tw^{n}t^{-1}
. This was first done by Cohen and Lyndon in
[
CL63
]
.

The main use for
Theorem

2.4.1
is to compute the (co)homology of a one-relator group. Indeed, by
[
Bro94
, Proposition 5.4]
, if
G=F(S)/N
, we have an exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\ab}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{s\in S}\mathbb{Z}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

So in the case that
G=F/\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle
, with
w
not a proper power and
n=1
, the above gives us a free resolution of
\mathbb{Z}
as a trivial
\mathbb{Z}G
-module and we may compute homology by applying
-\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}G}M
or cohomology by applying
\hom_{\mathbb{Z}G}(-,M)
to the above. If
n>1
, then
N_{\ab}
is not free (or even projective), but we may extend the exact sequence to a free resolution:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{s\in S}\mathbb{Z}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w-1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}w^{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w-1}

We have not described three of the above maps. The remaining maps are:

z\mapsto z\cdot\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial s}(w^{n})\right)_{s\in S},\quad(z_{s})_{s\in S}\mapsto\sum_{s\in S}z_{s}\cdot(s-1),\quad z\mapsto\sum_{g\in G}z_{g}

where
\frac{\partial}{\partial s}
denotes the
Fox derivative
with respect to
s
.

Corollary 2.4.2
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group with
w
not a proper power, then
\cd_{\mathbb{Q}}(G)\leqslant 2
. If
n=1
, then
\cd_{\mathbb{Z}}(G)\leqslant 2
.

The fact that
\cd_{\mathbb{Q}}(G)\leqslant 2
when
G
has torsion follows by noting that after applying
-\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
to Lyndon’s resultion, the map
w-1
becomes the zero map and so we obtain a resolution

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{s\in S}\mathbb{Q}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

since
\mathbb{Q}
is a flat
\mathbb{Z}
-module.

Lyndon’s identity theorem allows us to compute the derived functors
\Tor^{RG}_{n}(-,R)
and
\Ext_{RG}^{n}(-,R)
when
G
a one-relator group. In order to compute

\Tor^{RG}_{n}(-,-),\quad\Ext_{RG}^{n}(-,-)

one needs to be able to produce resolutions of arbitrary
RG
-modules
M
, not just the trivial one
R
. Lewin–Lewin proved a ‘two sided simple identity theorem’ in
[
LL78
]
. As a corollary, they showed that if
G=F(S)/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a torsion-free one-relator group,
K
is a field and
M
is a (left)
KG
-module, then

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KG\otimes_{K}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{s\in S}KG\otimes_{K}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KG\otimes_{K}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

is a free resolution of
M
. In particular,
\Tor_{i}^{KG}(-,-)=\Ext^{i}_{KG}(-,-)=0
for all
i>2
. This resolution was used in
[
JZL23
]
to compute the weak dimension of certain
KG
-modules with
G
a torsion-free one-relator group.

Recall that a ring
R
has
(left) global dimension
at most
n
if
\Ext^{n+1}_{R}(A,B)=0
for all (left)
R
-modules
A
and
B
.
[
LL78
, Theorem 9]
states:

Corollary 2.4.3
.

Let
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
be a torsion-free one-relator group and let
R
be a ring. Then the (left) global dimension of
RG
is at most the (left) global dimension of
R
plus two. In particular, if
R=K
is a field, the global dimension of
KG
is at most two.

Note that if
R
is commutative, the opposite ring
RG^{\op}
is isomorphic to
RG
and so left and right global dimension of
RG
coincide.

Remark 2.4.4
.

Such a resolution can be constructed in a similar way for arbitrary groups, see
[
JZL23
, Proposition 2.2]
.

2.4.2.
Groups with cyclic relation module

A natural question that arises from
Theorem

2.4.1
is whether having a cyclic relation module is limited to one-relator groups.

Question 2.4.5

(Harlander)
.

If
G=F/N
is a group with relation module
N_{\ab}
cyclic, is
N
normally generated by a single element?

This question is a special case of the
relation gap problem
which asks whether a finitely presented group
G=F/N
can have strictly fewer relation module generators than
N
has normal generators. Bestvina–Brady showed in
[
BB97
]
that there exist groups with infinite relation gap. That is, groups that are not finitely presented but have type
\fp_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, a property equivalent to the relation module being finitely generated. Counterexamples to the relation gap problem could lead to counterexamples to old conjectures on the homotopy type of CW-complexes such as Wall’s
D(2)
conjecture. The following theorem from
[
Lin24b
]
makes it unlikely that counterexamples will come from groups with cyclic relation module.

Theorem 2.4.6
.

If
G=F/N
is a right orderable group with
N_{\ab}
a cyclic relation module, then
N
is normally generated by a single element. In particular,
G
is a one-relator group.

Recall that a locally indicable group is right orderable by the Burns–Hale theorem
[
BH72
]
and a right orderable group is one with a total order on its elements, invariant under multiplication from the right. A key step in the proof of
Theorem

2.4.6
involved showing that normal subgroups of one-relator groups which have right orderable quotients, must be normally generated by a single element. Indeed, if
G=F/N
and
N_{\ab}
is cyclic, then there is an element
w\in N
whose image in
N_{\ab}
generates the relation module and such that the map
F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle\to G
has perfect kernel. This is explained in detail in
[
Har15
]
.

Question 2.4.7
.

Are normal perfect subgroups of one-relator groups normally generated by a single element?

A positive answer to this question would solve Question
2.4.5
. Note that perfect subgroups of one-relator groups are necessarily infinitely generated. In the torsion-free case this is by local indicability, in the torsion case, the natural quotient map
F/\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle\to F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
onto the torsion-free one-relator group allows us to reduce to considering finitely generated subgroups in the kernel. But the kernel splits as a free product of (infinitely many) copies of
\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
, a group which contains no perfect subgroups. This last fact is due to Fisher–Karrass–Solitar
[
FKS72
, Theorem 1]
.

Another closely related problem is the
relation lifting problem
, asked by Wall in
[
Wal66
]
. If
G=F/N
is a presentation and
r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\in N_{\ab}
are
\mathbb{Z}G
-module generators, then the relation lifting problem asks whether there exist elements
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\in N
such that

\langle\!\langle w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\rangle\!\rangle=N,\quad w_{1}[N,N]=r_{1},\ldots,w_{n}[N,N]=r_{n}.

The relations
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
are called
lifts
of
r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}
. Using the fact that
\mathbb{Z}G
is a domain and contains no non-trivial units under the hypotheses of
Theorem

2.4.6
, one can show that every generator of
N_{\ab}
can be lifted. On the other hand, Dunwoody showed in
[
Dun72
]
that the following element

(1-a+a^{2})b\cdot a^{5}[N,N]

cannot be lifted to a normal generator of
\langle\!\langle a^{5}\rangle\!\rangle=N\triangleleft F(a,b)
. The proof uses the Magnus property for free groups,
Theorem

2.2.8
, to show that there is no lift and the fact that
(1-a+a^{2})b
is a non-trivial unit in
\mathbb{Z}G
to show that
(1-a+a^{2})b\cdot a^{5}[N,N]
is a generator.

Generalisations of both the relation lifting and relation gap problems can be considered for relation modules over arbitrary rings
R
. In this case, the
R
-relation module of
G=F/N
is the left
RG
-module

R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}N_{\ab}.

The main theorem of
[
Lin24b
]
applies to
R
-relation modules with
R
an arbitrary domain. In this slightly more general setting, it is no longer true that every generator
r\in R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}N_{\ab}
may be lifted to a normal generator of
N
. However, there will always be some element
w\in N
and a unit
u\in R^{\times}
such that
\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle=N
and
r=u\otimes w[N,N]
.

Conjecture 2.4.8
.

Let
G=F/N
be a torsion-free group such that
\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}N_{\ab}
is cyclic as a
\mathbb{Q}G
-module, then
N
is normally generated by a single element.

Remark 2.4.9
.

There exist groups with torsion which are not one-relator groups, but which have a presentation with cyclic
\mathbb{Q}
-relation module. Such an example is given in
[
Lin24b
]
.

2.4.3.
L^{2}
-Betti numbers and the strong Atiyah conjecture for one-relator groups

Let
G
be a group and denote by
\ell^{2}(G)
the space of square summable functions
G\to\mathbb{C}
. In other words,
\ell^{2}(G)
is the space of formal sums

\ell^{2}(G)=\left\{\sum_{g\in G}c_{g}\cdot g\,\Bigm|\,c_{g}\in\mathbb{C},\sum_{g\in G}\|c_{g}\|<\infty\right\}.

with square summable support. The
group von Neumann algebra
is the algebra of bounded
G
-equivariant operators from
\ell^{2}(G)
to itself

\mathcal{N}(G):=\mathcal{B}(\ell^{2}(G))^{G}.

The ring of affiliated operators
\mathcal{U}(G)
is the Ore localisation of
\mathcal{N}(G)
with respect to the set of non-zero divisors. Then one way to define the
n^{\text{th}}

L^{2}
-Betti number
of
G
is:

b_{n}^{(2)}=\dim_{\mathcal{U}(G)}\Tor^{\mathbb{Z}G}_{n}(\mathcal{U}(G),\mathbb{Z})

where
\dim_{\mathcal{U}(G)}
is the von Neumann dimension, see Lück’s book
[
Lüc02
, Definition 8.30]
for details. The
L^{2}
-Betti numbers of one-relator groups were computed by Dicks–Linnell
[
DL07
]
.

Theorem 2.4.10
.

Let
G=F/\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle
be a one-relator group with
w
not a proper power and
n\geqslant 1
. Then

\displaystyle b_{0}^{(2)}(G)=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n}&\text{ if $G\cong\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$}\\
0&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle b_{1}^{(2)}(G)=

\displaystyle\,1-\rk(F)+\frac{1}{n}

\displaystyle b_{2}^{(2)}(G)=

\displaystyle\,0

The strong Atiyah conjecture predicts that the von Neumann dimension of the kernel of operators
\ell^{2}(G)^{n}\to\ell^{2}(G)^{m}
induced by right multiplication by
m\times n
matrices over
\mathbb{C}G
should lie in
\frac{1}{\lcm(G)}\mathbb{Z}
, where
\lcm(G)
denotes the lowest common multiple of the orders of finite subgroups of
G
. Another equivalent formulation of the strong Atiyah conjecture can be stated in terms of
L^{2}
-Betti numbers, see
[
Lüc02
, Chapter 10]
. For torsion-free groups, the strong Atiyah conjecture over a subfield
K\subset\mathbb{C}
is equivalent to the division closure of
KG
in
\mathcal{U}(G)
being a division ring. See
[
LS12
, Proposition 1.2]
for this fact.

Jaikin-Zapirain–López-Álvarez confirmed the strong Atiyah conjecture for one-relator groups in
[
JZLA20
]
. In fact, they prove something much stronger.

Theorem 2.4.11
.

If is a
G
is a locally indicable group, then
G
satisfies the strong Atiyah conjecture over any subfield
K\subset\mathbb{C}
.

Combining
Theorem

2.4.11
with a result of Kevin Schreve
[
Sch14
]
, Jaikin-Zapirain–López-Álvarez obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 2.4.12
.

If
G
is a one-relator group, then
G
satisfies the strong Atiyah conjecture.

2.4.4.
Embedding the group ring of a torsion-free one-relator group into a division ring

Recall that a
division ring
is a ring in which each non-zero element is a unit. Lewin–Lewin proved in
[
LL78
]
that the group ring of a torsion-free one-relator group embeds into a division ring.

Theorem 2.4.13
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a torsion-free one-relator group and
k
is a division ring, then
kG\hookrightarrow\Di
for some division ring
\Di
.

Theorem

2.4.13
provided the first proof of Kaplansky’s zero divisor conjecture for torsion-free one-relator groups. Kaplansky’s zero divisor conjecture is also a consequence of local indicability, proved later by Brodskii and Howie.

The proof of
Theorem

2.4.13
uses the Magnus hierarchy and various ring constructions to explicitly and inductively construct the division ring
\Di
. These constructions include Ore localisation, skew polynomial ring constructions, Cohn’s universal matrix inverting construction and coproducts of rings. The background and details on these notions are covered in detail in Cohn’s book
[
Coh06
]
.

One of the main difficulties in Lewin–Lewin’s construction was ensuring that at each stage in the hierarchy, the division rings were
(H,G)
-free for all Magnus subgroups
H
of the one-relator group
G
. Let
k
be a division ring,
G
a group and
kG\hookrightarrow\Di
an embedding into a division ring. If
N\leqslant H\leqslant G
are subgroups and
\Di_{N}\leqslant\Di
denotes the division closure of
kN
in
\Di
, then
\Di
is
(N,H)
-free
if the multiplication map

\Di_{N}\otimes_{kN}kH\to\Di

given by
d\otimes h\mapsto dh
is injective. Note that if
T\subset H
is a right transversal for
N
, then we have
\Di_{N}\otimes_{kN}kH\cong\bigoplus_{t\in T}\Di_{N}\otimes t
as a left
kN
-module.

There is another way to embed a group ring of a torsion-free one-relator group into a division ring which we shall now explain. Firstly, we define two types of division ring embeddings which play a particularly important role. Let
kG\hookrightarrow\Di
be an epic division ring embedding. Recall that
kG\hookrightarrow\Di
is epic of
\Di
coincides with the division closure of
kG
. The embedding is
Hughes-free
if it is
(N,H)
-free for every finitely generated subgroup
H\leqslant G
and every normal subgroup
N\triangleleft H
with
H/N\cong\mathbb{Z}
. A well-known theorem of Hughes
[
Hug70
]
states that a locally indicable group admits at most one Hughes-free embedding into a division ring, up to isomorphism. The embedding is
Linnell
if it is
(N,G)
-free for any subgroup
N\leqslant G
. When the division closure of
\mathbb{C}G
in the ring of affiliated operators
\mathcal{U}(G)
is a division ring, this division ring embedding is Linnell. Furthermore, if
G
is locally indicable, then by Hughes’ theorem, its Linnell division ring in
\mathcal{U}(G)
will also be Hughes-free. In particular, the division ring embedding one obtains for
KG
, with
G
a torsion-free one-relator group and
K\subset\mathbb{C}
a subfield, from
Theorem

2.4.11
is Linnell.

Let
G
be a right orderable group, let
\leqslant
be a right
G
-invariant total order and let
k
be a division ring. Denote by

k(\!(G,\leq)\!)=\left\{r=\sum_{g\in G}r_{g}\cdot g\Bigm|\supp(r)\text{ is well-ordered}\right\}

the
space of Mal’cev–Neumann series
. A powerful theorem of Malcev and Neumann
[
Mal48
,
Neu49
]
shows that if
\leq
is a bi-ordering of
G
, then
k(\!(G,\leq)\!)
actually has the structure of a division ring called the Mal’cev–Neumann completion. In particular, if
G
is a free group, then it admits a bi-ordering and so it embeds in its Mal’cev–Nuemann completion. This is in fact the base case of the Lewin–Lewin division ring construction. Lewin showed in
[
Lew74
]
that in this case the embedding of
kG
into its division closure in its Mal’cev Neumann completion is Hughes-free (in fact, also Linnell).

When
\leq
is not a bi-ordering,
k(\!(G,\leq)\!)
does not have a natural ring structure. In this case one should look at the
endomorphism ring
of
k(\!(G,\leq)\!)
, denoted by
\End(k(\!(G,\leq)\!))
. The group ring
kG
acts naturally on the right, and so
kG
embeds in
\End(k(\!(G,\leq)\!))
. It turns out that the endomorphism ring
\End(k(\!(G,\leq)\!))
is a division ring in the case that
G
is locally indicable and
\leq
is a
Conradian
right order. Note that admitting a Conradian right order is equivalent to being locally indicable, a fact which is a consequence of a result of Brodskii
[
Bro84
]
, explained in
[
RR02
]
. The following is obtained by combining a result of Jaikin-Zapirain–López-Álvarez
[
JZLA20
, Corollary 1.4]
with a result of Gräter
[
Grä20a
, Theorem 8.1 & Corollary 8.3]
. See also
[
JZL23
, Theorem 2.5]
and the preceding discussion.

Theorem 2.4.14
.

Let
G
be a locally indicable group,
\leq
a Conradian right order on
G
and let
K
be a field of characteristic zero. Then the ring
\End(K(\!(G,\leq)\!))
is a division ring. Moreover, the division closure of
KG
in
\End(K(\!(G,\leq)\!))
is a Linnell ring.

Corollary 2.4.15
.

If
G
is a torsion-free one-relator group and
K
is a field of characteristic zero, then
KG
embeds into a Linnell division ring
\Di_{KG}
. Moreover,
\Di_{KG}
is the unique epic Linnell division
KG
-ring.

By a result of Fisher–Sánchez-Peralta
[
FSP24
]
, the Lewin–Lewin division ring construction coincides with the division ring from
Theorem

2.4.14
for
KG
where
K
is a field of characteristic zero and
G
a torsion-free one-relator group.

2.4.5.
Coherence of the group ring

Recall that a presentation of a (left)
R
-module
M
is an exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

where
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
are free (left)
R
-modules.

Definition 2.4.16
.

A ring
R
is
(left) coherent
if all of its finitely generated (left) ideals are finitely presented.

Coherence of rings was introduced by Bourbaki in the 1960’s. In general, it is important to distinguish between left and right modules as there are examples of rings which are left coherent but not right coherent. See, for example,
[
La772
, Example (e), p. 139]
. However, for group rings over a commutative ring, the two notions coincide. One of the main results in
[
JZL23
]
is the following.

Theorem 2.4.17
.

If
G
is a one-relator group and
K
is a field of characteristic
0
, then
K[G]
is coherent.

A right
R
-module
M
is said to have
weak dimension at most
k
if
\Tor_{k+1}^{R}(M,N)=0
for all left
R
-modules
N
. Say
M
has weak dimension
k
if it has weak dimension at most
k
, but not at most
k-1
. The proof of
Theorem

2.4.17
makes use of the following criterion for when a finitely generated
R
-module is finitely presented. See
[
JZL23
, Proposition 3.1]
.

Proposition 2.4.18
.

Let
R
be a ring of global dimension at most two and let
R\hookrightarrow\Di
be an embedding into a division ring. If
M
is a finitely generated right
R
-module, then

M\text{ is finitely presented}\iff\dim_{\Di}\Tor_{1}^{R}(M,\Di)<\infty.

In particular, if
\Di
has weak dimension at most one, then
R
is coherent.

To see that the last claim in
Proposition

2.4.18
follows from the first, note that if
I\leqslant R
is an ideal, then

\Tor_{1}^{R}(I,\Di)=\Tor_{2}^{R}(R/I,\Di)

and so if
\Di
has weak dimension at most one, then
\Tor_{1}^{R}(I,\Di)=0
.

The idea to prove
Theorem

2.4.17
is to use
Proposition

2.4.18
on the group ring of a one-relator group. There are two cases to consider: when
G
is torsion-free and when
G
has torsion.

Suppose that
G
is a torsion-free one-relator group and
K
is a field. As we saw in
Section

2.4.1
,
K[G]
has global dimension at most two and so it suffices to show that
\Di
has weak dimension at most one. Let
M
be a left
K[G]
-module. From
Section

2.4.1
we have the following resolution

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KG\otimes_{K}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{s\in S}KG\otimes_{K}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KG\otimes_{K}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

which comes from the standard resolution of
K
as a trivial
K[G]
-module. By
Theorem

2.4.14
, there exists a division ring embedding
K[G]\hookrightarrow\Di
, turning
\Di
into a right
K[G]
-module. Applying
\Di\otimes_{K[G]}-
we obtain a complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Di\otimes_{K}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{s\in S}\Di\otimes_{K}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Di\otimes_{K}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}

By definition:

\Tor_{2}^{K[G]}(\Di,M)=\ker(d_{2}).

The delicate part of the proof of
Theorem

2.4.17
is now to show that
\Di\otimes_{K}M
is a torsion-free
K[G]
module, and show that this implies that
\ker(d_{2})=0
, see
[
JZL23
, Lemma 5.1 & Theorem 5.3]
. In order to carry out this step, one needs to know that the division ring
\Di
into which
K[G]
embeds is Hughes-free. Since such a division ring is only known to exist when
K
has characteristic 0 by
Theorem

2.4.14
,
Theorem

2.4.17
requires this hypothesis. However, the proof carries over directly if one were to show that a Hughes-free division ring exists for
K[G]
with
G
a torsion-free one-relator group and
K
an arbitrary field.

If
G
is a one-relator group with torsion, the ring
K[G]
does not have global dimension at most two. Dawid Kielak and the first author showed in
[
KL24
]
that one-relator groups with torsion have a finite index subgroup that is free-by-cyclic, see
Section

2.5.5
. Let
F\rtimes_{\psi}\mathbb{Z}
be the finite index free-by-cyclic subgroup of
G
. Now
K[F]
embeds into its Mal’cev–Neumann completion, giving us a division ring embedding
K[F]\hookrightarrow\Di
. Since
K[F\rtimes_{\psi}\mathbb{Z}]\cong(K[F])[t^{\pm 1},\psi]
, localising
\Di[t^{\pm 1},\overline{\psi}]
, where
\overline{\psi}
denotes the extension of
\psi\colon K[F]\to K[F]
to
\Di
, yields a division ring embedding
K[F\rtimes_{\psi}\mathbb{Z}]\hookrightarrow\Ore\left(\Di\left[t^{\pm 1},\overline{\psi}\right]\right)
. Then general properties of the twisted Laurent polynomial ring and Ore localisation implies that
\Ore\left(\Di\left[t^{\pm 1},\overline{\psi}\right]\right)
has weak dimension one and so
Proposition

2.4.18
can also be applied in this case. Since the coherence property passes between finite codimensional ideals, the group ring
K[G]
is also coherent.

We conclude this section by remarking that the proof of group ring coherence does not carry over to
R[G]
when
R
is not a field as the global dimension of
R[G]
is no longer at most two. Even the case
R=\mathbb{Z}
is open and interesting.

2.5.
Subgroup properties

2.5.1.
Stackings and non-positive immersions

Dani Wise introduced the notions of non-positive immersions and negative immersions in
[
Wis03
,
Wis04
]
as tools to understand better the subgroup structure of certain groups with an eye towards solving Baumslag’s conjecture on the coherence of one-relator groups.

The independent proofs that torsion-free one-relator groups have presentation complexes with non-positive immersions by Helfer–Wise
[
HW16
]
and Louder–Wilton
[
LW17
]
, led to a sequence of beautiful developments in understanding the subgroups of one-relator groups which we shall explain in the next few sections.

Definition 2.5.1
.

A 2-complex
X
has
non-positive immersions
if for every immersion
Y\looparrowright X
with
Y
compact and connected, either
\pi_{1}(Y)=1
or
\chi(Y)\leqslant 0
.

Remark 2.5.2
.

Other variations of the non-positive immersions property replace the condition in
Theorem

2.5.5
that
Y
be simply connected with the condition that
Y
be contractible or collapsible. Yet another variation,
weak non-positive immersions
, requires that
\chi(Y)\leqslant 1
for all immersions
Y\looparrowright X
with
Y
compact and connected.

When Dani Wise introduced the notion of non-positive immersions in
[
Wis03
]
, he conjectured that all one-relator complexes
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
with
\deg(\lambda)=1
have non-positive immersions. Wise had reduced his conjecture to a conjecture about free groups which he called the
w
-cycles conjecture: if
F
is a free group,
w\in F
is not a proper power and
H\leqslant F
is any finitely generated free group, then

\sum_{WfH}\rk(W^{f}\cap H)\leqslant\rk(H)

with equality only if
H\leqslant\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
. This can also be interpreted as a rank 1 version of the (strengthened) Hanna Neumann inequality. In
[
LW17
]
Louder–Wilton solved the
w
-cycles conjecture. Their proof uses the idea of a stacking.

Definition 2.5.3
.

Let
\lambda\colon\mathbb{S}\looparrowright\Gamma
be an immersion of a disjoint union of copies of
S^{1}
into a graph
\Gamma
. A
stacking
of
\lambda
is an embedding
\widetilde{\lambda}\colon\mathbb{S}\hookrightarrow\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}
such that
\pi\circ\widetilde{\lambda}=\lambda
where
\pi\colon\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}\to\Gamma
is the projection map.

Now fix an immersion
\lambda
as in
Definition

2.5.3
and a stacking
\widetilde{\lambda}
. Denote by
\iota\colon\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
the projection to
\mathbb{R}
. Define the
top
of the stacking to be

\mathcal{T}=\{x\in\mathbb{S}\mid\forall y\neq x\quad(\lambda(x)=\lambda(y)\implies\iota(\widetilde{\lambda}(x))>\iota(\widetilde{\lambda}(y)))\}\subset\mathbb{S}

whereas the
bottom
is the subset

\mathcal{B}=\{x\in\mathbb{S}\mid\forall y\neq x\quad(\lambda(x)=\lambda(y)\implies\iota(\widetilde{\lambda}(x))<\iota(\widetilde{\lambda}(y)))\}\subset\mathbb{S}

The stacking is
good
if the top and the bottom intersect each component of
\mathbb{S}
non-trivially. Louder–Wilton then show that good stackings always exist for immersions
\lambda\colon S^{1}\looparrowright\Lambda
, where
\deg(\lambda)=1
. Their argument makes use of the existence of maximal
\mathbb{Z}
-tower liftings of
\lambda
, a fact due to Howie
[
How82
]
. The topological one-relator hierarchy described in
Section

2.2.2
yields, in fact, an explicit maximal tower lifting of
\lambda
. Alternatively, as Wise points out in
[
Wis20
]
, one can use a result of Farrell
[
Far76
]
, the right orderability of torsion-free one-relator groups and Weinbaum’s theorem
[
Wei72
]
.

A key insight of Louder–Wilton is that good stackings can be used to compute Euler characteristics. This led to the following result, whch is a combination of
[
LW17
, Lemma 12 & 16]
. Recall that the
core
of a graph
\Gamma
, denoted by
\core(\Gamma)
, is the union of the images of all immersed cycles
S^{1}\looparrowright\Gamma
.

Proposition 2.5.4
.

Let
\Lambda
be a graph and let
\lambda\colon\mathbb{S}\looparrowright\Lambda
be an immersion of a disjoint union of cycles
S^{1}
. Suppose that
\lambda
admits a good stacking (for example, if
\mathbb{S}=S^{1}
and
\deg(\lambda)=1
). Let
\Lambda^{\prime}\looparrowright\Lambda
be a graph immersion and consider the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\core(\mathbb{S}\times_{\Lambda}\Lambda^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

arising from the pullback diagram. Then either there is some edge
e\subset\Lambda^{\prime}
such that
\lambda^{\prime-1}(e)
is a single edge, or we have

-\chi(\im(\lambda^{\prime}))\geqslant\deg(\sigma).

The
w
-cycles conjecture follows immediately from
Proposition

2.5.4
. We explain in some detail how to obtain the non-positive immersions property from
Proposition

2.5.4
.

Theorem 2.5.5
.

If
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
is a one-relator complex with
\deg(\lambda)=1
, then
X
has non-positive immersions.

Proof.

Let
Z=(\Gamma,\gamma)
be a 2-complex and let
Z\looparrowright X
be an immersion. If there is a 1-cell
e\subset\Gamma
such that
\gamma^{-1}(e)
contains a single 1-cell, then by removing
e
and the 2-cell whose boundary cycle traverses
e
, we obtain a subcomplex of
Z
which is homotopy equivalent to
Z
. Thus, we may assume that no such
1
-cell exists. Consider the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{1}\times_{\Lambda}\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
\looparrowleft
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\looparrowleft
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\looparrowleft
\looparrowleft
\looparrowleft
\looparrowleft
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

where
\gamma\colon\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}\looparrowright\Gamma
is the attaching map for the 2-cells in
Z
and where the fact that
\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}\hookrightarrow S^{1}\times_{\Lambda}\Gamma
is injective follows from the fact that
Z\looparrowright X
is an immersion. We may apply
Proposition

2.5.4
to the immersion
\Gamma\looparrowright\Lambda
to conclude that

-\chi(\Gamma)\geqslant-\chi(\gamma(\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma}))\geqslant\deg(\sigma)=|\pi_{0}(\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma})|.

Since

\chi(Z)=\chi(\Gamma)-|\pi_{0}(\mathbb{S}_{\Gamma})|

the proof is complete.
∎

Remark 2.5.6
.

The proof actually shows something stronger: if
Z\looparrowright X
is an immersion of a compact and connected 2-complex, then either
\chi(Z)\leqslant 0
or
Z
collapses through free faces to a point (collapsing non-positive immersions).

Other proofs of
Theorem

2.5.5
are also worth mentioning. Helfer–Wise prove
Theorem

2.5.5
at the same time as Louder–Wilton in
[
HW16
]
, using the notion of
(bi)-slim
structures. In
[
BCGW24
]
Bamberger–Carrier–Gaster–Wise showed that the existence of a good stacking for an immersion
\lambda\colon\mathbb{S}\looparrowright\Lambda
is equivalent to the existence of a bi-slim structure on
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
. Since the existence of a slim structure is strictly more general, the proof in
[
HW16
]
applies to a somewhat larger class of 2-complexes. For example, they show that
reducible
2-complexes without proper powers (in the sense of Howie
[
How82
]
) have slim structures and so have non-positive immersions. A third proof was provided by Howie–Short in
[
HS23
]
using orderability and diagrams. This last proof could be applied in a more general ‘relative’ setting. A similar result was independently obtained by Millard in his thesis
[
Mil21
]
using ‘relative’ stackings. Finally, a completely different proof was given in
[
JZL23
]
, using homological algebra.

Not many general properties of 2-complexes with non-positive immersions are known. Wise conjectured in
[
Wis03
]
that if
X
has non-positive immersions, then
\pi_{1}(X)
is coherent. That is, every finitely generated subgroup of
\pi_{1}(X)
is finitely presented. Although this conjecture remains open, in
Section

2.5.4
we will see that significant progress has been made. Properties that are known to hold for all 2-complexes with non-positive immersions are the following, due to Wise
[
Wis22
]
(see
[
JZL23
, Proposition 2.7]
).

Theorem 2.5.7
.

If
X
is a 2-complex with non-positive immersions and
\pi_{1}(X)\neq 1
, then:

•

\pi_{1}(X)
is locally indicable.

•

X
is aspherical.

Note that these properties were known to hold for (presentation complexes of) torsion-free one-relator groups, see
Section

2.2.2
.

2.5.2.
(Uniform) Negative immersions

In
[
Wis04
]
, Dani Wise introduced a strengthening of non-positive immersions which he called negative immersions. After Louder–Wilton introduced stackings in
[
LW17
]
, they then wrote
[
LW22
]
in which they used stackings to characterise precisely when a one-relator complex has negative immersions. However, the definition Louder–Wilton used was slightly different, although morally very similar, to that of Wise. The advantage of the Louder–Wilton definiton is that it is invariant under Nielsen equivalence. Since we shall be covering the results of Louder–Wilton, we shall be working with their definition here.

Before stating the definition of negative immersions, we shall need to explain what it means for a 2-complex to be reducible or irreducible. For this we follow
[
LW24
]
. There the notion of
branched immersions
is also used. However, we stick to regular immersions.

Let
X
be a 2-complex. We say
X
is
visibly reducible
if one of the following holds:

(1)

X
contains a vertex of degree at most one.

(2)

X
contains a locally separating vertex.

(3)

X
contains a free face.

Morally,
X
is visibly reducible if there is an obvious way to simplfy it: remove hanging trees (corresponding to (
1
)), decompose
X
as a wedge of two subcomplexes (corresponding to (
2
)) or collapse a 2-cell through an edge (corresponding to (
3
)). A combinatorial map of 2-complexes
\phi\colon Y\to X
is an
essential equivalence
if the following two conditions hold:

(1)

\phi^{(1)}\colon Y^{(1)}\to X^{(1)}
is a homotopy equivalence that factors as a sequence of folds (in the sense of Stallings
[
Sta83
]
)

(2)

\phi
is a homeomorphism on the interiors of 2-cells.

Definition 2.5.8
.

A 2-complex
X
is
reducible
if there exists an essential equivalence
Y\to X
such that
Y
is visibly reducible,
irreducible
otherwise.

Definition 2.5.9
.

A 2-complex
X
has
negative immersions
if for every immersion
Y\looparrowright X
with
Y
compact and connected, either
\chi(Y)<0
or
Y
is reducible.

One of the main theorems of
[
LW22
]
characterises when a one-relator complex has negative immersions in terms of the subgroup structure of the fundamental group. Recall that a group is
k
-free
if every
k
-generated subgroup is free.

Theorem 2.5.10
.

If
X
is a one-relator complex, then
X
has negative immersions if and only if
\pi_{1}(X)
is 2-free.

Louder–Wilton later introduced a stronger uniform variant of negative immersions in
[
LW24
]
.

Definition 2.5.11
.

A 2-complex
X
has
uniform negative immersions
if there exists some
\epsilon>0
such that for every immersion
Y\looparrowright X
with
Y
compact, connected and irreducible, we have

\frac{\chi(Y)}{\#\{\text{2-cells in $Y$}\}}\leqslant-\epsilon.

Remark 2.5.12
.

Wise’s definition of negative immersions is more akin to
Definition

2.5.11
, except that reducible is replaced with ‘no free faces and not a point’. In
[
LW24
, Section 3.4]
it is shown that these two notions are distinct even for one-relator complexes.

The main advantage of the uniform negative immersions property over the negative immersions property is that it allows for much stronger results about subgroups. This was exploited in
[
LW24
]
in which Louder–Wilton showed that 2-free one-relator groups are coherent. We will cover this result and other further corollaries in
Section

2.5.4
. The following is
[
LW24
, Theorem C]
.

Theorem 2.5.13
.

If
X
is a one-relator complex with negative immersions, then
X
has uniform negative immersions.

The proof of
Theorem

2.5.13
is a beautiful application of linear programming techniques. The authors encode certain types of maps from 2-complexes to a 2-complex
X
in a linear system of equations and inequalities, showing that the maps which maximise “curvature” are rational vertices in a convex polytope. Since these rational vertices can be realised by some map
Y_{\max}\to X
, the “curvature” of
Y_{\max}
provides the
\epsilon
needed to verify that
X
has uniform negative immersions. The reader is invited to consult
[
Wil24a
]
in which Wilton formally defines these curvature invariants for all 2-complexes, explores their properties and proposes several interesting directions for further research.

So far, we have only considered one-relator complexes
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
with
\deg(\lambda)=1
and so the reader might be wondering what can one say about the case
\deg(\lambda)>1
. In
[
LW17
]
, Louder–Wilton show that such one-relator complexes have the property of
not-too-positive immersions
. Stronger properties were obtained in
[
LW20
]
and
[
LW24
]
. We state
[
LW24
, Theorem 5.1]
below which combines
Proposition

2.5.4
with a result of Fisher–Karrass–Solitar
[
FKS72
]
stating that a one-relator group with torsion is virtually torsion-free. A similar result can be found in
[
Wis22
]
(see also
[
Wis20
]
).

Theorem 2.5.14
.

If
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
is a one-relator complex with
\deg(\lambda)>1
, then there is a finite sheeted cover
Y\looparrowright X
and an inclusion
\iota\colon Z\hookrightarrow Y
inducing an isomorphism on
\pi_{1}
such that
Z
has uniform negative immersions.

In order to get around passing to a finite index subgroup, a one-relator group with torsion can be considered as the fundamental group of a one-relator orbicomplex with a single non-trivial cone point in the centre of the 2-cell. Louder–Wilton show in
[
LW20
]
that such complexes have uniform negative immersions.

2.5.3.
Primitivity rank and
w
-subgroups

Recall that an element
w
in a free group
F
is
primitive
if it is part of a free basis for
F
. Equivalently, if
F\cong F^{\prime}*\langle w\rangle
for some
F^{\prime}\leqslant F
. Call
w

imprimitive
if
w
is not primitive.

Primitivity rank was introduced by Puder in
[
Pud14
]
. The definition plays a key role in Louder–Wilton’s results in
[
LW22
]
.

Definition 2.5.15
.

If
F
is a free group and
w\in F
is an element, its
primitivity rank

\pi(w)
is the following quantity:

\pi(w)=\min{\{\rk(H)\mid w\in H\leqslant F,\,\text{$w$ is imprimitive in $H$}\}}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{+\infty\},

where
\pi(1)=0
and
\pi(w)=+\infty
if
w
is primitive.

The first connection Louder–Wilton discovered between properties of a one-relator group and the primitivity rank of its defining relation is the following.

Theorem 2.5.16
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group, then
G
is
k
-free if and only if
k<\pi(w)
. In particular:

(1)

G
is torsion-free if and only if
\pi(w)\geqslant 2
.

(2)

The one-relator presentation complex for
G
has uniform negative immersions if and only if
\pi(w)\geqslant 3
.

Several
k
-freeness results had been previously established for cyclically pinched, cyclically conjugated and related one-relator groups, for instance, by Baumslag in
[
Bau62b
]
, Rosenberger in
[
Ros82
]
and Fine–Gaglione–Rosenberger–Spellman in
[
FGRS95
]
. These are all encompassed by Louder–Wilton’s results.

If
w\in F
is an element, one might wonder what the subgroups that arise in
Definition

2.5.15
tell us about the subgroup structure of
F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
. This leads us to the notion of
w
-subgroups.

Definition 2.5.17
.

If
F
is a free group and
w\in F
is an element, the
w
-subgroups of
F
are the subgroups
H\leqslant F
satisfying the following:

•

w\in H
and
\pi(w)=\rk(H)
.

•

H
is maximal for inclusion amongst subgroups satisfying the above.

The following is due to Puder
[
Pud14
, Appendix A]
. See also
[
LW22
, Lemma 6.4]
.

Lemma 2.5.18
.

For any given
w\in F
, there are finitely many
w
-subgroups and there is an algorithm to compute them, and hence to compute
\pi(w)
.

The upshot of the above and
Theorem

2.5.16
is that there is an algorithm to decide when a one-relator group is
k
-free for some
k\geqslant 1
and hence when its presentation complex has (uniform) negative immersions.

We briefly sketch the algorithm, due to Puder
[
Pud14
]
. Let
\lambda\colon S^{1}\looparrowright\Lambda
be an immersion realising the inclusion
\langle w\rangle\hookrightarrow F
. Enumerate all graph immersions
\gamma\colon\Gamma\looparrowright\Lambda
that
\lambda
surjectively factors through:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
\looparrowleft
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

Using Whitehead’s algorithm
[
Whi36
]
, determine in which graphs
\Gamma
the cycle
S^{1}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma
represents a primitive element of
\pi_{1}(\Gamma)
and throw all of these away. Additionally, throw away all graphs
\Gamma
such that there is already a graph immersion
\Gamma^{\prime}\looparrowright\Lambda
in our list that
\Gamma\looparrowright\Lambda
factors through and such that
\chi(\Gamma^{\prime})\geqslant\chi(\Gamma)
. Finally, throw away all graphs
\Gamma
whose Euler characteristic do not attain the maximal value amongst the graphs remaining. The graph immersions
\Gamma\looparrowright\Lambda
left over each correspond to
w
-subgroups of
F=\pi_{1}(\Lambda)
(under the
\pi_{1}
-map at the basepoint) and the primitivity rank can be computed as
\pi(w)=1-\chi(\Gamma)
.

In
[
LW22
]
it is shown that if
H\leqslant F
is a
w
-subgroup, then
H
is malnormal and the homomorphism

H/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle\to F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle

is injective
[
LW22
, Lemma 6.3 & Theorem 6.17]
. The image subgroups
H/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle\leqslant F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
are then also called
w
-subgroups
of the one-relator group.
Theorem

2.5.16
tells us about subgroups of rank at most
\pi(w)-1
, it turns out that one can also say something about the subgroups of rank
\pi(w)
, see
[
LW22
, Theorem 1.5]
.

Theorem 2.5.19
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group, then every subgroup
H\leqslant G
with
\rk(H)=\pi(w)
is either free or conjugate into a
w
-subgroup of
G
.

If
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
is a one-relator complex, there are natural one-relator complexes
Q\looparrowright X
immersing into
X
which represent the
w
-subgroups. These are obtained by taking the unique core graph immersion
\Gamma\looparrowright\Lambda
which represents a
w
-subgroup of
\pi_{1}(\Lambda)
and attaching a 2-cell along the unique lift of
\lambda
. The following is
[
Lin22b
, Theorem 3.3.16]
.

Theorem 2.5.20
.

If
X=(\Lambda,\lambda)
is a one-relator complex and
Q\looparrowright X
represents a
w
-subgroup, then there is a one-relator tower

Q=X_{N}\looparrowright\ldots\looparrowright X_{1}\looparrowright X_{0}=X.

This has the corollary that one can decide whether a given element of a one-relator group lies inside a
w
-subgroup.

When
\pi(w)=2
, Louder–Wilton show that there is only one
w
-subgroup.

Lemma 2.5.21
.

If
w\in F
is an element with
\pi(w)=2
, then there is precisely one
w
-subgroup
H\leqslant F
.

In light of
Theorem

2.5.19
and
Lemma

2.5.21
, we ask the following question.

Question 2.5.22
.

If
F
is a free group and
w\in F
is an element with
\pi(w)\geqslant 3
, is it true that there is a unique
w
-subgroup?

In
[
CH23
]
, Cashen–Hoffman used a computer to verify various conjectures about one-relator groups with relator length that is not too long. In particular, they answer Question
2.5.22
positively for all words
w\in F_{4}
of length at most
16
and with
\pi(w)=3
. Note that if
\pi(w)=\rk(F)
, then
w
trivially has a unique
w
-subgroup, namely
F
. The primitivity rank may also have a relation with the stable commutator length, see
[
LW24
, Conjecture 1.7]
. This was also explored computationally by Cashen–Hoffman.

Moldavanskiĭ asked the following question in the case
\pi(w)=1

[
KM18
, 11.63]
. We believe it should be true for any primitivity rank.

Question 2.5.23
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group and
K
is a
w
-subgroup. Is it true that if
H\leqslant G
is a subgroup such that
H\cap\langle\!\langle K\rangle\!\rangle=1
, then
H
is free?

If Question
2.5.22
turns out to have a negative answer, then Question
2.5.23
should be modified appropriately.

If
K
is a finite field of characteristic
p
, Ernst-West–Puder–Seidel define in
[
EWPS24
]
the
p
-primitivity rank
of an element
w\in F
in terms of ideals in the group ring
K[F]
of the free group with coefficients in
K
:

\pi_{p}(w)=\min{\{\rk(I)\mid w-1\in I\lneq K[F],\text{ $w-1$ is imprimitive in $I$}\}}.

They proved that
\pi_{q}(w)\leqslant\pi(w)
for all
q
and conjectured that
\pi_{q}(w)=\pi(w)
. One could also conjecture whether an analog of
Theorem

2.5.16
holds for group rings of free groups. Say a ring
R
is
k
-free if all ideals of rank at most
k
in
R
are free.

Question 2.5.24
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group and
K
is a finite field of characteristic
p
, is it true that
K[G]
is
k
-free for
k<\pi_{q}(w)
?

Question
2.5.24
is true when
\pi_{q}(w)=2
for the following reason. We remarked that
\pi_{q}(w)\leqslant\pi(w)
and so
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is torsion-free. Hence, if
\pi_{q}(w)=2
, then
G
is locally indicable and so
K[F]
has no-zero divisors by
Theorem

2.4.13
. Since cyclic ideals of
K[G]
being free is the same thing as
K[G]
being a domain, the claim follows.

If true, Question
2.5.24
is likely to be very difficult to prove. Some related work was done by Avramidi in
[
Avr22
]
who proved that the group ring of the fundamental group of a surface of sufficiently high genus is 2-free. A possibly simpler and also interesting variation of Question
2.5.24
could be the question as to whether
K[G]
is 2-free when
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
with
\pi(w)\geqslant 3
.

2.5.4.
Coherence

We saw in
Section

2.4.5
the coherence property for rings, in this section we cover the analogous notion for groups.

Definition 2.5.25
.

A group
G
is
coherent
if all finitely generated subgroups of
G
are finitely presented.

Although the coherence property for groups had been studied before, the term appears to have first been used for groups by Serre in
[
New74
]
. The first explicit example of a finitely presented incoherent group was provided by Baumslag–Boone–Neuman in
[
BBN59
]
. The example they gave was
F_{2}\times\bs(1,2)
.

Some of the first classes of groups shown to be coherent were the classes of cyclically pinched and cyclically conjugated one-relator groups, a result due to Karrass–Solitar
[
KS70
,
KS71b
]
. A group is
cyclically pinched
if it has a presentation of the form:

\langle A,B\mid u(A)=w(B)\rangle\cong F(A)\underset{\langle u\rangle=\langle w\rangle}{*}F(B).

That is, if it is the amalgamated free product of two free groups over a cyclic subgroup. The cyclically conjugated one-relator group are defined analogously, but with HNN-extensions. A group is
cyclically conjugated
if it has a presentation of the form:

\left\langle A,t\mid t^{-1}u(A)t=w(A)\right\rangle\cong F(A)*_{\langle u\rangle^{t}=\langle w\rangle}.

Karrass–Solitar mention in
[
KS70
]
that their coherence result solved a conjecture of Baumslag’s. Later, Baumslag conjectured that in fact all one-relator groups should be coherent in
[
Bau74b
]
.

A push to solve this conjecture by Dani Wise, and later Lars Louder and Henry Wilton, sparked a lot of progress in understanding the subgroup structure of one-relator groups. We will discuss all this progress in detail in this section. First we mention the most general, but weakest, result which was proven by Andrei Jaikin-Zapirain and the first author in
[
JZL23
]
.

Theorem 2.5.26
.

If
G
is a one-relator group, then
G
is coherent.

In general, how might one prove a finitely generated group
H
is finitely presented? Starting with a finitely generated free group
G_{0}
and a surjection
G_{0}\twoheadrightarrow H
, we might try and choose elements
r_{1},r_{2},\ldots\in G_{0}
such that
\langle\!\langle r_{1},r_{2},\ldots\rangle\!\rangle=\ker(G_{0}\twoheadrightarrow H)
. This gives us a diagram of epimorphisms

(2.4)

\displaystyle G_{0}\twoheadrightarrow G_{1}\twoheadrightarrow\ldots\twoheadrightarrow G_{n}\to G_{n+1}\twoheadrightarrow\ldots\twoheadrightarrow H

where each
G_{i}=G_{0}/\langle\!\langle r_{1},\ldots,r_{i}\rangle\!\rangle
is finitely presented and so that

\lim_{i\to\infty}G_{i}=H.

Then
H
will be finitely presented if and only if for some
k
the epimorphism
G_{k}\to H
is an isomorphism. When
H
is a subgroup of a group with sufficiently nice properties, then it might be possible to define a ‘complexity’ on the candidate presentation, choose relations
r_{i}
in a clever way so that this complexity goes down, eventually reaching something of minimal complexity which yields an actual presentation. This is the main idea in the proof of coherence for instance for three-manifold groups
[
Sco73a
]
or mapping tori of free groups
[
FH99
]
.

The ‘sufficiently nice’ properties we need for coherence results will be the uniform negative immersions and non-positive immersions properties. As such, we need a version of
Eq.

2.4
for immersions. The following is due to Louder–Wilton
[
LW24
]
and makes fundamental use of a generalisation of Scott’s Lemma
[
Sco73b
]
, due to Delzant (see
[
Swa04
]
).

Lemma 2.5.27
.

Let
X
be a compact 2-complex and let
H\leqslant\pi_{1}(X)
be a finitely generated subgroup. There is a sequence of
\pi_{1}
-surjective immersions of compact connected 2-complexes:

Z_{0}\looparrowright Z_{1}\looparrowright\ldots\looparrowright Z_{k}\looparrowright\ldots\looparrowright X

such that

\lim_{i\to\infty}\pi_{1}(Z_{i})\cong H.

Moreover, if
H
is non-cyclic and freely indecomposable, then after possibly replacing
H
by a conjugate, each
Z_{i}
can be taken to be irreducible.

In the next two sections, we shall use
Lemma

2.5.27
to understand subgroups of
\pi_{1}(X)
for
X
a finite 2-complex with uniform negative immersions or non-positive immersions.

Strong coherence and uniform negative immersions

If we plug into
Lemma

2.5.27
a 2-complex with uniform negative immersions and a non-cyclic freely indecomposable subgroup
H\leqslant\pi_{1}(X)
, then there is an
\epsilon>0
such that

\frac{\chi(Z_{i})}{\#\{\text{2-cells in $Z_{i}$}\}}\leqslant-\epsilon.

Rearranging the inequality and using the fact that
\chi(Z_{i})\geqslant 1-b_{1}(Z_{i})\geqslant 1-b_{1}(Z_{0})
, we may obtain a uniform bound on the number of 2-cells:

\#\{\text{2-cells in $Z_{i}$}\}\leqslant\frac{b_{1}(Z_{0})-1}{\epsilon}.

Since each
Z_{i}
is irreducible, every 0-cell and every 1-cell in
Z_{i}
is traversed by the attaching map of some 2-cell, implying a uniform bound on the number of cells in
Z_{i}
. Hence, all but finitely many of the immersions
Z_{i}\looparrowright X
are the same. Being careful with the direct limit, this implies that
H
is finitely presented. To obtain coherence of
\pi_{1}(X)
, one needs to combine this fact with Grushko’s theorem. With a more careful analysis, Louder–Wilton establish a much stronger theorem on subgroups of one-relator groups with uniform negative immersions,
[
LW24
, Theorems A & B]
.

Theorem 2.5.28
.

Let
G
be a 2-free one-relator group. Or more generally, let
G=\pi_{1}(X)
with
X
a finite 2-complex with uniform negative immersions. Then
G
is coherent and the following properties hold:

(1)

For any integer
n
, there are only finitely many conjugacy classes of finitely generated one-ended subgroups
H\leqslant G
with
b_{1}(H)\leqslant n
.

(2)

Every finitely generated one-ended subgroup
H\leqslant G
is co-hopfian, in the sense that
H
is not isomorphic to any proper subgroup of itself.

(3)

Every finitely generated non-cyclic subgroup
H\leqslant G
is large, in the sense that it contains a finite index subgroup that surjects a rank two free group.

By
Theorem

2.5.14
,
Theorem

2.5.28
applies to a finite index subgroup of a one-relator group with torsion. In particular,
Item

1
from the above hold for one-relator groups with torsion. The proof that one-relator groups with torsion are coherent was obtained previously by Louder–Wilton in
[
LW20
]
and, independently, by Wise in
[
Wis22
]
.

We remark that coherence,
Item

1
and
Item

2
are properties shared by (one-ended) locally quasi-convex hyperbolic groups, see work of Sela
[
Sel97
, Theorem 4.4]
and Kapovich–Weidmann
[
KW04
, Corollary 1.5]
. In fact Wilton has conjectured that all finite 2-complexes with uniform negative immersions have locally quasi-convex hyperbolic fundamental group in
[
Wil24a
, Conjecture 12.9]
.

Homological coherence and non-positive immersions

The same strategy employed in
Section

2.5.4
does not work for non-positive immersions. Indeed, except for possibly the coherence, none of the conclusions from
Theorem

2.5.28
hold for finite 2-complexes with non-positive immersions. In order to get around this, we will need to consider a weaker property than coherence.

A finitely generated group
G=F/N
has
type
\fp_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
if any one of the following equivalent conditions hold:

(1)

Its relation module
N_{\ab}
is finitely generated as a
\mathbb{Z}G
-module.

(2)

The augmentation ideal
I_{\mathbb{Z}G}\leqslant\mathbb{Z}G
is finitely presented.

(3)

The trivial
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
\mathbb{Z}
admits a projective resolution
P_{*}\to\mathbb{Z}
with
P_{i}
finitely generated for
i=0,1,2
.

(4)

There is a surjective homomorphism
\phi\colon H\to G
from a finitely presented group
H
such that
H_{1}(\ker(\phi),\mathbb{Z})=0
.

Definition 2.5.29
.

A group
G
is said to be
homologically coherent
if all of its finitely generated subgroups have type
\fp_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

These definitions naturally extend to arbitrary rings
R
and to dimensions other than 2. Note that homological coherence of a group
G
follows from coherence of the group ring
\mathbb{Z}G
by the second definition of
\fp_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

A priori, it seems that homological coherence should be a weaker property than coherence. Indeed, there are well known examples of groups that have type
\fp_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
but are not finitely presented by work of Bestvina–Brady
[
BB97
]
. There are also examples which distinguish
\fp_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
from
\fp_{2}(K)
where
K
is any field by Bieri–Strebel
[
BS80
]
. However, it turns out that within a very large class of groups the two notions coincide. See
[
JZL23
, Theorem 4.5]
, stated below.

Theorem 2.5.30
.

Let
\mathcal{CG}
be the smallest class of groups containing all coherent groups and that is closed under subgroups, finite extensions, amalgamated free products, HNN-extensions and directed unions. Then within
\mathcal{CG}
, the homologically coherent groups coincide with the coherent groups.

Just as in the case of group rings from
Section

2.4.5
, homological coherence of a group can be shown via a criterion which depends on an embedding of the group ring into a division ring. The following is
[
JZL23
, Corollary 3.4]
.

Proposition 2.5.31
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring and let
G
be finitely generated group with
\cd_{R}(G)\leqslant 2
. If
R[G]\hookrightarrow\Di
is an embedding into a division ring, then

G\text{ has type $\fp_{2}(R)$}\iff\dim_{\Di}H_{2}(G,\Di)<\infty.

In particular, if
\Di
has weak dimension at most one, then
G
is homologically coherent.

Now let us plug into
Lemma

2.5.27
a 2-complex
X
with non-positive immersions and a finitely generated non-cyclic and freely indecomposable subgroup
H\leqslant\pi_{1}(X)
. We have a sequence of immersions

Z_{0}\looparrowright Z_{1}\looparrowright\ldots\looparrowright Z_{k}\looparrowright\ldots\looparrowright X

such that the following properties hold:

(1)

The homomorphisms
\pi_{1}(Z_{i})\to\pi_{1}(Z_{i+1})\to H\leqslant\pi_{1}(X)
are surjective for all
i
.

(2)

\lim_{i\to\infty}\pi_{1}(Z_{i})=H
.

(3)

Z_{i}
is compact for all
i
.

(4)

\chi(Z_{i})\leqslant 0
and
Z_{i}
is aspherical for all
i
(see
Theorem

2.5.7
).

In particular, letting
F=\pi_{1}\left(Z_{0}^{(1)}\right)
and
N_{i}=\ker(F\to\pi_{1}(Z_{i}))
, we obtain a dual sequence

N_{0}\hookrightarrow N_{1}\hookrightarrow\ldots\hookrightarrow N_{k}\hookrightarrow\ldots\hookrightarrow F

with the following dual properties:

(1)

The homomorphisms
N_{i}\to N_{i+1}\to\ker(F\to H)\leqslant F
are injective for all
i
.

(2)

\lim_{i\to\infty}N_{i}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}N_{i}=\ker(F\to H)=N
.

(3)

The relation module
(N_{i})_{\ab}
for
\pi_{1}(Z_{i})=F/N_{i}
is a finitely generated (left)
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Z_{i})]
-module for all
i
.

(4)

If
\Di
is a division ring which is also a right
\mathbb{Z}[H]
-module, then

\dim_{\Di}(\Di\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[F/N_{i}]}(N_{i})_{\ab})-\rk(F)+1\leqslant 0

for all
i
.

This last inequality comes from the fact that each
Z_{i}
is aspherical and so we have the following projective resolution of
\mathbb{Z}
as a trivial
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Z_{i})]
-module:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N_{i})_{\ab}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{s\in S}\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Z_{i})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Z_{i})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

where here
S\subset F
is a free basis. See
Section

2.4.1
.

Since
\pi_{1}(X)
is locally indicable by
Theorem

2.5.7
, we may use
Theorem

2.4.14
to obtain a division ring embedding
\mathbb{Q}[H]\hookrightarrow\Di
. When
X
is a one-relator complex, we may instead use the Lewin–Lewin division ring from
Theorem

2.4.13
. We have

\dim_{\Di}(\Di\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}H}N_{\ab})\leqslant\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\dim_{\Di}\left(\Di\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(Z_{i})]}(N_{i})_{\ab}\right)\leqslant\rk(F)-1.

Since
H_{2}(H,\Di)
is a
\Di
-submodule of the finite dimensional
\Di
-module
\Di\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}H}N_{\ab}
, it is itself finite dimensional. Hence, we obtain the following theorem by
Proposition

2.5.31
. This is
[
JZL23
, Theorem 1.2]
.

Theorem 2.5.32
.

If
X
is a 2-complex with non-positive immersions, then
\pi_{1}(X)
is homologically coherent.

The coherence of torsion-free one-relator groups now follows by applying
Theorems

2.5.5
,
2.5.30
and
2.5.32
.

It remains an open question of Wise as to whether all 2-complexes with non-positive immersions are coherent, see
[
Wis20
, Conjecture 12.11]
. It is also open as to whether the rational group ring of the fundamental group of a 2-complex with non-positive immersions is coherent, see
[
JZL23
, Conjecture 6]
.

Effective coherence

A coherent group
G
is
effectively coherent
if there is an algorithm which, given as input a finite subset
S\subset G
, computes a finite presentation for the subgroup
\langle S\rangle\leqslant G
. For now, the following is wide open.

Problem 2.5.33
.

Prove that one-relator groups are effectively coherent.

Cyclically pinched and cyclically conjugated one-relator groups are known to be effectively coherent by work of Kapovich–Myasnikov–Weidmann
[
KWM05
]
. They show that graphs of effectively coherent groups with virtually polycyclic edge groups, and some extra conditions, are effectively coherent. Unfortunately, the same techniques do not work when applied to graphs of groups with free edge groups. New ideas will be needed for one-relator groups.

The proof that one-relator groups are coherent is very non-constructive and so unfortunately does not shed light on further structure of finitely generated subgroups of one-relator groups. Thus, in order to approach Problem
2.5.33
, it will be necessary to understand the subgroup structure of one-relator groups more explicitly. Even the case of one-relator groups
F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
with
\pi(w)\geqslant 3
is open and was explicitly proposed by Louder–Wilton in
[
LW24
]
.

2.5.5.
Virtual algebraic fibring and generalisations

Recall from
Section

2.3.3
that a group
G
algebraically fibres if there is a finitely generated normal subgroup
N\triangleleft G
such that
G/N\cong\mathbb{Z}
. We say a group
G

virtually algebraically fibres
if it has a finite index subgroup
H\leqslant G
which algebraically fibred. We saw with Brown’s criterion that we may characterise precisely when a one-relator group algebraically fibres. Determining when it is virtually algebraically fibred is significantly more difficult.

Dawid Kielak obtained a powerful criterion in
[
Kie20
]
for when a group with the RFRS property virtually algebraically fibres. A group
G
is
RFRS
if it admits a sequence of finite index subgroups
N_{i}\triangleleft G
such that
N_{i+1}\leqslant\ker(N_{i}\to H_{1}(N_{i},\mathbb{Q}))
and
\bigcap N_{i}=1
. The main class of examples of RFRS groups are right angled Artin groups and all their subgroups. In particular, fundamental groups of special cube complexes are RFRS. Kielak proved that an infinite RFRS group
G
virtually algebraically fibres if and only if
b_{1}^{(2)}(G)=0
.

An immediate consequence of Kielak’s result and Dicks–Linnell’s computation of the
L^{2}
-Betti numbers of one-relator groups is that If
G
is a virtually RFRS torsion-free two-generator one-relator group, then
G
is virtually algebraically fibred. Since one-relator groups are coherent, the fibre subgroup will be a finitely presented subgroup. Using a result of Fel’dman
[
Fel71
, Theorem 2.4]
, we may conclude that the fibre subgroup drops in cohomological dimension and so is a free group by a result of Stallings
[
Sta68
]
. These results together imply the following statement.

Theorem 2.5.34
.

If
G
is a torsion-free two-generator one-relator group that is virtually RFRS, then
G
is virtually
{
finitely generated free
}
-by-cyclic.

Note that by
[
Lüc02
, Theorem 7.2]
, a virtually algebraically fibred group
G
has
b_{1}^{(2)}(G)=0
. Hence, if
G
is a one-relator group that virtually algebraically fibres, then
G
must be two-generator and be torsion-free by
Theorem

2.4.10
.

An interesting variation of the virtual algebraic fibration problem which has no restriction on
b_{1}^{(2)}
is the following. Given a group
G
with
\cd(G)=n
, when does
G
admit a finite index subgroup which embeds as a subgroup of a group
H
that algebraically fibres over a group
N
with
\cd(N)=n-1
? For one-relator groups this amounts to asking when they admit finite index subgroups that embed in {finitely generated free}-by-cyclic groups. Under strong geometric hypotheses, Kielak and the first author proved a criterion in
[
KL24
]
for when a one-relator group admits a positive answer to this question, solving a problem of Baumslag from
[
Bau86
]
.

Theorem 2.5.35
.

Let
G
be a one-relator group. If
G
is hyperbolic and virtually special, then
G
has a finite index subgroup
H
that is a subgroup of a
{
finitely generated free
}
-by-cyclic group.

The class of {finitely generated free}-by-cyclic groups form an extensively studied class of groups with a deep and rich theory, connecting dynamics, geometry and algebra. Such groups lie within the slightly more general class of free-by-cyclic groups, where here the free part is not necessarily finitely generated. In this generality, less is known although most results for {finitely generated free}-by-cyclic groups can be upgraded to the more general case. Weakening the assumptions from
Theorem

2.5.35
, Fisher proved in
[
Fis24
]
that any virtually RFRS one-relator group is virtually free-by-cyclic.

Theorem 2.5.36
.

Let
G
be a one-relator group. If
G
is virtually RFRS, then
G
is virtually free-by-cyclic.

Replacing the hypothesis that
G
be a one-relator group with the hypothesis that
b_{2}^{(2)}(G)=0
and
\cd_{\mathbb{Q}}(G)\leqslant 2
,
Theorem

2.5.35
and
Theorem

2.5.36
remain valid. There are also similar statements for groups of arbitrary finite cohomological dimension in
[
KL24
,
Fis24
]
.

Chong–Wise show in
[
CW24
]
that a finitely generated ascending HNN-extension of a free group is a subgroup of an ascending HNN-extension of a finitely generated free group. The slight mismatch between the conclusions of
Theorem

2.5.35
and
Theorem

2.5.36
would be resolved if a similar statement was proven for free-by-cyclic groups (see
[
CW24
, Conjecture 1.2]
):

Conjecture 2.5.37

(Chong–Wise)
.

If
G
is a finitely generated free-by-cyclic group, then
G
is a subgroup of a
{
finitely generated free
}
-by-cyclic group.

We do not know to what extent
Theorem

2.5.36
characterises which one-relator groups are virtually free-by-cyclic. Button showed in
[
But19
, Corollary 8]
that there are residually finite one-relator groups which are not virtually free-by-cyclic (not even virtually an ascending HNN-extension of a free group). Wise made the following conjecture in
[
Wis20
, Conjecture 17.8]
.

Conjecture 2.5.38

(Wise)
.

If
G
is a hyperbolic one-relator group, then
G
is virtually free-by-cyclic.

2.5.6.
Mel’nikov’s conjecture, finite index subgroups and infinite index subgroups

A well-known conjecture in the theory of one-relator groups is due to Mel’nikov, first stated in the 1980 edition of the Kourovka notebook
[
KM18
, Problem 7.36]
: if
G
is a residually finite group in which all finite index subgroups are one-relator, then
G
is either free or the fundamental group of a closed surface. The entry in the Kourovka notebook notes that a counterexample was quickly provided by Churkin: the Baumslag–Solitar groups
\bs(1,n)
each have all their finite index subgroups isomorphic to
\bs(1,m)
for various values of
m
. However, the following modification is still open.

Conjecture 2.5.39

(Mel’nikov)
.

If
G
is a residually finite group in which all finite index subgroups are one-relator, then
G
is either free, isomorphic to
\bs(1,n)
for some
n
or the fundamental group of a closed surface.

A group satisfying the assumptions of Conjecture
2.5.39
is known as a
Mel’nikov group
. Some progress on Conjecture
2.5.39
has been made. For instance, Curran studied finite index subgroups of one-relator groups with surface-like presentations in
[
Cur89
]
, characterising when they are actually surface groups. Ciobanu–Fine–Rosenberger confirm Conjecture
2.5.39
for all cyclically and conjugacy pinched one-relator groups in
[
CFR13
]
. Gardam–Kielak–Logan showed in
[
GKL23
]
that 2-generator Mel’nikov groups satisfy the conclusion of Conjecture
2.5.39
