. Jaikin-Zapirain–Morales showed in
[
JZM24b
]
that Mel’nikov groups generated by at least three elements are 2-free, so in particular they have (uniform) negative immersions by
Theorem

2.5.16
. Finally, we mention that the pro-
p
version of Mel’nikov’s original conjecture has been known to hold for a long time by work of Dummit–Labute
[
DL83
]
, where the pro-
p
analogue of surface groups are known as Demušhkin groups.

Several variations of Mel’nikov’s conjecture have also been posed. For instance, Fine–Rosenberger–Wienke asked
[
KM18
, Problem 20.22]
whether a one-relator group in which every infinite index subgroup is free is either free or the fundamental group of a closed surface. This was also confirmed for two-generator one-relator groups in
[
GKL23
]
. Wilton later confirmed this variation for all one-relator groups, see
[
Wil24b
, Theorem D]
.

Theorem 2.5.40
.

If
G
is an infinite one-relator group that is not free or a surface group, then
G
contains an infinite index non-free subgroup.

When
G
is a one-relator group generated by at least three elements, Wilton actually shows that there is a finitely generated non-free subgroup of infinite index. When
G
is also hyperbolic and cubulated (for instance when
G
is 2-free, see
Section

2.6.3
), this subgroup is also quasi-convex. Whether this stronger property holds also for two-generator one-relator groups is an open question of Wilton’s
[
Wil24b
, Question 5.4]
.

Question 2.5.41

(Wilton)
.

If
G
is a two-generator one-relator group which is not free and is not isomorphic to
\bs(1,n)
for any
n
, does
G
contain a finitely generated non-free subgroup of infinite index?

A related and well-known conjecture is Gromov’s surface subgroup conjecture which predicts that every one-ended hyperbolic group contains a surface subgroup. Wilton made a significant contribution to this conjecture in
[
Wil18
]
when he confirmed the surface subgroup conjecture for graphs of free groups with cyclic edge groups. Many special cases were established previously by several authors, the reader is directed to consult Wilton’s article for details. The following is a special case of Wilton’s theorem.

Theorem 2.5.42
.

If
G
is a non-free cyclically pinched or cyclically conjugated one-relator group, then either
G
contains a
\bs(1,n)
subgroup for some
n
, or
G
is hyperbolic and contains a quasi-convex surface subgroup.

Whether an analogous statement holds for all one-relator groups is wide open, although Calegari–Walker showed that random one-relator groups do contain surface subgroups
[
CW15
, Theorem 5.2.4]
.

2.5.7.
SQ-universality, virtual Betti numbers and largeness

A group
G
is
SQ-universal
if every countable group can be embedded in a quotient of
G
. Peter Neumann made the following conjecture in
[
Neu73
]
.

Conjecture 2.5.43

(P. Neumann)
.

A one-relator group is either solvable or SQ-universal.

Note that a solvable one-relator group is either cyclic or isomorphic to
\bs(1,n)
for some
n\neq 0
, see work of Čebotar
′

[
Che71
, Theorem 3]
.

Sacerdote–Schupp
[
SS74a
]
established a criterion for an HNN-extension to be SQ-universal (which is almost identical to the Minasyan–Osin criterion for acylindrical hyperbolicity from
[
MO15
]
) and used it to show that any one-relator group generated by at least three elements is SQ-universal. Currently, the most general result regarding SQ-universality of one-relator groups is due to Button–Kropholler
[
BK16
]
. One of their statements is conditional on a conjecture which was resolved by Mutanguha in
[
Mut21
]
, so below we state the unconditional statement.

Theorem 2.5.44
.

If
G
is a one-relator group, then either
G
is SQ-universal or
G
is residually finite and
b_{1}(H)=1
for all finite index subgroups
H\leqslant G
.

The
virtual
n^{\text{th}}
Betti number
of a group
G
is

\vb_{n}(G)=\sup{\{b_{n}(H)\mid H\text{ is a finite index subgroup of $G$}\}}.

By
Theorem

2.5.44
, Neumann’s conjecture reduces to the case of residually finite one-relator groups
G
with
\vb_{1}(G)=1
.

Conjecture 2.5.45
.

A residually finite one-relator group with
\vb_{1}(G)=1
is either infinite cyclic or
\bs(1,n)
for some
n\neq 0
.

In
[
BK16
]
the authors show that any such group that is not SQ-universal must split as a strict ascending HNN-extensions of a finitely generated free group and must also contain some
\bs(1,n)
for
n\geqslant 2
.

Problem 2.5.46
.

Characterise one-relator groups
G
with
\vb_{i}(G)<\infty
for
i=1,2
.

Fruchter–Morales characterise when a cyclically pinched and cyclically conjugated one-relator group
G
has
\vb_{2}(G)<\infty
in
[
FM24
]
: either
G
is free or the fundamental group of a closed surface.

A property related to SQ-universality is that of largeness. A group
G
is
large
if it admits a finite index subgroup which surjects onto the free group of rank two. Edjvet–Pride pose the problem of determining which torsion-free two-generator one-relator groups are large
[
EP84
, Problem 9]
. Baumslag–Pride had shown previously in
[
BP78
,
BP79
]
that all non-elementary one-relator groups with torsion and all one-relator groups generated by at least three elements are large. In fact, something much stronger is true for a one-relator group
F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
when
\pi(w)\neq 2
: all finitely generated non-elementary subgroups are large by the results in
Section

2.5.4
.

A large group is SQ-universal, but the converse is not necessarily true. For example,
\bs(2,3)
is an SQ-universal group that is not large (see
[
EP84
]
). It is not clear whether there is a natural conjectural picture for which one-relator groups are large as the property appears far more subtle than SQ-universality. We direct the reader to the work of Jack Button for many partial results, see for instance
[
But10
,
But11
]
.

2.6.
Geometric properties

Geometric group theory is the study of groups via their actions by isometries on metric spaces.
\cat(0)
cube complexes and hyperbolic spaces are two rich classes of spaces that have taken centre stage in geometric group theory since Gromov’s famous essay
[
Gro87
]
. If a one-relator group
G
acts in some non-trivial way on one of these spaces, then a wealth of new tools become available to study
G
and often this leads to significant constraints on its structure. In this section we shall go into depth on what is known about geometric actions of one-relator groups.

Metric spaces will always be assumed to be proper and geodesic and our actions will be geometric, unless stated otherwise. Recall that a
geometric action
is one that is by isometries and is properly discontinuous and cocompact.

2.6.1.
Hyperbolic one-relator groups

A group
G
is
hyperbolic
if it acts geometrically on a hyperbolic metric space. Introduced by Gromov in
[
Gro87
]
, their structure is now well-understood enough that their automorphism groups have been completely computed and their isomorphism problem has been solved, two things which are currently far from true for one-relator groups. A simple obstruction for a group to be hyperbolic is the presence of Baumslag–Solitar subgroups
\bs(1,n)
. A famous conjecture of Gersten’s predicts that this is the only obstruction for one-relator groups.

Conjecture 2.6.1

(Gersten’s conjecture)
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group containing no Baumslag–Solitar subgroups
\bs(1,n)
for any
n\neq 0
, then
G
is hyperbolic.

In this section we cover recent progress on Conjecture
2.6.1
, mostly based on work from
[
Lin22a
,
Lin24a
]
.

Remark 2.6.2
.

A more general version of Gersten’s conjecture was open until recently, when Italiano–Martelli–Migliorini showed in
[
IMM23
]
that there exist groups
G
of type
F
, containing no Baumslag–Solitar subgroups, but that are not hyperbolic. Their examples have cohomological dimension four and arise from a fibration of a hyperbolic 5-manifold over
S^{1}
.

It is still open as to whether there exist finitely presented groups of cohomological dimension two without Baumslag–Solitar subgroups that are not hyperbolic. Groups of cohomological dimension two are quite special: Gersten proved that finitely presented subgroups of finitely presented hyperbolic groups of cohomological dimension two are themselves hyperbolic
[
Ger96
]
. Combining
[
Ger96
, Corollary 7.8]
with the coherence of one-relator groups, we have the following.

Theorem 2.6.3
.

If
G
is a hyperbolic one-relator group, then every finitely generated subgroup of
G
is hyperbolic.

An early geometric result for one-relator groups is known as Newman’s spelling theorem
[
New68a
]
, see
Section

1.6.1
. A corollary of Newman’s result is that a one-relator group with torsion
G=F/\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle
is hyperbolic. Below is a slight strengthening of Newman’s result, and a generalisation of the Freiheitssatz, which was obtained by Schupp in
[
Sch76b
]
. Here when we say a word
u
mentions a letter
s
, we mean that
s
or
s^{-1}
appears in
u
.

Theorem 2.6.4
.

Let
n\geqslant 1
be a positive integer, let
F(S)
be a free group,
w\in F(S)
a cyclically reduced word that is not a proper power and consider the one-relator group
G=F(S)/\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle
. Let
A\subset S
be a subset of the generators,
v\in F(S)
a freely reduced word and suppose that
w
and
v
mention a letter in
S-A
.

If
v
, as an element of
G
, is contained in the Magnus subgroup
\langle A\rangle\leqslant G
, then
v
contains a subword
u^{n-1}u_{0}
where
u
is a cyclic conjugate of
w
or
w^{-1}
and
u_{0}
is a prefix of
u
which mentions each letter from
S-A
that appears in
w
.

Setting
A=\emptyset
, Newman’s theorem shows that the presentation of a one-relator group with torsion is actually a Dehn presentation (after symmetrising) and so the uniform word problem and conjugacy problem can both be solved very quickly in practice. More generally, Newman’s result shows that we can also solve membership in
\langle A\rangle
using Dehn’s algorithm. In particular, this implies that Magnus subgroups are actually convex in the sense that the shortest words over the generating set
S
that represent elements in a Magnus subgroup
\langle A\rangle
, must actually be words over
A
. Schupp’s strengthening yields the same consequences when applied to torsion-free one-relator groups whose relator contains appearances of letters that are sufficiently far apart. For instance, it applies to surface relators, relators of the form
a^{n}b^{n}c^{n}d^{n}
and many more.

Although in general hyperbolic one-relator group might not have a presentation with the properties from
Theorem

2.6.4
, it is known that Magnus subgroups are always at least
quasi-convex
. That is, geodesics in a hyperbolic one-relator group between two elements in a Magnus subgroup always remain uniformly close to elements in the Magnus subgroup. This is proved in
[
Lin24a
]
, building on work from
[
Lin22a
]
. Note that for hyperbolic groups, quasi-convexity is equivalent to being undistorted.

Theorem 2.6.5
.

Magnus subgroups of hyperbolic one-relator groups are quasi-convex.

A hyperbolic one-relator group may have many distorted (free) subgroups, see
[
Kap99
]
for an explicit example.

The Bestvina–Feighn combination theorem
[
BF92
]
is an important tool that can be used to show when a HNN-extension of a hyperbolic group is again hyperbolic. The combination theorem states that if
G=H*_{\psi}
is a HNN-extension with
H
hyperbolic, both associated subgroups quasi-convex in
H
and such that the HNN-extension has a property called
flaring annuli
, then
G
is hyperbolic. Conversely, Gersten showed in
[
Ger98
, Theorem 6.4 & Corollary 6.7]
that if
H
is hyperbolic, both associated subgroups are quasi-convex in
H
and
G
is hyperbolic, then the HNN-extension has flaring annuli.

By the above discussion, if
G\cong H*_{\psi}
is a one-relator splitting, either
H
is not hyperbolic in which case
G
is not hyperbolic by
Theorem

2.6.3
, or
H
is hyperbolic, both associated subgroups are quasi-convex by
Theorem

2.6.5
and so
G
is hyperbolic if and only if annuli flare in
H*_{\psi}
. As such, if one can understand annuli in one-relator splittings, then in principal one understands when a one-relator group is hyperbolic or not. Unfortunately, flaring annuli turns out to be a tricky condition to check. See the article of Kapovich
[
Kap99
]
for an explicit use of the combination theorem to show that a specific one-relator group is hyperbolic.

On the other hand, if
G
acts acylindrically on its Bass–Serre tree, then
G
automatically has no flaring annuli. Hence, combining this with
Theorem

2.3.5
and an inductive argument on one-relator hierarchies, in
[
Lin22a
]
it was shown that 2-free one-relator groups are hyperbolic. In
[
Lin24a
]
, a slightly more general statement was established.

Theorem 2.6.6
.

Let
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
be a one-relator group. If two-generator one-relator subgroups of
G
are hyperbolic, then
G
is hyperbolic.

A more refined statement than
Theorem

2.6.6
reducing Gersten’s conjecture to two specific families of two-generator one-relator groups was proven in
[
Lin24a
]
. We describe these families in the next section. Combining Newman’s theorem and
Theorem

2.6.6
with
Theorem

2.5.16
, we have a clean condition for hyperbolicity.

Corollary 2.6.7
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
with
\pi(w)\neq 2
, then
G
is hyperbolic.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
with
\pi(w)=2
, recall that by
Theorem

2.5.19
there is a two-generator subgroup
H\leqslant F
containing
w
such that every non-free two-generator subgroup of
G
is conjugate into
P=H/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle\leqslant G
, the
w
-subgroup. Thus, in order to apply
Theorem

2.6.6
to such a one-relator group, one only needs to check that
P
is hyperbolic. Louder–Wilton conjectured that in fact
G
should be hyperbolic relative to
P
in
[
LW22
, Conjecture 1.9]
, this remains open.

Conjecture 2.6.8

(Louder–Wilton)
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
with
\pi(w)=2
, then
G
is hyperbolic relative to its
w
-subgroup
P\leqslant G
.

A well-known problem asks whether hyperbolic groups are residually finite. This is not known for hyperbolic one-relator groups.

Question 2.6.9
.

Are hyperbolic one-relator groups residually finite?

We close this section with some remarks on small cancellation conditions for one-relator groups. Pride showed that one-relator groups
F/\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle
with torsion satisfy the small cancellation condition
C(2n)

[
Pri83
]
. However, it is possible that they do not satisfy
C(2n+1)
and may not even satisfy
T(4)
when
n=2
. More refined small cancellation conditions were developed by Ivanov–Schupp and applied to characterise hyperbolicity of certain families of one-relator groups, not necessarily with torsion, in
[
IS98
]
. Blufstein–Minian introduce a small cancellation condition
T^{\prime}
in
[
BM22
]
and use it to prove even more one-relator groups are hyperbolic. Cashen–Hoffmann
[
CH23
]
implemented several hyperbolicity tests for one-relator groups, including the small cancellation conditions mentioned above, and produced a database of one-relator groups of relator length up to 17, verifying hyperbolicity when possible. They found many one-relator groups whose hyperbolicity could not be confirmed with any known small cancellation conditions. Finally, Ol’shanskiĭ proved in
[
Ol’92
]
that one-relator groups are (exponentially) generically
C^{\prime}(1/6)
and hence hyperbolic.

Primitive extension groups

In order to describe our two families of one-relator groups, we shall need to introduce Christoffel words, so called as they were first studied by Christoffel in
[
Chr73
]
.

Let
F=F(a,b)
be the free group of rank two and let
p/q\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geqslant 0}\cup\{+\infty\}
. Let
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the Cayley graph for
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with generating set
\{\overline{a},\overline{b}\}
, the images of
a,b
under the abelianisation map
F\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. The generator
\overline{a}
is the vertex
(1,0)
while the generator
\overline{b}
is the vertex
(0,1)
. Now let
L\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the line passing through the origin with slope
p/q
. Now let
I\to\Gamma
be the path connecting
(0,0)
with the first integral point on
L
, not going above
L
and such that the region enclosed by
L
and the image of this path contains no integral points in its interior. Then the
p/q
-Christoffel word
, which we denote by

\pr_{p/q}(a,b)

is the word over
\{a,b\}
traced out by this path. See
Fig.

2.8
for an illustration.

Figure 2.8.
The
2/3
-Christoffel word is
a^{2}bab
.

The notation for the Christoffel words comes from the fact that they are all primitive elements of
F(a,b)
. Nielsen showed that the abelianisation map
F\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}
induces a bijection between conjugacy classes of primitive elements in
F
and primitive elements in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}

[
Nie17
]
. Osborne–Zieschang showed in
[
OZ81
]
that the Christoffel words and their inverses provide a complete set of conjugacy class representatives for the primitive elements of
F
. This means that if
w\in F
is a primitive element, then
w
is conjugate to some
\pr_{p/q}(a,b)^{\pm 1}
. Moreover,
p/q
is precisely the exponent sum of
b
in
w
divided by the exponent sum of
a
in
w
.

The two types of
primitive extension groups
are the two-generator one-relator groups with presentations:

(2.5)

\displaystyle E_{p/q}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\langle a,b\mid\pr_{p/q}(x,y)\rangle

(2.6)

\displaystyle F_{p/q}(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\langle a,b\mid\pr_{p/q}(xy,z)\rangle

where

\displaystyle A_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\langle b^{-i}ab^{i},b^{-i-1}ab^{i+1},\ldots,b^{-j}ab^{j}\rangle\quad\text{ where $i<j\in\mathbb{N}$}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\in A_{0,k-1}-A_{1,k-1}

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\in A_{1,k}-A_{1,k-1}

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\in A_{1,k-1}-1

and
\{\langle x\rangle,\langle y\rangle\}
is a malnormal family in case (
2.5
) and
\langle z\rangle
is malnormal in case (
2.6
). There are actually a couple of extra conditions on the words
x,y,z
in
[
Lin24a
]
, but these are just to ensure that there is no other way to express the relator as a primitive word over words which violate the malnormality conditions. The upshot of these extra conditions is that when we go down one step in the hierarchy for
E_{p/q}
or
F_{p/q}
we reach a 2-free one-relator group, see
[
Lin24a
, Theorem 3.7]
.

Note that since
\pr_{p/q}(-,-)
is a primitive word, this implies that in
E_{p/q}(x,y)
we have
\langle x,y\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}
and in
F_{p/q}(xy,z)
we have
\langle xy,z\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}
.

The motivation behind introducing these groups is the main theorem from
[
Lin24a
]
which reduces the problem of determining which one-relator groups are hyperbolic to determining which primitive extension groups are hyperbolic.

Theorem 2.6.10
.

A one-relator group
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is hyperbolic if and only if all its primitive extension subgroups are hyperbolic. In particular, Gersten’s conjecture is true if it is true for primitive extension groups.

Let us mention some examples of primitive extension groups.

•

If
p,q>0
are coprime integers, then we have

\bs(p,\pm q)\cong E_{p/q}(a,b^{-1}a^{\pm 1}b).

We can see this as follows. We have

E_{p/q}(a,b^{-1}a^{\pm 1}b)\cong\langle a_{0},a_{1},b\mid b^{-1}a_{0}b=a_{1},\pr_{p/q}(a_{0},a_{1}^{\pm 1})\rangle.

We have
F(a_{0},a_{1})/\langle\!\langle\pr_{p/q}(a_{0},a_{1}^{\pm 1})\rangle\!\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}
and so
E_{p/q}(a,b^{-1}a^{\pm 1}b)
splits as a HNN-extension over
\mathbb{Z}
where the associated isomorphism is given by
p\mathbb{Z}\to\pm q\mathbb{Z}
.

•

Any one-relator group
G\cong F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
with non-empty BNS-invariant is a primitive extension group. This is explained more in detail in
[
Lin24a
, Example 5.5]
. More precisely, if
G
has non-empty BNS-invariant, then
G\cong E_{p/1}(a,y)
for some
p,y
.

Note that such groups have been shown by Mutanguha
[
Mut21
]
to be hyperbolic if and only if they contain no Baumslag–Solitar subgroups.

The presentations (
2.5
) and (
2.6
) may seem a little mysterious, but there are very explicit graphs of groups decompositions which express their structure better.

The primitive extension groups
E_{p/q}(x,y)
have the following graph of groups decomposition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0,k-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1,k-1}*\langle t\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1,k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1,k-1}*\langle x\rangle=A_{1,k-1}*\langle t^{p}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1,k-1}*\langle t^{q}\rangle=A_{1,k-1}*\langle y\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0,k-1}=A_{1,k}}

where the top isomorphism is given by shifting
b^{-i}ab^{i}\to b^{-i-1}ab^{i+1}
for each
0\leqslant i<k
and the lower isomorphisms are given by the identity on
A_{1,k-1}
and by
x\to t^{p}
and
y\to t^{q}
.

The primitive extension groups
F_{p/q}(xy,z)
have the following graph of groups decomposition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0,k-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1,k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1,k-1}*\langle x\rangle=A_{1,k-1}*\langle x\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1,k-1}*\langle y\rangle=A_{1,k-1}*\langle y\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0,k-1}=A_{1,k}}

where
H
splits as:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1,k-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle t\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle x,y\rangle}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle z\rangle=\langle t^{p}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle t^{q}\rangle=\langle xy\rangle}

Note that we have
x^{-1}zx=yzy^{-1}
in
H
. Moreover, since
xy
is primitive in the free group
\langle x,y\rangle
, we see that we have

H\cong\left(A_{1,k-1}\underset{\langle z\rangle=\langle t^{p}\rangle}{\ast}\langle t\rangle\right)*\mathbb{Z}

In light of
Theorem

2.6.10
and its proof, understanding the geometry of primitive extension groups appears to be an important goal in order to understand the geometry of all one-relator groups.

Problem 2.6.11
.

Characterise non flaring annuli in the sense of
[
BF92
]
of primitive extension groups with respect to the above graph of groups structures.

2.6.2.
\cat(0)
and cubulable one-relator groups

A group
G
is a
\cat(0)
group
if it acts geometrically on a
\cat(0)
space. Roughly speaking, a metric space is
\cat(0)
if its geodesic triangles are not fatter than Euclidean triangles with the same edge lengths. We refer the reader to
[
BH99
]
for a more formal definition and an in depth treatment of the theory. A group is a
\cat(0)
group
if it acts geometrically on a
\cat(0)
space.

A class of
\cat(0)
spaces which are easy to construct and have particularly nice combinatorial properties are
\cat(0)
cube complexes. A
cube complex
is a combinatorial complex built out of Euclidean cubes which are glued to each other isometrically along faces. A group is
cubulable
if it acts properly and cocompactly on a
\cat(0)
cube complex. A cube complex is
non-positively curved
if the link of every vertex, which is a simplicial complex, is flag. In other words, if the link of each vertex has no missing faces. The universal cover of a non-positively curved cube complex is
\cat(0)
by Gromov’s well-known link condition. The reader is directed to
[
Wis21
]
for details.

The question of which one-relator groups are
\cat(0)
appears to be more subtle than the hyperbolicity question. A
\cat(0)
group cannot contain
unbalanced Baumslag–Solitar
subgroups
\bs(m,n)
where
m\neq\pm n
. But a
\cat(0)
group can contain balanced
\bs(m,\pm m)
subgroups. Gardam–Woodhouse considered the following family of one-relator groups in
[
GW19
]
:

R_{p,q}=\langle x,t\mid t^{-1}x^{-2q}tx^{2p-1}t^{-1}x^{-2q}tx^{2p+1}\rangle

They showed that
R_{1,1}
contains as an index two subgroup the {fg free}-by-cyclic group that Gersten showed was not
\cat(0)
in
[
Ger94
]
. In particular,
R_{1,1}
does not contain any unbalanced Baumslag–Solitar subgroups, but is not
\cat(0)
. The groups
R_{p,q}
have some further interesting properties we believe are worth noting.

Theorem 2.6.12
.

If
p,q>0
are positive integers, the groups
R_{p,q}
have the following properties:

(1)

R_{p,q}
does not contain any unbalanced Baumslag–Solitar subgroups.

(2)

R_{p,q}
is residually finite if and only if
q\mid 2p
.

(3)

If
p<q
, then
R_{p,q}
is
\cat(0)
.

(4)

If
p=q
, then
R_{p,q}
is virtually
{
fg free
}
-by-cyclic and is not
\cat(0)
.

(5)

If
p>q
, then
R_{p,q}
has Dehn function
\simeq n^{2\log_{2}(2p/q)}
and does not act freely on any
\cat(0)
cube complex.

The first and the third fact are due to Gardam who introduced these groups in
[
Gar17
, Theorem G]
. The second fact is due to Button
[
But19
, Corollary 7]
. The fourth fact follows from a slight generalisation of Gardam–Woodhouses argument for
R_{1,1}
. The claimed finite index subgroup is the kernel of the map
R_{p,p}\to\mathbb{Z}/2p\mathbb{Z}
given by
x\to 1
and
t\to 0
. Then the statement for when
p>q
is
[
GW19
, Theorem A]
. Gardam–Wodhouse deduce this last fact by showing that
R_{p,q}
contains as an index two subgroup a snowflake group, see work of Brady–Bridson
[
BB00
]
. Note that admitting a free action on a
\cat(0)
space is much weaker than admitting a geometric action.

Other examples of
\cat(0)
one-relator groups with interesting properties are:

•

Hsu–Wise showed in
[
HW10
]
that graphs of free groups with cyclic edge groups are
\cat(0)
(cubulable in fact) if and only if they do not contain unbalanced Baumslag–Solitar subgroups. In particular, all cyclically pinched one-relator groups are
\cat(0)
and a cyclically conjugated one-relator group
\langle A,t\mid t^{-1}ut=v\rangle
is
\cat(0)
if and only if there is no
w\in F(A)
such that
u
and
v
are conjugate to
w^{m}
,
w^{n}
respectively with
m\neq\pm n
.

•

Infinite one-relator groups with non-trivial centre are
\cat(0)
. Murasugi showed in
[
Mur64
]
that they are all {fg free}-by-cyclic. Pietrowski showed
[
Pie74
]
that they all split as graphs of infinite cyclic groups. Since {fg free}-by-cyclic groups cannot contain unbalanced Baumslag–Solitar subgroups, Hsu–Wise’s result
[
HW10
]
implies the claim.

•

Lauer–Wise showed in
[
LW13
]
that any finitely generated one-relator group
F/\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle
acts properly on a
\cat(0)
cube complex if
n\geqslant 2
and acts cocompactly if
n\geqslant 4
.

•

Hydra groups

H_{k}=\langle a,t\mid[[\ldots[a,\underbrace{t],\ldots],t]}_{k}\rangle

introduced by Dison–Riley in
[
DR13
]
are
\cat(0)
, biautomatic and {fg free}-by-cyclic groups which contain free subgroups with distortion function
\simeq A_{k}
, where
A_{k}
is the
k^{\text{th}}
Ackermann function, defined recursively by
A_{1}(n)=2n,A_{k+1}(n)=A_{k}^{(n)}(1)
.

•

Brady–Crisp
[
BC07
]
proved that the one-relator group
\langle a,b\mid aba^{2}=b^{2}\rangle\cong F_{3}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}
is
\cat(-1)
(and hence is hyperbolic), but its
\cat(0)
dimension is two, whereas its
\cat(-1)
dimension is three.

In the next section we shall see that any one-relator group
F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
with
\pi(w)\geqslant 3
is virtually cocompactly cubulated. The following conjecture should provide more cubulated one-relator groups.

Conjecture 2.6.13
.

Let
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
be a one-relator group with
\pi(w)=2
and such that the
w
-subgroup
P\leqslant G
is virtually (cocompactly) cubulated. Then
G
is virtually (cocompactly) cubulated.

2.6.3.
Virtually compact special one-relator groups

A class of non-positively curved cube complexes known as
special cube complexes
have played a particularly important role in several significant developments in geometric group theory. Special cube complexes were introduced by Haglund–Wise in
[
HW08
]
. If
X
is a compact special cube complex, Haglund–Wise showed that
\pi_{1}(X)
embeds into a right angled Artin group,
\pi_{1}(X)
is residually finite and linear and, if hyperbolic, all its quasi-convex subgroups are separable.

In his monograph, Dani Wise
[
Wis21
]
developed criteria for when a group is virtually special in terms of hierarchies.

Definition 2.6.14
.

Define the class
\mathcal{VQH}
of groups with a
virtual quasi-convex hierarchy
as the smallest class of groups containing the trivial group and such that the following hold:

(1)

If
A,B\in\mathcal{VQH}
, then
G=A*_{C}B\in\mathcal{VQH}
if
C
is quasi-isometrically embedded in
G
.

(2)

If
A\in\mathcal{VQH}
, then
G=A*_{C}\in\mathcal{VQH}
if
C
is quasi-isometrically embedded in
G
.

(3)

If
A\leqslant\mathcal{VQH}
and
A\leqslant G
with
[G:A]<\infty
, then
G\in\mathcal{VQH}
.

One of the main results in
[
Wis21
]
determines when a hyperbolic group is virtually compact special.

Theorem 2.6.15

(Wise)
.

A hyperbolic group
G
lies in
\mathcal{VQH}
if and only if it is virtually compact special.

Wise used his quasi-convex hierarchy machinery in
[
Wis21
]
, combined with Newman’s spelling theorem
[
New68a
]
, to prove that one-relator groups with torsion are virtually compact special. The first author then proved in
[
Lin22a
]
that 2-free one-relator groups are hyperbolic and that all their one-relator hierarchies are quasi-convex. The proof involved a careful analysis of the action of a one-relator group on its Bass–Serre tree, leading to
Theorem

2.3.5
, proving that certain Magnus subgroups are quasi-convex and using a criterion for quasi-convexity in graphs of hyperbolic groups due to Kapovich
[
Kap01
]
.

Theorem 2.6.16
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group with
\pi(w)\neq 2
, then every Magnus hierarchy for
G
is a quasi-convex hierarchy. Hence,
G
has a finite index subgroup that is the fundamental group of a compact special cube complex.

Theorem

2.6.16
provides an answer to Question
2.6.9
for a large class of one-relator groups.

Corollary 2.6.17
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group with
\pi(w)\neq 2
, then
G
is residually finite, linear and quasi-convex subgroups are separable.

We conclude with a conjecture. This should be compared with a conjecture of Wise
[
Wis04
, Conjecture 14.2]
and a conjecture of Wilton
[
Wil24a
, Conjecture 12.9]
.

Conjecture 2.6.18
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a cubulated one-relator group, then every finitely generated subgroup of
G
is undistorted if and only if
\pi(w)\neq 2
or
\pi(w)=2
and the
w
-subgroup
P\leqslant G
is virtually
F_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}
for some
n\geqslant 1
.

2.7.
Residual properties

2.7.1.
Residually solvable and rationally solvable one-relator groups

Recall that
G^{(i)}=[G^{(i-1)},G^{(i-1)}]
denotes the
i^{\text{th}}
term of the derived series of
G
and that the
i^{\text{th}}
term of the rational derived series is the subgroup

G^{(i)}_{\mathbb{Q}}:=\left\{g\bigm|g^{k}\in\left[G_{\mathbb{Q}}^{(i-1)},G^{(i-1)}_{\mathbb{Q}}\right],k\neq 0\right\}\trianglelefteq G.

A group
G
is residually solvable if
\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}G^{(i)}=1
and it is residually rationally solvable if
\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}G^{(i)}_{\mathbb{Q}}=1
. The transcendental (rational) derived series is the extension of the (rational) derived series to arbitrary ordinals.

A one-relator group is positive if its relator is a positive word over the generators. A classical result due to Baumslag
[
Bau71b
]
states that a positive one-relator group is residually solvable. In
[
Lin24c
]
, residually rationally solvable one-relator groups were characterised in terms of their relator, generalising Baumslag’s result. Additionally, if
G
is a one-relator group and
H^{2}(G)=0
, it was shown that the rational derived series and the usual derived series of
G
coincide using work of Strebel
[
Str74
]
, yielding a characterisation of residual solvability amongst such one-relator groups. We now explain this characterisation and its consequences.

The usual example of a one-relator group which is not residually (rationally) solvable is the Baumslag–Gersten group

\bg(1,n)=\langle a,t\mid[a^{t},a]=a^{n-1}\rangle.

Since the relator expresses a power of
a
as a commutator of
a
and a conjugate of
a
, we see that
\langle\!\langle a\rangle\!\rangle
is rationally perfect in
\bg(1,n)
for
n\geqslant 2
. In other words,
H_{1}(\langle\!\langle a\rangle\!\rangle,\mathbb{Q})=0
. When
n=2
, we also have
H_{1}(\langle\!\langle a\rangle\!\rangle,\mathbb{Z})=0
. Hence,
G_{\mathbb{Q}}^{(1)}=G_{\mathbb{Q}}^{(2)}=\langle\!\langle a\rangle\!\rangle
for each
n\geqslant 2
and
G^{(1)}=G^{(2)}=\langle\!\langle a\rangle\!\rangle
for
n=2
. The main theorem in
[
Lin24c
]
shows that this phenomenon is not far off from the general case.

Theorem 2.7.1
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group, then there is some
r\in F
and some
n\geqslant 1
such that
w\in r^{n}[\langle\!\langle r\rangle\!\rangle,\langle\!\langle r\rangle\!\rangle]
and

G_{\mathbb{Q}}^{(\omega+1)}=G_{\mathbb{Q}}^{(\omega)}=\langle\!\langle r\rangle\!\rangle_{G}.

Furthermore, if
H^{2}(G)=0
, then
n=1
and
G^{(\omega+1)}=G^{(\omega)}=\langle\!\langle r\rangle\!\rangle_{G}
.

With the notation of
Theorem

2.7.1
,
G
is residually (rationally) solvable if and only if
r=_{G}1
and so
r
is conjugate to
w
or
w^{-1}
by
Theorem

2.2.8
.

Corollary 2.7.2
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group, then
G/G_{\mathbb{Q}}^{(\omega)}
is a residually rationally solvable one-relator group.

In particular,
Theorem

2.7.1
characterises precisely when the only rationally solvable quotients of a one-relator group
F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
have a certain derived length:

•

If all rationally solvable quotients of
F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
have rational derived length 1, then
F/\langle\!\langle r\rangle\!\rangle
would have to be abelian. This means that either
\rk(F)=1
or
\rk(F)=2
and
r
is primitive.

•

If all rationally solvable quotients of
F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
have rational derived length at most 2 (but not all 1), then
F/\langle\!\langle r\rangle\!\rangle
would have to be non-abelian and metabelian. Since the Baumslag–Solitar groups
\bs(1,n)
with
n\neq 0,1
are the only non-abelian metabelian one-relator groups, this means that
F/\langle\!\langle r\rangle\!\rangle\cong\bs(1,n)
for some
n\neq 0,1
. Moreover, by a result of Brunner
[
Bru74
, Theorem 2.4]
,
r
must lie in the same
\Aut(F)
-orbit of
(b^{-1}aba^{-n})^{\pm 1}
.

•

If
F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
admits rationally solvable quotients of rational derived length 3 but not 2, then
F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
admits rationally solvable quotients of arbitrary rational derived length.

Another corollary obtained in
[
Lin24c
]
is that it is algorithmically decidable whether a one-relator group is residually rationally solvable; one can compute the element
r
from
Theorem

2.7.1
and decide whether it is trivial in
G
.

It is not known whether the derived series of a one-relator group in general has length at most
\omega
, but in
[
Lin24c
]
it is conjectured that this is the case.

Conjecture 2.7.3
.

Let
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
be a one-relator group. There is a word
r\in F
such that
w\in r[\langle\!\langle r\rangle\!\rangle,\langle\!\langle r\rangle\!\rangle]
and

G^{(\omega+1)}=G^{(\omega)}=\langle\!\langle r\rangle\!\rangle_{G}.

In particular, the maximal residually solvable quotient of
G
is the one-relator group
F/\langle\!\langle r\rangle\!\rangle
.

A related class of groups is that of amenable groups. Like solvable groups, amenable groups also cannot contain non-abelian free subgroups. Unlike solvable groups, amenable groups do not form a variety and so finding a non-residually amenable group appears to be quite difficult. Arzhantseva has asked the following general question, see
[
KM18
, Problem 18.6]
.

Question 2.7.4

(Arzhantseva)
.

Is every one-relator group residually amenable?

It is not known whether the non residually solvable Baumslag–Gersten group
\bg(1,2)
is residually amenable. If the answer to Question
2.7.4
is negative, then it is likely to be negative for
\bg(1,2)
.

2.7.2.
Residually nilpotent and parafree one-relator groups

Recall that
G_{i}=[G_{i-1},G]
denotes the
i^{\text{th}}
term of the lower central series of
G
and that
G
is residually nilpotent if
\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}G_{i}=1
. In contrast with the (rational) derived series, it is known that the lower central series of a one-relator group can have length
\omega^{2}
by work of Mikhailov
[
Mik16
]
. Thus, a solution to the following problem is likely to be more subtle than the case of residually solvable one-relator groups.

Problem 2.7.5
.

Characterise when a one-relator group is residually nilpotent.

Azarov proved in
[
Aza98
, Theorem 1]
that a cyclically pinched one-relator group

G=F_{1}*_{\langle u\rangle=\langle v\rangle}F_{2}

is residually nilpotent if and only if either
u
or
v
is not a proper power in the free group
F_{1}
or
F_{2}
respectively. Outside of this result, only sporadic families are known to be residually nilpotent.

A group
G
is
parafree
if it is residually nilpotent and if there is a free group
F
such that

G/G_{i}\cong F/F_{i}\quad\quad\text{for all $i\in\mathbb{N}$}

Baumslag provided the first non-free examples of such groups in
[
Bau64
]
. The example he provided was the one-relator group
\langle a,b,c\mid a^{5}b^{3}c^{2}\rangle
. Baumslag then initiated the systematic study of parafree groups in a series of two papers
[
Bau67b
,
Bau69a
]
. In
[
Bau69a
]
, Baumslag proved that a parafree group must be 2-free. The reader is directed to Baumslag’s survey
[
Bau05
]
for further background on parafree groups and many one-relator examples. Using
Theorem

2.6.16
, the first author showed in
[
Lin22a
]
that parafree one-relator groups are hyperbolic and virtually special.

A major open problem in group theory is Remeslennikov’s conjecture: if
G
is a residually finite group with the same finite quotients as a free group, is
G
necessarily free? A positive answer is known within several classes of groups, but no systematic approach has been taken for the class of one-relator groups. Recently, Jaikin-Zapirain
[
JZ23
]
has shown that a residually finite group with the same finite quotients as a free group must be parafree. In light of this, understanding when a one-relator group is parafree becomes very interesting.

Magnus showed in
[
Mag39
]
that an
n
-generator group with the same lower central series quotients as a free group of rank
n
is actually a free group of rank
n
. Combining this with a result of Stallings
[
Sta65a
]
, we see that a one-relator group
G
that is residually nilpotent is parafree if and only if
H^{2}(G)=0
. Hence, solving
2.7.5
would also yield a characterisation of parafree one-relator groups.

In particular, combining Azarov’s result with these remarks, a cyclically pinched one-relator group
G=F_{1}*_{u=v}F_{2}
is parafree if and only if
u
or
v
is not a proper power and
H_{1}(G,\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}^{b_{1}(F_{1})+b_{1}(F_{2})-1}
. This was greatly generalised by Jaikin-Zapirain–Morales in
[
JZM24a
]
who characterised when a graph of parafree groups with cyclic edge groups is also parafree.

2.7.3.
Residual finiteness and profinite completions

If
G
is a group, the
profinite completion
of
G
, denoted by
\hat{G}
, is the inverse limit of the inverse system of finite quotients
G/N
of
G
. The group
G
is residually finite precisely when the canonical map
G\to\hat{G}
is injective. A classical result of Dixon–Formanek–Poland–Ribes
[
DFPR82
]
states that the profinite completions of two finitely generated groups
G
and
H
are isomorphic (as topological groups) precisely when the set of finite quotients of
G
and
H
coincide.

A group
G
is
profinitely rigid
(within a class of groups) if for any residually finite group
H
(within that class of groups), an isomorphism
\hat{H}\cong\hat{G}
implies that
H\cong G
. The profinite genus of a group
G
is the set of residually finite groups
H
such that
\hat{H}\cong\hat{G}
. With this language, Remeslenikov’s conjecture can be stated as predicting that free groups are profinitely rigid.

The study of profinite invariants and profinite rigidity of groups is an active area of research. However, within the class of one-relator groups, very little is known. Moldavanskiĭ–Sibyakova
[
MS95
]
showed that the Baumslag–Solitar groups
\bs(1,n)
are profinitely rigid amongst one-relator groups and Jaikin-Zapirain–Morales showed in
[
JZM24b
]
that closed surface groups are profinitely rigid amongst one-relator groups. Experimental work was also carried out in
[
LL94
,
BCH04
]
where it was shown that certain families of parafree one-relator groups had different sets of finite quotients.

Problem 2.7.6
.

Find more examples of residually finite one-relator groups that are profinitely rigid amongst one-relator groups.

Baumslag posed the question of distinguishing cyclically pinched one-relator groups
[
Bau74b
, Problem 1]
by their finite quotients after having shown that they are all residually finite in
[
Bau63b
]
. This appears to still be open and is likely to be a source of examples for Problem
2.7.6
.

Question 2.7.7
.

Are there residually finite one-relator groups with the same finite quotients that are not isomorphic? Are there finitely many residually finite one-relator groups with the same finite quotients?

Baumslag showed in
[
Bau74a
]
that even within the class of virtually cyclic groups, there exist (two-relator) groups which are not profinitely rigid so one cannot be too optimistic regarding Question
2.7.7
.

By
Theorem

2.6.16
, a one-relator group
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is residually finite if
\pi(w)\neq 2
. This would be a natural family of one-relator groups to attempt to answer Question
2.7.7
given how much extra structure is known to hold in these cases. In particular, since parafree one-relator groups
F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
are 2-free and hence have
\pi(w)\geqslant 3
, it suffices to look at these one-relator groups for Remeslenikov’s conjecture. Wilton showed in
[
Wil18
]
that any cyclically pinched or cyclically conjugated one-relator group
F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
with
\pi(w)\geqslant 3
does not have the same finite quotients as a free group.

The first examples of pairs of finitely presented residually finite groups
H,G
and a homomorphism
H\to G
that is not an isomorphism, but induces an isomorphism on profinite completions
\hat{H}\to\hat{G}
were discovered by Bridson–Grunewald in
[
BG04
]
, after finitely generated ones had been discovered by Platonov–Tavgen’
[
PT90
]
. Such examples should not exist amongst one-relator groups.

Problem 2.7.8
.

Prove that if
G
is a residually finite one-relator group and
H\leqslant G
is a finitely generated subgroup, then the induced map
\hat{H}\to\hat{G}
is an isomorphism only if
H=G
.

One could also wonder whether it is worth studying profinite completions of non-residually finite groups. However, Baumslag–Miller–Troeger showed in
[
BMT07
]
that, given any one-relator group
G
, one can construct another one-relator group
H
that is not residually finite but has profinite completion isomorphic to
G
. In fact, the isomorphism they construct is induced by a surjection
H\to G
. The first example of such a surjection was the abelianisation map
\bg(1,2)\to\mathbb{Z}
, proven by Baumslag in
[
Bau69b
]
.

2.8.
Algorithmic properties

2.8.1.
The word problem

Although the word problem for all one-relator groups was solved almost a century ago, the complexity of the word problem has not seen much improvement from Magnus’ original solution. One way of measuring the complexity of the word problem of a group
G
is via its Dehn function
\delta_{G}
. An upper bound on the Dehn function of one-relator groups was obtained by Bernasconi in
[
Ber94
]
.

Theorem 2.8.1
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group, then

\delta_{G}(n)\precsim A_{2|w|}(n),

where
A_{k}
denotes the
k^{\text{th}}
Ackermann function, defined recursively by
A_{1}(n)=2n,A_{k+1}(n)=A_{k}^{(n)}(1)
.

Note that
A_{2}(n)=2^{n}
and
A_{3}(n)=\exp_{2}^{(n)}(1)
already grows faster than any finite tower of exponentials. Gersten states in
[
Ger93
]
that it is an open question as to whether for each
k
, there exists a one-relator group
G
with
\delta_{G}(n)\succsim A_{k}(n)
. We conjecture that no such one-relator groups exist for all
k
and they perhaps do not even exist for
k=3
.

A candidate one-relator group for having the worst possible Dehn function is the Baumslag–Gersten group

\bg(1,2)=\left\langle a,t\mid a^{a^{t}}=a^{2}\right\rangle.

which splits as a HNN-extension of the Baumslag–Solitar group
\bs(1,2)
where one generator is conjugated to the other. Platonov determined the precise Dehn function of the Baumslag–Gersten group
[
Pla04
]
after Gersten established lower bounds in
[
Ger92
]
and Bernasconi established upper bounds in
[
Ber94
]
.

Theorem 2.8.2
.

If
G=\bg(1,2)
is the Baumslag–Gersten group, then

\delta_{G}(n)\sim\exp^{(\log_{2}(n))}(1).

One can attempt to find one-relator groups with worse Dehn functions by performing iterated HNN-extensions to
\bg(1,2)
, conjugating one generator to the other, and hoping this yields a growth in Dehn function comparable to that of
\bg(1,2)
from
\bs(1,2)
. Interestingly, Bernasconi also showed that
A_{3}(n)
is an upper bound for the Dehn function of such groups. Other one-relator groups with interesting Dehn functions were already discussed in
Section

2.6.2
.

Another way of measuring the complexity of the word problem is via the time function of an algorithm to solve it. The Dehn function of a group provides an upper bound for the time function of a non-deterministic Turing machine solving its word problem. This roughly means that we can verify that an input word is trivial in time bounded above by the Dehn function. In general it is much more difficult to prove lower bounds on the time complexity of the word problem than on the Dehn function. Indeed, the following question, which appears as
[
BMS02
, Question (OR3)]
, is open.

Question 2.8.3

(A. Myasnikov)
.

Is the word problem in every one-relator group decidable in polynomial time?

Myasnikov–Ushakov–Won
[
MUW11
]
proved the following upper bounds for the complexity of the word problem of the Baumslag–Gersten group, lending credence to a positive answer to Question
2.8.3
. Their proof used new integer compression data structures known as power circuits.

Theorem 2.8.4
.

There is an algorithm which solves the word problem for the Baumslag–Gersten group
\bg(1,2)
in polynomial time.

Diekert–Laun–Ushakov
[
DLU12
]
refined the complexity estimates to obtain a cubic time solution to the word problem in
\bg(1,2)
and Laun extended this to apply to any
\bg(1,n)
for
n\geqslant 2

[
Lau14
, Theorem 3.1]
. For
\bg(2,3)
the situation is more complicated and no time bounds are known (other than those offered by Magnus’ algorithm), but would be interesting (see
[
MUW11
, Problem 1.8]
).

One obstacle to understanding the complexity of the word problem in one-relator groups is that there is no general method to obtain a simple finite set of rewriting rules over the generators to solve the word problem. In
[
MUW11
]
, the authors find a particularly nice rewriting system solving the word problem for
\bg(1,2)
using the Magnus–Moldavanskiĭ hierarchy for
\bg(1,2)
which expresses it as a double HNN-extension of
\mathbb{Z}
. In general, the Magnus hierarchy is more intricate. It would be of considerable interest to develop rewriting systems with nice properties for all one-relator groups, see
1.8.5
.

2.8.2.
The conjugacy, membership and normal root problems

Outside of a few special cases, the conjugacy problem for one-relator groups has remained largely untouched. Excluding hyperbolic one-relator groups, it is known to be decidable in cyclically pinched and conjugated one-relator groups (which includes Baumslag–Solitar groups) by Lipschutz
[
Lip66
]
and Larsen
[
Lar77
]
respectively, the Baumslag–Gersten group by Beese
[
Bee12
]
and for one-relator groups
G
with non-empty BNS-invariant
\Sigma^{1}(G)
by work of Logan
[
Log23
]
.

One particular special case of the conjugacy problem which should be more approachable in general is to determine when an element is conjugate into a Magnus subgroup.

Problem 2.8.5
.

If
G
is a one-relator and
A\leqslant G
is a Magnus subgroup, find an algorithm which decides whether an element
g\in G
is conjugate into
A
.

Another problem whose solution should be useful for understanding the conjugacy problem better would be an effective version of Collins’
Theorem

2.3.2
on intersections of conjugates of Magnus subgroups. As remarked previously,
Theorem

2.3.1
already has an effective version proved by Howie.

Problem 2.8.6
.

If
G=F(S)/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group and
A,B\leqslant G
are Magnus subgroups generated by subsets of
S
, determine the double cosets
AgB\neq AB
such that
A^{g}\cap B\neq 1
.

The membership problem for finitely generated subgroups of one-relator groups is another problem which is wide open.

Question 2.8.7
.

Is the subgroup membership problem decidable for one-relator groups?

It was shown by Gray
[
Gra20b
, Theorem B]
that there are one-relator groups with undecidable submonoid membership problem. As such, one cannot hope to go too far with regards to membership problems.

Problem 2.8.8

(Lyndon)
.

If
G=F(S)/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group and
A,B\leqslant G
are Magnus subgroups generated by subsets of
S
, find an algorithm which decides membership in a double coset
AgB
.

Lyndon posed this problem for
g=1

[
Lyn62
, Problem 3.6]
, which Newman solved for one-relator groups with torsion in
[
New68a
, Corollary 3]
. The extended word problem for a group
G
with presentation
\langle S\mid R\rangle
is the problem of determining membership in subgroups generated by subsets of the generators
S
. Lyndon’s motivation for this problem came from trying to solve the extended membership problem in groups with staggered presentations, following Magnus’ ideas for the extended word problem for one-relator groups.

The
normal root problem
was first studied by Magnus in
[
Mag30
]
. If
F
is a free group and
w\in F
, then an element
r\in F
is a
normal root
of
w
if
w\in\langle\!\langle r\rangle\!\rangle
.

Problem 2.8.9

(Normal Root Problem)
.

Let
F
be a free group and let
w\in F
. Determine the normal roots of
w
.

In
[
Mag32
]
, Magnus determined the normal roots of the commutator
[a,b]
. Some further work on the root problem was carried out by Steinberg in
[
Ste86
]
who characterised normal roots of
a^{k}b^{l}
when
k,l
are primes. Remeslennikov conjectured that if
r
is a (cyclically reduced) normal root of
w
and
|r|>|w|
, then
w
is conjugate to
[r,f]
or
[r^{-1},f]
for some
f\in F
, see
[
BMS02
, Problem F16]
. However, this was then disproved by McCool in
[
McC01
]
. One can also ask a more specific version of the Normal Root Problem.

Question 2.8.10
.

If
F
is a free group, does there exist an infinite sequence of words
w_{0},w_{1},\ldots\in F
such that
\langle\!\langle w_{i}\rangle\!\rangle\lneq\langle\!\langle w_{i+1}\rangle\!\rangle
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
?

This question is of particular interest because an answer determines whether or not one-relator groups are closed in the space of marked groups.

2.8.3.
The isomorphism problem, Nielsen generating sets and
T
-systems

Despite almost a century of progress since Magnus solved the word problem for one-relator groups, the conjugacy and isomorphism problem remain very far from being solved in general.

Currently the most general result in this direction is that the isomorphism problem for one-relator groups
F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
with
\pi(w)\neq 2
is decidable amongst one-relator groups. This is because they are hyperbolic and one can appeal to deep work of Sela
[
Sel95
]
(in the torsion-free case) and Dahmani–Groves
[
DG11
]
(in the torsion case).

Theorem 2.8.11
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group with
\pi(w)\neq 2
, then the problem of deciding whether another one-relator group is isomorphic to
G
is decidable.

One way to understand the isomorphisms between one-relator groups is by understanding their generating sets.

Definition 2.8.12
.

Two sets
X_{1},X_{2}\subset G=F/N
are
Nielsen equivalent
if there is an automorphism
\psi\in\Aut(F)
such that
\psi(X_{1})=X_{2}
. They are in the same
T
-system
if there is an automorphism
\phi\in\Aut(G)
such that
\phi(X_{1})
is Nielsen equivalent to
X_{2}
.

Let
F
be a free group with free generating set
S\subset F
. Given two normal subgroups
N_{1},N_{2}\triangleleft F
and an isomorphism
\theta\colon F/N_{1}\to F/N_{2}
, if the two generating sets
S_{2}
and
\theta(S_{1})
lie in the same
T
-system, where here
S_{i}
denotes the image of
S
in
F/N_{i}
, then there is an automorphism
\psi\in\Aut(F)
such that
\psi(N_{1})=N_{2}
.

When
F/N_{1}
and
F/N_{2}
are one-relator presentations, we may say something much stronger. If
N_{1}=\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
and
N_{2}=\langle\!\langle r\rangle\!\rangle
, the Magnus property for free groups (see
Theorem

2.2.8
) implies that if
S_{2}
and
\theta(S_{1})
lie in the
T
-system, then there is an automorphism
\psi\in\Aut(F)
such that
\psi(w)=r^{\pm 1}
. Hence, one-relator groups
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
with a single
T
-system of (one-relator) generating set admit a particularly simple solution to the isomorphism problem amongst one-relator groups: given another one-relator presentation
H=F/\langle\!\langle r\rangle\!\rangle
, to determine whether
G\cong H
, we only need to check whether
w
and
r^{\pm 1}
lie in the same
\Aut(F)
-orbit which can be done in polynomial time using Whitehead’s algorithm
[
RVW07
]
(see
[
Whi36
]
for Whitehead’s original algorithm).

The isomorphism problem for two-generator one-relator groups with torsion was solved first by Pride
[
Pri77a
]
who showed that if
G=F/\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle
is a two-generator one-relator group with
n\geqslant 2
, then
G
has only one
T
-system of generating pair, see
Theorem

1.6.12
. Note that
G=\langle a,b\mid a^{5}\rangle
has more than one Nielsen class of generating set since
\{a^{2},b\}
also generates
G
. However, the automorphism given by
b\to b
and
a\to a^{2}
shows that
\{a,b\}
and
\{a^{2},b\}
lie in the same
T
-system.

Although now it is known that the isomorphism problem is decidable for all one-relator groups with torsion, one still might wonder whether Pride’s
T
-system result can be extended to obtain a more intrinsic solution to the word problem. This question was explicitly raised by Pride in
[
Pri75
]
.

Question 2.8.13

(Pride)
.

If
G=F/\langle\!\langle w^{n}\rangle\!\rangle
is a one-relator group
n\geqslant 2
, is it true that
G
has one
T
-system of generating set of minimal cardinality?

Making explicit the connection between the work of Louder–Wilton on two-complexes
X
with uniform negative immersions and Nielsen equivalence classes of generating sets for
\pi_{1}(X)
would be a worthwhile task. In particular, their work should imply at least that one-relator complexes
X
with negative immersions have fundamental group with finitely many Nielsen classes of generating sets of any given finite cardinality. Furthermore, we suspect that Pride’s Question
2.8.13
can be answered positively also for this setting.

Question 2.8.14
.

If
X
be a one-relator complex with negative immersion, is it true that
G=\pi_{1}(X)
has one
T
-system of generating set of minimal cardinality?

2.8.14
is known to have a positive answer for closed surfaces: if
G
is the fundamental group of a closed surface, Lars Louder showed in
[
Lou15
]
that
G
has precisely one Nielsen class of generating set of any finite cardinality, improving on previous work of Zieschang
[
Zie70
]
.

See also
[
BMS02
, Problem (OR13)]
for a more general phrasing of Question
2.8.14
.

During the 70’s, a significant number of contributions were made towards understanding the generating sets of one-relator groups. We summarise below some of the main results in this direction. The reader is directed to
Section

1.8.2
for more details.

•

The first known examples of one-relator groups with more than one Nielsen class of one-relator generating sets were the torus knot groups
\langle a,b\mid a^{m}=b^{n}\rangle
. This was proved by McCool–Pietrowski
[
MP73
]
.

•

Delzant
[
Del91
]
showed that torsion-free two-generator hyperbolic groups have finitely many Nielsen classes of generating pairs (a result announced by Gromov
[
Gro87
]
), although even
C^{\prime}(1/6)
two-generator one-relator groups can gave more than one Nielsen generating pair by work of Pride
[
Pri76
]
.

•

If
G\cong\bs(1,n)
for any
n\neq 0
, Brunner showed that
G
has only one
T
-system of generating pair
[
Bru74
, Theorem 2.4]
and thus every two-generator presentation of
G
is a one-relator presentation. In the same article, Brunner also showed that
\bs(2,3)
has infinitely many
T
-systems of generating pairs which are not associated with one-relator presentations and at least two
T
-systems of generating pairs which are associated with one-relator presentations.

•

Brunner provided an example in
[
Bru76
]
of a two-generator one-relator group with infinitely many Nielsen equivalence classes of generating pairs associated with one-relator presentations. The group was the Baumslag–Gersten group and the one-relator presentations associated with the generating pairs were:

\bg(1,2)\cong\left\langle a,t\mid t^{-1}a^{-2^{n}}tat^{-1}a^{2^{n}}t=a^{2}\right\rangle

where
n
is any natural number. It can be checked that each relator has minimal length in its
\Aut(F(a,t))
-orbit and so the associated generating sets

\{t^{-1}a^{-n}tat^{-1}a^{n}t,t\}\subset F(a,t)/\langle\!\langle a^{a^{t}}a^{-2}\rangle\!\rangle

are also in distinct
T
-systems.

•

Non-cyclic one-relator groups with centre may have many
T
-systems of generating pairs (they are all generated by two elements). However, Pietrowski
[
Pie74
]
showed that their isomorphism problem is decidable amongst all one-relator groups. Indeed, Baumslag–Taylor proved that it is decidable whether a given one-relator group has centre in
[
BT68
]
. Pietrowski then showed that any non-cyclic one-relator group with centre splits as a graph of infinite cyclic groups in an essentially unique way
[
Pie74
]
which then yields a solution to the isomorphism problem using Tietze transformations.

•

Rosenberger solved the isomorphism problem for cyclically pinched one-relator groups in
[
Ros94b
]
by an in depth analysis of the Nielsen classes of generating sets. Some partial results were also obtained for cyclically conjugated one-relator groups by Fine–Röhl–Rosenberger
[
FRR93
]
.

•

A generic one-relator group has precisely one Nielsen class of (minimal size) generating set by work of Kapovich–Schupp
[
KS05
]
.

Although it appears that the isomorphism problem might be easily solvable amongst one-relator groups
G=F/\langle\!\langle w\rangle\!\rangle
with
\pi(w)\neq 2
, the above results for two-generator one-relator groups show that the general case presents several additional difficulties we do not yet know how to overcome.
